# Consideration of funding proposals - Addendum XVI

## Funding proposal package for FP274

### Summary

This addendum contains the following six parts:

a) A funding proposal titled "Building the Climate Resilience of Children and Communities through the Education Sector (BRACE)";

b) No-objection letter issued by the national designated authority(ies) or focal point(s);

c) Secretary's assessment;

d) Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment;

e) Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment; and

f) Gender documentation.

This document is prepared and transmitted for consideration by the GCF Board at a Board meeting. It is published on the GCF public website in its current, unedited form. The GCF Secretariat will make publicly available in accordance with applicable GCF policies.

## Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template

• Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.

• Accredited Entities are required to submit a completed funding proposal based on the data provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be reflected in the updated version of the funding proposal.

• The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 30 pages, unless exceeding the prescribed limit will not be assessed within the usual service standard time.

• The recommended font is Arial, size 11, out.

• Upon submission of the funding proposals, project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website simultaneously with the submission to the Board. Subject to the confidentiality requirements set forth in the GCF Agreement, the IDP Accredited Entities are asked to fill out information on disclosure in section G-4.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 3

1. **Globally, nearly half of children, nearly half the world's children, live in countries at extreme vulnerability to the impacts of climate change**. All three targeted countries by BRAC through Component 1 - Cambodia, South Sudan and Tonga - are among those most vulnerable to the impacts of climate change (e.g., droughts, floods, and high-temperature events) which result in a range of education-related impacts. The main impact is on children's access to school, learning loss, and learning outcomes. Other impacts include teachers absent from school, learning loss and impacts on learning outcomes, and gender-related impacts (e.g., increased burden for girls). These are all the impacts that climate hazards have on school facilities (e.g., water, sanitation and hygiene [WASH] impacts), etc.

2. **Component 1** will directly address and respond to the impacts of climate change on the education sector and children's learning by: (Activity 1.2.1) strengthening disaster risk reduction (DRR) at school level; and school-level interventions to create sufficient conditions for stability, and sufficient mitigation to catalyse the transition towards more sustainable schools. The DRR activities will be implemented in collaboration with supported, climate and education policies, laws and guidelines will be prepared with Ministries of Education and Environment and other relevant stakeholders. A national DRR committee will be established (Activity 1.2.1). Relevant line ministries will be strengthened (Activity 1.1.2). Disaster Risk Reduction (DRR) plans will be facilitated, tested and implemented in schools (Activity 1.2.3). The project will support the development of climate change policy activities (Activity 1.1.4). In Output 1.2, school facilities are safer and greener; national standards for climate-resilient schools are established; DRR plans are developed and tested at school level; and capacity is strengthened to strengthen climate resilience (Activity 1.2.2). To ensure sustainability, school communities and education officials will be supported in the development of a national DRR strategy (Activity 1.3.4) and in ensuring that schools and educational continuity management systems are operating effectively; master trainers from the Ministry of Education will be trained to support climate change adaptation and mitigation activities at school level (Activity 1.2.5). Climate-related safety plans (Activity 1.3.2.1), school districts supported to implement their plans and update their disaster risk reduction plans (Activity 1.3.2.4) and the development of a national Early Warning System (EWS) will also be distributed to schools (Activity 1.3.4). And Early Warning Systems (EWS) and Climate Information Systems (CIS) will be developed and implemented in schools (Activity 1.3.5).

3. In Component 2, the project will support Ministries of Education in the three countries - Cambodia, South Sudan and Tonga - supporting them to further access climate finance and to lead climate-resilient sustainable development through education. The project will support the three countries to: (Activity 2.1) develop a national action plan on impact of climate change on the education sector and children's learning (Activity 2.1.1). MoEs to engage in National Adaptation Programmes of Action (NAPAs) and other relevant processes at the country level, including with the UNFCCC. MoEs will also be supported to develop and implement climate finance mobilization road maps, with Direct beneficiaries being 300 Ministry of Education officials from the three countries (Activity 2.1.4). Direct beneficiaries will be 210 Ministry of Education and other education and climate change officials from the three countries (Activity 2.1.5).

4. **Component 2** will support the education community to better coordinate, collaborate, and share lessons learned and experiences with a focus on climate finance. The project will ensure that education and climate stakeholders are committed to working together in order to access climate finance and to lead climate-resilient sustainable development through education. The project will also support the three countries to: (Activity 2.1) develop a national action plan on impact of climate change on the education sector and children's learning (Activity 2.1.1). MoEs to engage in National Adaptation Programmes of Action (NAPAs) and other relevant processes at the country level, including with the UNFCCC. MoEs will also be supported to develop and implement climate finance mobilization road maps, with Direct beneficiaries being 300 Ministry of Education officials from the three countries (Activity 2.1.4). Direct beneficiaries will be 210 Ministry of Education and other education and climate change officials from the three countries (Activity 2.1.5).

Webinars, thematic sessions and learning events will also be held to support the BRACED countries (Activity 3.2.3), and strategic communications materials on climate and education preparedness will be produced (Activity 3.2.4). The project’s goal is to ensure that education systems are better equipped for climate change and education change actors in the three target countries. B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B.1. Project Context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

#### South Sudan
South Sudan will experience a significant warming trend, with mean temperatures rising by 3.3°C per decade. By mid-century, maximum temperatures will exceed 40°C in some areas, with extreme heat days above 35°C projected to increase from the current average of 18 days per year to more than 27 days per year. In the north, where temperature anomalies are expected to exceed +1.25°C. For school-age children, extreme heat will heighten risks of heat-related illnesses and reduce their ability to concentrate, impacting learning environments.

Despite projected increases in rainfall, drought risk is expected to increase, reflected by a decline in the dry season drought index. However, rainfall variability and changes in wet and dry spells present pressing challenges. Central and northern regions will see slight reductions in consecutive dry days, but southeastern regions will face more frequent dry spells, disrupting traditional planting cycles, impacting food security and nutrition—a critical concern for children's development and learning.

#### Rainfall
Rainfall is projected to increase slightly, with annual totals averaging around 1236 mm and modeled upward trends expected. However, the variability in rainfall patterns and extremes will exacerbate flood risks, disrupting infrastructure and access to schools, hospitals, and markets.

### Cambodia's climate projections under the SSP5-4.5 scenario from 2020 to 2050 indicate significant changes in rainfall patterns, temperature increases, and heightened risks of extreme weather events. These changes will profoundly affect livelihoods and development across Cambodia.

#### 10. Annual rainfall is projected to increase slightly, averaging 1,791.78 mm with a 1% per decade upward trend. But regional and seasonal differences can result in uneven impacts. Central regions are expected to receive more rainfall, while parts of the northwestern region may experience reduced rainfall. There will be increased rainfall intensity during the rainy season from July to October and reduced rainfall in critical planting months like April and May. Extreme rainfall events, such as flash floods and landslides, could become more frequent across Cambodia.

#### 11. Cambodia will experience a significant warming trend, with minimum, mean, and maximum temperatures increasing by 0.7°C per decade. By the century's end, average maximum temperature will reach 32.65°C, with intense areas exceeding the highest anomalies. The number of hot days exceeding 35°C will rise dramatically, from 67 days annually to over 90 days in some regions.

#### 12. This sustained heat poses significant risks to school-age children, including heat-related illnesses, dehydration, and reduced learning performance due to discomfort in poorly ventilated classrooms. Prolonged exposure to extreme temperatures can also lead to increased mortality rates among vulnerable populations such as the elderly and those with pre-existing health conditions.

#### 13. While the drought index (SPI) shows an overall improvement, indicating fewer and less severe droughts, regional disparities persist. The northwest may face increased drought severity, while other areas experience moderate droughts. Increased evaporation rates in the northwest could exacerbate water scarcity, impacting agriculture and household water supply.

14. Tonga's climate projections under the SSP-2.4 scenario reveal increased changes in rainfall, extreme weather events, and sea-level rise that will significantly affect its people and economy. These changes pose distinct challenges for school-age children, who are particularly vulnerable to disruptions in education, health, and well-being. Tonga is especially exposed to more frequent and intense tropical cyclones, which can cause flooding and lead to intensified extreme rainfall events. The northern islands are likely to face higher extremes, with one-day and five-day precipitation totals increasing by 20% and 15%, respectively. These changes will intensify the frequency of heavy rainfall events, causing greater year-to-year variability and potentially disrupting water availability and agricultural cycles. For example, prolonged periods of flooding can lead to infrastructure damage or unsafe travel conditions during extreme rainfall events, especially in remote areas.

15. While drought conditions are projected to improve slightly, increasing aridity and expansion into some regions could strain water resources and agriculture. This will likely increase the number of people facing at least moderate droughts, which can directly impact hygiene and nutrition—key determinants of children's health and cognitive development.

16. Tonga will face significant warming trends, with minimum, mean, and maximum temperatures rising by approximately 0.5°C per decade between now and 2030. The frequency of extreme heat events is projected to increase under these conditions becoming the norm for over 270 days annually. Northern regions will experience longer warm spells, with durations exceeding 14 days in most years, while minimum temperature levels are expected for extended periods.

17. The frequency of category 3-5 cyclones is projected to decrease slightly, but tropical cyclone damage is a significant threat given their potential for widespread destruction. Schools are particularly vulnerable to damage from cyclones which can displace children, interrupt education, and exacerbate mental health challenges due to the trauma of extreme weather events.

## Impacts on School-Age Children, School Infrastructure, and Learning

18. The projected climate changes for South Sudan, Cambodia, and Tonga under the SSP2-4.5 scenario highlight significant challenges for school-age children, school infrastructure, and learning environments, with shared themes of extreme weather events leading to flooding, landslides, and drought.

19. In **South Sudan**, increasing rainfall extremes and heightened flood risks will likely disrupt school attendance and damage educational infrastructure. Regions prone to flooding, such as central and northern areas, may experience repeated closures due to waterlogging and structural damage. Flooding can also lead to the collapse of buildings, affecting both students and extended heat waves, will exacerbate health issues like dehydration and heat stress for children, impacting their ability to learn effectively.

20. **Cambodia** faces similar challenges with intensified monsoons and increased frequency of extreme rainfall events leading to flooding in low-lying areas. This can disrupt school attendance and pose sanitation challenges, increasing the risk of waterborne diseases. Rising temperatures, with an average increase of 1°C by 2050, will exacerbate heat stress and poor cooling systems will affect students' focus and attendance. Droughts and shifting rainfall patterns could lead to food security issues affecting children's nutrition and learning capabilities, especially in rural areas.

21. In **Tonga**, extreme rainfall events and tropical cyclones will continue to be significant threats to school infrastructure, with the primary concern being rising temperatures, leading to extended warm spells increasing risks of heat-related illnesses and

## Climate and non-climate drivers of climate-related school disruptions in Cambodia

### Country: Cambodia

#### Climate driver:
- **Driver:** Increasing temperature

#### Climate evidenced impacts:
- **Drought**
- **Increased evaporation rate**

#### Contributing non-climate drivers:
- **Poverty, household economic status,** child-headed households, hunger
- **Low access to education for indigenous and ethnic minority groups** in rural areas
- **Socio-cultural economics:** withdrawing children from school due to household income/growing family size; lack of resources for uniforms and books; inability of families to pay school fees; lack of interest in education; poor health and livelihoods, leading to increased drop-out rates
- **Geographical location:** Last mile & remote communities, long commutes to schools, fetching water, and working fields

## Driver: Increasing temperature

### Hazards:
- Heat stress/hot weather
- Hotter days
- Hotter nights

#### Effects:
- Child malnutrition due to heat-stressed impacts on land and water resources
- Deforestation, especially linked to illegal logging that may lead to changes in forest ecology, reduced biodiversity and exacerbate the drying effect
- Poverty: household economic status, food security, access to education
- Poorest children and children from indigenous ethnic groups are most likely to be out of school
- Social-cultural-economic: withdrawing children to contribute labour for family/farm/industry
- Geographical location: exposure levels vary across the country and regions

- Rainfall patterns shifting towards monsoons (Jul-Oct) from planting months (Apr-May)
- Extreme weather events (5-day maximums, flooding)

- Poverty: household economic status, food security, access to education
- Poorest children and children from indigenous ethnic groups are most likely to be out of school
- Social-cultural-economic: withdrawing children to contribute labour for family/farm/industry
- Geographical location: exposure levels vary across the country and regions

## Driver: Increasing temperature

### Hazards:
- Heat stress/heatwaves
- Hotter days
- Higher night-time temperatures
- Reduced rainfall (including students collapsing/dehydration, indoor classroom heat due to negative impacts on teacher/student learning environments (e.g., in the shade of the school building), and reduced outdoor time for students to hatch weather conditions, with increased risk of heat-related illnesses and educational game due to school closures)

## Driver Variable: rainfall

### Hazards:
- Extreme rainfall events (including heavy rainfalls, maximums)
- Flash floods
- Damage to school infrastructure leading to school closures (190 schools closed due to damage in 2021), increased likelihood of heat stress/heatwaves, increased likelihood of heat stress/heatwaves, damage to school buildings, compromised access to water and sanitation facilities, reduced number of people attending school attendance due to continued economic activities (e.g., household economy; early marriage and early childbearing, lack of access to education for women and girls), geographical location: highest exposure levels in Rumbek North, Rumbek South and the northeast/southeast regions

### Consequences:
- Poverty: limited financial resources within communities to enroll their children in school
- Refugee and migrant child influx in schools due to displacement
- Socio-economic: withdrawal of children from school (e.g., early marriage and early childbearing), lack of access to education for women and girls
- Socio-cultural: alienation of mentoring/girls, increased vulnerability for boys and child soldiers
- Geopolitical: Conflict affects attendance in schools and ability to concentrate
- Environmental: Increased exposure to heat stress/heatwaves

## Driver: Sea-level rise (SLR)
- Damage to school infrastructure; loss of land
- Encroachment on existing education land
- Loss of coastal wetlands and mangrove forests in Maluku, leading to increased loss of biodiversity and habitat
- Increased incidence of vector-borne diseases
- Psychological/health-related distress

## Hazard: Sea intrusion and saline inundation
- Coastal erosion

## Driver: Strong or severe winds
- Damage to school infrastructure resulting in structural damage and collapse
- Impacts on ability to teach (Tropical Cyclone Pam, 2015)
- Loss of educational materials and equipment impacting the education of approx. 23,000 students in Vanuatu
- Infrastructure to school (blocking roads, river flooding) leading to loss of access to schools
- Increasingly severe cyclones leading to increased damage to infrastructure

## Hazard: Cyclones
- Damage to school infrastructure resulting in structural damage and collapse
- Loss of educational materials and equipment impacting the education of approx. 23,000 students in Vanuatu
- Infrastructure to school (blocking roads, river flooding) leading to loss of access to schools
- Increasingly severe cyclones leading to increased damage to infrastructure

### Poverty, especially for children in outer islands and young people who are more likely to be living in poor households
- Poor general maintenance and building quality of school infrastructure (between 50% to 90% of schools have been identified as being in a strong cyclone* condition)
- Increased risk of abuse against children, including child sexual abuse
- Gender and sexual diversity. Increased risk of gender-based violence and sexual harassment as gender or sexuality diverse*
- Violence against children leading to school refusal or camps in the aftermath of a cyclone.*

23. BRACE offers a holistic solution to the climate vulnerabilities faced by the education sector: it is first and has an overarching focus on unique, locally-led solutions. Second, the projects are designed to be scalable. Third, these projects have addressed aspects of climate change resilience in the education sector but have been limited in their scope and scale. Fourth, there is a need for more attention to be paid to the education sector within the Local Life Skills Education (LLSE) project which has focused on climate change and environmental teaching and education and has not yet included disaster risk reduction or gender equality. Fifth, there are significant gaps of underinvestment in climate-related education with interventions focused on humanitarian needs (Education in Emergencies) rather than long-term resilience building. Sixth, the need for a more integrated approach to Climate Resilience Programme and NORD Framework Agreement. In Tonga, the Safe and Resilient Schools Project (SRSP) is working towards this goal by focusing on the development of a school-based curriculum of action on climate change and improving the quality of education management, curricula and assessments and BRACE specifically builds upon the SRSP.

### If the education stakeholders in climate-vulnerable countries are better informed of climate risks and adaptation options for the education sector, as well as have access to global knowledge, policy exchange and financing, and if the target education sector is more resilient to climate change, then these countries will be better prepared to address climate change vulnerability and the barriers faced.

### Components 1-3 are interlinked. Component 1 builds a Climate-Resilient School Infrastructure and Systemmatic outputs and activities leading to Outcome 1 – The education sector at national and sub-national levels in targeted countries is more resilient to the impacts of climate change, with enhanced climate action in the education sector.

### Components 2 and 3 are interlinked. Component 2 builds a Climate-Resilient School Infrastructure and Systemmatic outputs and activities leading to Outcome 2 – Education ministries at the target countries have improved access to climate finance to increase the resilience of the education sector, with enhanced climate action in the education sector.

### Components 1-3 are interlinked. Component 3 builds a Climate-Resilient School Infrastructure and Systemmatic outputs and activities leading to Outcome 3 – The education sector at national and sub-national levels in targeted countries is more resilient to the impacts of climate change, with enhanced climate action in the education sector.

Output 1.3 on school safety and educational infrastructure is linked to Output 1.4 on climate-informed decision-making and influencing.

The outputs are interlinked as follows: Output 1 which is linked to Output 2 in climate risk-informed decision-making and influencing, which targets information and institutional strengthening. ## B.3. Project/programme description (max. 2000 words, approximately 5 pages)

BRACE focuses on enhancing the climate resilience of the education sector and addressing risks and impacts from climate change in three target countries: Bangladesh, Cambodia, and Nepal. The project will combine climate-intervention levels at national, sub-national, and school levels. Component 2 will work directly with schools to improve their capacity for climate-resilient education by providing training on climate resilience, developing a set of easy-to-use guidance and engagement in National Adaptation Planning processes. Component 3 will strengthen the institutional capacity of the Ministry of Education (MoE) at national level through technical assistance and support. In addition, BRACE will work with local communities to promote social inclusion and gender equity in education in Cambodia, South Sudan, and Togo. BRACE will directly benefit 807,500 people in Component 1, including 392,180 school teachers, 415,320 students, and 60,000 community members. The indirect benefits of the project are expected to cover all school-aged children in the three target countries of BRACE therefore. It is estimated that Component 2 will directly benefit 79,000 schools and 8 million people (including teachers, students, and communities) through climate-resilient education systems and support children's learning through a multi-pronged approach as set out in the project document (Annexes 1-3). The project will also provide technical assistance to the MoE at national level (Annexes 2). Furthermore, the BRACE project will be complemented by parallel project finance from the Global Partnership for Education (GPE) and UNESCO. GPE will support the education sector in Cambodia, Nepal, and South Sudan with education in climate stressed environments, such as absenteeism and dropping-out, as the GPE and UNESCO support countries to implement climate-resilient initiatives of the education systems which includes tackling the broader causes of absenteeism and dropping-out.

### Component 1

DEVELOPING education sector at national and sub-national levels in targeted countries is more resilient to the impacts of climate change. The project will enable policies to be in place to support climate-resilient education to ensure that school facilities are safer and more climate resilient, that school safety systems are in place and educational continuity is protected; and that the education sector has the capacity to address governance, social inclusion, information, financial, technological institutions, gender and social inclusion issues.

### Output 1.1 Enabling systems and policies are in place and supported

The project will support the MoE at national level to mainstream climate change within their education sector plans and policies and to strengthen internal coordination within the Ministry of Education on climate change and cross-sectoral

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 21

## Activity 1.1.1 Facilitate preparation and endorsement of climate and education policies, plans, and guidelines

### 1.1.1.2 Strengthen coordination on climate change within Ministries of Education and other relevant line ministries

This activity will support the Ministry of Education to have stronger internal and cross-sectoral coordination with Ministries of Environment and Energy, as well as other relevant line ministries. This will be achieved through multi-stakeholder dialogue events on climate change within the education sector.

### 1.1.3.2 Facilitate integration of Disaster Risk Reduction (DRR) into existing and new plans with a focus on school facilities

This activity will support Education authorities at national and sub-national level in Cambodia and South Sudan to be supported to prepare and test through multi-stakeholder dialogues, processes to have procedures in place to minimise the impact of future climate-related emergencies on the education sector.

### 1.1.4 Support children to participate in climate change and education policy initiatives

This activity will support children’s participation in climate change and education policy dialogues. In Cambodia, BRAC will develop a moderated virtual web-based platform that will serve as a hub for young people to share their views on climate change and education.

### Output 1.2 School facilities are safer and greener

The activities under this output focus on ensuring that school facilities are safer and climate-resilient for children, including through the development of national standards for school infrastructure, school staff, and School Management Committees (SMCs), as well as caregivers.

## Activity 1.2.3 Develop a national standard for climate-resilient school facilities

### 1.2.3.1 Strengthen the climate resilience of school infrastructure, including classrooms, and WASH facilities, but this guidance has not always been developed at country level: In Cambodia, BRAC will work with the MEF to develop a national standard for climate-resilient school facilities in collaboration with the Ministry of Education and South Sudan. BRAC will use the climate change and environment review of MAGICS Basic Construction Standards and guidelines as well as other relevant international standards.

### 1.2.3.2 Strengthen school facilities, including WASH and internet connectivity, to strengthen climate resilience

This activity will apply the country-level standards developed in Activity 1.2.1 to strengthen the climate resilience of school infrastructure, including classrooms, and WASH facilities. The project will also support the development of a national standard for Australia’s ESS Certificate C Accreditation and be gender-sensitive and disability inclusive. The infrastructure planning to be supported by this activity includes: classroom construction (Cambodia), water supply and sanitation (South Sudan) are anticipated to include, for example, cooling systems (Cambodia, Tonga) and improved ventilation (South Sudan). This will also support the development of a national standard for internet connectivity in schools. The project will provide shade planting through tree planting within school grounds (South Sudan), as well as supporting testing and upgrading of existing water supply infrastructure to ensure that it is climate-resilient.

### 1.2.3.3 Develop awareness-raising sessions on climate change and hygiene

This activity will develop awareness-raising sessions for students to be organized to promote good handwashing and hygiene. In South Sudan, girls will receive sessions on menstrual health and hygiene.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 22

As part of the overall plan for upgrading of school facilities, school communities, including education officials (Cambodia, South Sudan, and Tonga) will be trained in operations and maintenance of the school facilities. Joint monitoring and supervision visits by the Ministry of Education and other relevant stakeholders will also be conducted.

### 4.0 Output 13 School safety and educational continuity management systems are operating effectively

The activities under this output will focus on ensuring that school safety management and educational continuity systems are in place to ensure that schools can continue their operations even when faced with extreme weather events. This includes training teachers, SMCs, Parent Teacher Associations (PTAs) and children on how to develop and implement climate-related school safety plans, including evacuation procedures, emergency response plans, and other measures. The training will also include the distribution of kits for use in schools, access to Early Warning Systems (EWS) and Climate Information Services (CIS) strengthened.

#### 4.0.1 Activity 13.1 Build capacity of national and sub-national education officials to be master trainers on climate-related school safety plans

This activity uses a "Train the Trainer" (TtT) approach to build the capacity of education officials at national and/or subnational level to train teachers, SMCs, PTAs and children in developing and implementing climate-related school safety plans. The master trainers for this project will be identified in partnership with the Ministry of Education in each country.

#### 4.0.2 Activity 13.2 Build knowledge and capacity of school management, teachers, and children to prepare climate-related school safety plans

Following the implementation of Activity 13.1, SMCs/PTAs, and teachers and children will be trained by education officials in developing a climate-related school safety plan that includes evacuation procedures, emergency response plans, and other measures involved as they play a key role in the wider school community. Refresher training and on-going support will be provided to ensure continuous improvement.

#### 4.0.3 Activity 13.3 Implement school-level climate-related school safety plans

In this activity, SMCs, teachers and children from target schools trained in Activity 13.2 will be supported to implement what they have learned by developing a school-level climate-related school safety plan that includes evacuation procedures, emergency response plans, and other measures involved as they play a key role in the wider school community. The plan will also be inclusive of climate risk assessment of the school and preparing a climate-related school safety plan. The plans will also be shared with local authorities and communities to ensure their inclusion in the overall disaster management plan. This includes information, and integrating the school safety plan into the overall SIP, and in the case of Cambodia linking with community emergency response plans.

#### 4.0.4 Activity 13.4 Provide climate resilience tools, equipment and kits for schools

Schools will be provided with climate resilience kits specific to the needs in each context. It is anticipated these kits will include: for example, solar lamps, water filters, mosquito nets, and other equipment such as megaphones, as required.

#### 4.0.5 Activity 13.5 Strengthen access and use of Early Warning Systems (EWS) and Climate Information Systems (CIS)

This activity will focus on improving the availability of climate and early warning information to schools. In Cambodia, BRACE will utilise the existing green generation approach for distribution of EWS and CIS kits to schools. The kits will include various data sources and provide visual aids including maps to support decision making by MUEYS. In South Sudan, BRACE will work with partners to develop a climate risk assessment tool that can be used in conjunction with the EWS and CIS. The 13.2 and sharing early warning information on extreme events including floods, droughts and heatwaves via multiple channels such as SMS, radio, television, and social media. In Tonga, BRACE will work with partners to develop climate resilience materials will be developed and distributed to schools to ensure they are reached by EWS. EWS and CIS modules will also be included in the school safety plan.

#### 4.0.6 Output 14.0 Climate change resilience teaching and learning embedded in national systems

The project will utilise existing green generation approach for distribution of climate-related educational materials for schools in Cambodia, South Sudan, and Tonga. It will also strengthen teachers' capacity to deliver climate change lessons through teacher training and the development of new curricula involving girls and boys, including those with disabilities who are vulnerable to lead to climate-resilience initiatives in their schools and communities. The project will also use digital tools to support the delivery of climate change information.

#### 4.0.7 Activity 14.1 Develop teaching and learning materials on climate change ready to implement through national, non-formal, and informal curricula

The project will develop new teaching and learning materials for schools in Cambodia, South Sudan, and Tonga on climate change for use in school. In Cambodia, BRACE will utilise the existing green generation approach for distribution of nationwide scale-up by the MUEYS as and required, developing additional materials on topics such as green states, child

### 5.2 Activity 1.4.2 Build knowledge and capacity of teachers and education managers to implement climate change materials in their school or community context

Teachers and education managers in Cambodia, South Sudan, and Tonga will be trained and provided with on-going support to integrate climate change into the curriculum. In Cambodia, a needs assessment for training needs assessment will be undertaken. In Cambodia, teachers will be trained in how to use the green generation materials and will receive ongoing support from the Ministry of Education and other partners. In South Sudan, teachers will receive training and ongoing support. In South Sudan: BRAC will conduct a needs assessment to determine the most relevant training content for the teachers. The training will be provided by the Ministry of Education and other partners. The training will be rolled out and aligned to the World Bank Building Skills for Human Development in South Sudan programme. In Tonga, teachers will receive training on how to use the climate change materials as well as ongoing support to integrate climate change materials.

### 5.3 Activity 1.4.3 Strengthen capacity of clubs and associations, including children's environmental organizations in their school or community context

Cambodia: BRAC will work with existing clubs and associations, including children’s environmental organizations, to provide in-school training for children on what they have learned about climate change in their homes and wider community. In Cambodia, BRAC will work with existing clubs and associations, including children's environmental organizations, to provide in-school training for children on what they have learned about climate change in their homes and wider community.

### 5.4 Activity 1.4.4 Disseminate climate change teaching and learning materials through Ministry of Education e-learning and other platforms

Each country will update the climate change materials to existing government e-learning platforms or other platforms. In Cambodia, the updated materials will be disseminated via the Ministry of Education's e-learning platform. In South Sudan, peer-to-peer support and learning. The green generation materials will also be integrated into MELTS online platform for other partners to use. In Tonga, the climate change materials will be disseminated through the national radio station.

### 5.5 Activity 1.4.5 Prepare learning documents and hold events with Ministries of Education and other partners to share experience of integrating climate change into the curriculum

Each country will prepare learning documents on how they are working to integrate climate change into different subjects, as is the case in Tonga. However, Cambodia and South Sudan will not undertake curriculum revisions during the project timeframe so BRAC will be able to provide guidance and support for teachers and education managers to integrate climate change materials into existing activities to share with Ministry of Education and other actors.

### 5.6 Comparison: Cambodia

Education ministries at the target countries have improved access to climate finance to increase the resilience of the education sector to climate change

- **Cambodia**: The government has provided additional resources at national level – especially Ministries of Education, Direct Access Entities, and government departments responsible for national adaptation planning to strengthen access to climate information. This will help address some of the barriers outlined in the feasibility study ( Annex 2).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 24

## Activity 5.1: The Development of education sector risk analyses on the impact of climate change and climate risks on the education sector and children’s learning

### Background

The Ministry of Education in BRACE countries will be supported with analysis that explores the expected impact of climate change and environmental degradation on the education sector and children's learning. This will include an assessment of the current state of education infrastructure, including school buildings, water supply systems, and other facilities, as well as the capacity of these institutions to adapt and respond to climate change.

### Objectives

1. To develop a comprehensive risk analysis framework for the education sector in BRACE countries.
2. To identify key risks associated with climate change and environmental degradation on the education sector and children's learning.
3. To provide recommendations for strengthening the resilience of the education sector against climate change impacts, including building capacity to adapt and respond.

### Outputs

1. A risk analysis framework for the education sector in BRACE countries.
2. Recommendations for strengthening the resilience of the education sector against climate change impacts.
3. Capacity-building activities for education sector stakeholders on climate change adaptation strategies.

## Activity 5.2: Support Ministries of Education to coordinate with climate change Ministries and engage in NAP processes

### Background

This activity will support Ministries in BRACE countries to advocate and ensure education is reflected in climate change adaptation plans (NAPs). The support will be provided through a series of workshops, training sessions, and capacity-building activities. These will involve providing training and capacity building support for Ministries of Education on UNFCCC processes and how to engage with the NAP process.

### Objectives

1. To ensure that education is adequately reflected in climate change adaptation plans (NAPs) developed by BRACE countries.
2. To provide technical assistance and guidance to Ministries of Education on how to integrate education into national climate action plans.

### Outputs

1. Enhanced capacity of Ministries of Education to advocate for the inclusion of education in NAP processes.
2. Improved understanding of UNFCCC processes among education sector stakeholders.
3. Recommendations for integrating education into national climate action plans.

## Activity 5.3: Key stakeholders (in BRACE countries) have increased access to, and the utility to, utilize scaled-up finance for climate action in the education sector

### Background

Climate finance is increasing but Ministries of Education in the BRACE countries are not accessing their fair share. The challenge lies in the complexity of accessing funds from multiple sources, including national and international funding mechanisms.

### Objectives

1. To increase access to and utilization of scaled-up climate finance for education sector projects.
2. To support Ministries of Education in BRACE countries to better understand and navigate existing financing mechanisms.

### Outputs

1. Enhanced capacity of Ministries of Education to access and utilize climate finance for education sector projects.
2. Improved understanding among education sector stakeholders on how to leverage available funding sources effectively.

## Activity 5.4: Development of climate finance roadmap building block

### Background

Through a better understanding of the impacts of climate change on the education sector (Activity 2.1.1), this activity will support Ministries in BRACE countries to develop a climate finance roadmap that outlines how to access and utilize funds for climate action.

### Objectives

1. To develop a climate finance roadmap that outlines strategies for accessing and utilizing climate finance for education sector projects.
2. To ensure the accessibility of all climate finance mechanisms in each country, mapping out co-financing and opportunity partnerships with other sectors.

### Outputs

1. A climate finance roadmap for BRACE countries to guide their efforts in accessing and utilizing climate finance for education sector projects.
2. Enhanced understanding among education sector stakeholders on how to access and utilize climate finance effectively.

## Activity 5.5: Direct Access Entity (DAE) support with working for the education sector on GCF proposals

### Background

This activity will target DAEs and emerging DAs in BRACE countries, it will focus on building the DAE's engagement with the education sector to ensure that climate change adaptation projects are aligned with GCF priorities.

### Objectives

1. To enhance the capacity of Direct Access Entities (DAEs) to engage effectively with the education sector.
2. To support the development of GCF proposals for climate change adaptation projects in BRACE countries, ensuring alignment with GCF priorities and funding opportunities.

### Outputs

1. Enhanced engagement between DAEs and the education sector on GCF proposals.
2. Improved understanding among education sector stakeholders on how to develop effective GCF proposals aligned with GCF priorities.

## Activity 5.6: Climate change education co-investment platform is established and effective in aligning strategic initiatives and financing

### Background

The climate change education co-investment platform will be established through a partnership between BRACE and global climate change and education stakeholders, focusing on the alignment of climate finance with education sector priorities.

### Objectives

1. To establish an effective co-investment platform for climate change education in BRACE countries.
2. To ensure that climate finance is aligned with strategic initiatives and financing needs within the education sector.

### Outputs

1. An established climate change education co-investment platform in BRACE countries.
2. Enhanced alignment of climate finance with education sector priorities, ensuring effective use of resources for climate action.

To ensure that education in education is strengthened through greater political recognition, enhanced coordination, and increased financing in Cambodia, Tonga and South Sudan this output will also contribute to ensuring sustainability of the climate change education sector and its linkages with other sectors around climate finance.

## 65. Activity 3.1.1: Support a BRACE Climate and Education Financing Coordination Platform, including steering

The BRACE platform will establish a climate and education coordination platform focused on financing to strengthen climate action in the education sector. The platform will be used as an entry point for climate change adaptation and mitigation activities that can strengthen alignment of diverse investments in the climate/education nexus, and to bring education into climate change policy dialogue. It will also provide a space for donors to support BRACE countries in implementing their climate change adaptation activities at the platform level. In addition, a core team will be established to deliver on the activities outlined below and in Output 3.1.2.

### 65.1 Activity 3.1.1.1: Facilitate the establishment of the BRACE Climate and Education Financing Coordination Platform

The BRACE coordination platform outlined in Activity 3.1.1 will provide a space to engage donors to further support BRACE countries in implementing their climate change adaptation activities at the platform level.

### 65.2 Activity 3.1.1.2: Facilitate the development of guidance materials (Output 3.1.2), hosting webinars (Activity 3.1.2) and maps (Activity 3.1.3), and link to other climate change and education tools and platforms.

### 65.3 Activity 3.1.2: Face-to-face meetings between Ministries of Education and Environment for cross-learning and building political momentum

The BRACE coordination platform will bring together the Ministries of Education, Environment, and Finance together for cross-learning and building political momentum for climate change and education. This will involve showcasing early successes in the BRACE countries to build on these successes and encourage other countries to join the initiative. The meeting will also provide an opportunity for the BRACE countries to share lessons learned from their experiences.

This will include face-to-face meetings at key moments, including COP 30 and beyond, identified by the steering committee outlined in Activity 3.1.1.

### 65.4 Activity 3.1.3: Map and track global climate finance opportunities in the education sector

This activity involves the mapping and tracking of global climate finance needs and opportunities from global climate funds (GCF, KfW, etc.), multilateral development banks (MDBs), bilateral donors, private sector investors, philanthropists, etc.

Findings will be translated into easy to understand and use guides with information by country that is updated regularly and hosted on the CIESI knowledge management platform as outlined in Activity 3.2.1 for easy access in Tonga, Cambodia and South Sudan.

### 65.5 Activity 3.1.4: Support adjustments to international climate finance reporting to reflect dual-benefit investments

This activity will support the development of common methodologies to track the volume of education investments which have a dual benefit (e.g., co-benefits for climate change adaptation and mitigation) in line with the work of the Climate Development (CDE) Development Assistance Committee (DAC) climate finance tracking to better reflect the role of education in climate action. It will also design and test the methodologies in the BRACE countries to ensure they can be replicated and scaled more broadly.

### 65.6 Output 3.2: Key stakeholders are supported to engage in peer learning and exchange through south-south

This output will focus on ensuring that key stakeholders in Tonga, Cambodia and South Sudan from the global education and climate change sectors have access to information about climate change adaptation activities at the platform level.

The activity includes leveraging the CIESI knowledge management platform, the development of guidance and knowledge products on climate change adaptation for the education sector, the organization of peer learning events, and the development and dissemination of strategic communication materials. The activities will address challenges in accessing and using information about climate change adaptation.

## B.4. Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words; approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)

As a multi-country project, BRACE will have multiple Executing Entities (EEs). Each country's project implementation will be carried out by an EE that has been selected through the competitive tender process of the GPEF Grant Agents. The CIP will flow with fiduciary requirements in accordance with CGI standards. In the Tonga, the EE is a newly registered legal entity with Save the Children International (SCI) as its implementing partner. In South Sudan, the EE is the Ministry of Education and Training (MEST). In South Sudan EE the EE will be Save the Children International in South Sudan, SICTON, SCI South Sudan, and the Ministry of General Education and Instruction (MGIEI). In Uganda, the EE is the Ministry of Education and Sports (MES). In Uganda, the EE is the Ministry of Education and Training (MEST) serving as an implementing partner, co-locating with Save the Children International (SCI) alongside the Country Coordination Unit (CCU), the Ministry of Education, Youth, and Sports (MEYS) serving as the overall project management unit, the Uganda National Curriculum Development Centre (UNCDC) serving as the sectoral coordination and technical support unit, and the Uganda National Curriculum Development Centre (UNCDC) serving as the sectoral coordination and technical support unit. In Viet Nam, the EE is the Ministry of Education and Training (MEST).

Implementing partner UNESCO is a sole-source procured party for this project. UNESCO will have no discretion in selecting partners or implementing entities.

No financial flows will be made until the PIsU has been established, and the PIUs have received final approval from the GCF for consultation.

### 77. Financial requirements standards of the GCF: legal and management oversight of the PIUs will be held by SC Tonga, SCI Cambodia and SCI South Sudan in the three countries respectively. All have the legal standing required to act as a PIU. The PIUs will also be responsible for ensuring that all financial transactions are conducted under the terms of the GCF Agreement with SCA, which acts as AE and holds the AMA with the GCF on behalf of the SC.

### 78. Each PIU will be responsible for establishing a project-specific bank account managed by the PIU in Tonga, Cambodia, and South Sudan. The PIUs will use these accounts to manage all financial transactions related to the projects. The PIUs will also be responsible for ensuring that the country Ministries, implementing partner (UNESCO), and any other procured partners (NMEs, implementing partners) are able to access their funds.

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 28

### B

**The project steering committee in Cambodia will be co-chaired by Save the Children International Cambodia (SCICambodia) Country Director and MOEYS Project Director. Members of the committee will include the Deputy Project Directors from both SCICambodia and MOEYS, the Chief of Party for the GCF Accelerated Funding Resources programme, a Project Secretariat and will be a part team comprising MOEYS and Save the Children Cambodia project staff who will work in close collaboration with the Ministry of Education, Youth and Sport (MOEYS) to ensure accountability to the Project Steering Committee (PSC) ensuring project governance and strategic guidance from the PSC.**

**Save the Children Cambodia has extensive experience delivering climate resilience and education projects, positioning it well to lead this project. The organization has a strong track record in implementing Climate Change Adaptation Generation programmes, a successful initiative integrating environmental education into formative and informal learning activities for children aged 5-14 years old. Save the Children Cambodia also has extensive experience with environmental challenges, with a focus on climate change education, waste management, and water conservation. Its projects have been implemented in schools across Cambodia, including the development of teaching units and hands-on eco-projects in schools.**

**Save the Children Cambodia has also demonstrated its capacity for emergency response and disaster preparedness through its work under the ASEAN Safe Schools Initiative and the development of Cambodia’s School Safety Guidelines. These projects have been implemented with a focus on ensuring continuity of education, including the provision of temporary shelters and educational continuity during extreme weather events. Save the Children Cambodia's strong partnership with the Cambodian government has enabled it to implement these projects in close collaboration with MOEYS, while also ensuring vulnerable children continue schooling even in the face of environmental crises.**

**Save the Children International South Sudan (SCISSD) will be co-chaired by SCICambodia and SCISSD. The project team will work in close collaboration and coordination with the Ministry of General Education and Instruction (MOGE) as an implementing partner. SCISSD has extensive experience managing education initiatives, including the GCF Accelerated Funding Resources programme integrated within the Global Partnership for Education (GPE). This programme has provided over $10 million to SCISSD in support of its $50 million for this program. Save the Children has proven its capacity to work with diverse donors such as GPE, ECW, UNICEF, and others. The project team will be supported by a Project Implementation Unit (PIU) that includes ministries like MOGES and SCISSD, as well as a wide range of international and local partners, ensuring the effective coordination of resources to deliver education services in South Sudan. Save the Children has extensive experience with organizations to ensure that even the most marginalized children are reached.**

**The GCF Accelerated Funding programme mobilizes shared responsibilities in planning, implementation, monitoring, and evaluation, enhancing accountability and sustainability. This partnership model ensures that all parties involved have a clear understanding of their roles and responsibilities. The Children's education programme focuses on addressing the protection and developmental rights of vulnerable children through the provision of quality education services. It also includes Early Childhood Development (ECD), Education for children with disabilities, and Education in Emergencies (EE). Additionally, it builds climate-resilient education systems to ensure long-term sustainability.**

## B

### Activities will be implemented in accordance with the World's General Action Plan and Action Plan (see Annexes A), which is aligned with the GCF’s Gender Policy and SC1 gender, inclusive development, and child safeguarding policies and Provisions of Sexual Exploitation and abuse policy and guidelines for the General Action Plan. Anticipated outcomes are incorporated into health facility upgrades where possible.

### B.S. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

The proposed project seeks to support the Government of Cambodia in implementing a national education sector plan to integrate climate change into the education sector. This funding is essential to enable Least Developed Country (LDC) countries such as Cambodia to meet their commitments under the Paris Agreement and the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The project will provide technical assistance, capacity building, and resources for the education sector in order to plan what they can and should be doing to protect education sector investment. This is a critical time for action on climate change, with many countries facing significant challenges due to limited planning; there is a gap in funds available for action at scale. Due to no clear financial return on investment, private sector funding has been slow to materialize. The project will provide technical assistance and capacity building that demonstrates a strong investment case in the face of a changing climate (see Annex 3 EFA).

The proposed project aligns with the following SDGs: SDG 4 (Quality Education) and SDG 13 (Climate Action). It demonstrates a commitment to providing quality education while addressing the urgent need for climate change adaptation. By equipping young people with the skills and knowledge to adapt to climate change, the programme contributes to the achievement of both SDGs.

Ministries of Education in Cambodia, South Sudan, and Tonga and beyond in CSEIS countries lack sufficient finance to integrate climate change into the education sector (see Component 1).

### Component 1

**Cambodia**

Climatic hazards are highly vulnerable to climate change, facing recurrent natural hazards such as floods, droughts, and extreme weather events. These climate impacts, as detailed in the National Action Plan for Disaster Risk Reduction (NAP DRR) 2024-2035, disrupt educational services, destroy infrastructure, and displace communities, compounding existing challenges in the education sector. Despite being a low emitter of greenhouse gases, Cambodia is disproportionately

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 10

## South Sudan

### Section 5.2: Education Sector

The Government of South Sudan does not currently have sufficient funding and required capacity to address the urgent need for climate resilience in the education sector, particularly given geographic disparities in access to education resources.

Despite the education budget increase over the past three years, there are still significant gaps in the provision of quality primary education and support system transformations. Current donor funding focuses on basic education support but does not cover other critical areas such as school infrastructure and safety during extreme weather events.

The education sector is highly vulnerable despite negligible Greenhouse Gas (GHG) emissions. Climate change results in extreme weather events, which exacerbate existing challenges within the education sector, creating an urgent need for a climate resilience program.

### Section 5.3: Tonga

In Tonga, the Government has been generally reliant on foreign funding and remittances of a significant number of citizens who have left the country to work abroad, which contributed to nearly 40% of the total national budget. The Government does not currently have sufficient funding or capacity to meet the immediate adaptation needs of the education sector.

Tonga's contribution to global GHG emissions is negligible, yet Tonga is one of the most vulnerable countries in the world due to its low-lying islands and reliance on fisheries for food security and economic sectors such as tourism. The country’s temperature is high; it has experienced a gradual decline from 2015 onwards.

The economy has been hit by multiple shocks: cyclone Gita in 2018, the Covid-19 pandemic, cyclone Harold in 2020 and the recent floods. These shocks have led to significant economic losses and increased spending needs for recovery over the coming years due to a gradual decrease in grants under the current commitments and growing spending needs for climate adaptation.

The country is also facing challenges with debt repayment to other countries; 85% of Tonga's upcoming debt payments will flow to other countries as it seeks other concessional loans. This situation has led to concerns about Tonga’s ability to meet its debt obligations by 2026. These current repayments will severely hamper Tonga's recovery and create much-needed funds for climate adaptation.

The lack of global climate finance is a significant challenge for Tonga. It is apparent that SDF status as representing a small island developing state (SIDS) does not necessarily translate into the same level of support as other SIDS.

## National Policy Alignment

The project is aligned with national policies in various countries to promote long-term sustainability.

### South Sudan
- **BRACE**: The project builds on the South Sudan Climate Change and Development Plan (SCDP) and the Revised National Development Strategy (RNDS).
- **BRACE focuses on**:
  - Gender-responsive policies, safer and greener school facilities.
  - Development of rural schools as climate-resilient infrastructure.
  - Ensuring minimal disruption to learning even during climate-related crises.

### BRAC in South Sudan
- Enhances education policies with a focus on climate-smart systems, environmental sustainability, and disaster risk management.
- Supports the implementation of the Climate Change Adaptation and Disaster Risk Management (CCADRM) 2015–2028 and the Tonga Education Policy Framework.

### BRAC in Cambodia
- Enhances the capacity of M&EYS and other relevant ministries to integrate climate resilience into educational planning and access climate finance.
- Implements the Climate Change Adaptation and Disaster Risk Management (CCADRM) 2015–2028, and the Tonga Education Policy Framework.

### BRAC in South Sudan
- Enhances the capacity of M&EYS and other relevant ministries to integrate climate resilience into educational planning and access climate finance.
- Implements the Climate Change Adaptation and Disaster Risk Management (CCADRM) 2015–2028, and the Tonga Education Policy Framework.

### Scaling best practices
- Focuses on scaling best practices across regions, ensuring maximum impact in South Sudan. Best practices include:
  - Climate-resilient education.
  - Water management.
  - Disaster preparedness.
  - Climate-resilient infrastructure planning.
  - Knowledge-sharing and learning.
  - Establishment of green school networks.
  - Implementation of the Green Generation initiative.

### BRAC in Tonga
- Builds on pre-assessment evaluations, such as the Outer Island Renewable Energy Project (OIREP).
- Focuses on:
  - Improving energy efficiency through solar PV systems.
  - Upgrading most climate-vulnerable schools to enhance resilience against extreme weather events.
  - Ensuring inclusive WASH facilities.

B

## Operations and Maintenance Strategy

12A: A robust operations and maintenance (O&M) plan is vital to the sustainability of the project's infrastructure and activities.

The project will ensure that all schools in the three BRAC countries will have the upkeep of water systems and climate-resilient school buildings, supported by ongoing monitoring and supervision to maintain effectiveness. The project includes providing training for school staff on basic upkeep and maintenance of school infrastructure and WASH facilities to prevent common issues from recurring.

The project will provide training to school staff and local administration on maintaining school infrastructure, while linking these efforts to broader community-based approaches that ensure gender and disability inclusivity. The project will also provide training in problem-solving for Internet connectivity issues, ensuring the sustainability of the Internet and monitoring systems over time with local stakeholders equipped to manage ongoing maintenance.

The strategy for Component 2 will ensure that Ministries of Education either have access to or are able to develop a robust O&M plan for their infrastructure. The project will ensure sustained results whereby Ministries of Education are leading efforts to build resilience and adapt to the impacts of climate change on school infrastructure, including water systems and WASH facilities.

Throughout the course of implementation, the most appropriate place for the platform to be housed will be identified as a result of ongoing monitoring and evaluation. The platform will continue to be accessible to all stakeholders after the end of the project. Furthermore, knowledge management assets will remain accessible to climate and education actors.

D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

The section reviews the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF's Initial Investment Framework.

### D.1 Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### Component 1: The project will make a contribution to GCF’s overarching adaptation impact, contributing to climate-resilient development and sustainable livelihoods for vulnerable communities across the three focus countries in Cambodia, Tonga and South Sudan. BRAC's total direct beneficiary population is 607,089 (comprising 259,953 females and 347,136 males), of which 30% are women and girls. The indirect beneficiaries will also address climate impacts and risks in 850 schools. The breakdown by countries under Component 1 is:

- **Cambodia:** At least 240 primary schools are selected as target schools in 3 provinces of Kampong Chhnang, Kampong Speu and Stung Treng. These schools will be supported with climate-resilient infrastructure and training for teachers on disaster risk reduction (DRR) and climate change adaptation. The education sector is significant, particularly due to the provinces' vulnerability to extreme weather events, especially flooding. Direct beneficiaries include 102,345 students (comprising 48% females), school staff, parents, school committees, principals, caregivers and other community members, education officials from the MoEYS, and Province and District Education Officers. The indirect beneficiaries will be reached through training and directly engaging with our project activities. A total of 392,165 direct beneficiaries will be reached (102,345 students + 87,820 staff + 202,000 parents), while a MAF of 4,231,400 indirect beneficiaries will be reached (1,982,126 families) amounting to 24% of the population.

- **South Sudan:** Children within a total of 510 schools across the project, with an estimated total of 207,242 direct beneficiaries (comprising 38.5% females), will be supported through climate-resilient infrastructure and training for teachers on DRR and climate change adaptation. These schools are located in areas most vulnerable to extreme weather events such as flooding, droughts, landslides, and heatwaves. The indirect beneficiaries include 102,943 families (comprising 57% females) living within the wider communities). Of these, just over half (52%) will be women and girls. BRAC in South Sudan will also impact an estimated total of 68,000 people through its other projects, including the CSESJ project that works in a total of 35 countries by sharing learning from the implementation of BRAC project with other countries.

- **Tonga:** The climate resilience of 100 remote schools will be strengthened to better cope with climate hazards such as sea level rise, droughts, flooding, extreme weather including tropical cyclones and heatwaves, and ongoing impacts of climate change. These schools are located in areas most vulnerable to these climate hazards. The direct beneficiaries include 362,744 students (comprising 51% females) out of a total Tongan population; in addition to 38,762 indirect beneficiaries (19,381 female) equating to 33% of the total population.

#### Component 2: Of BRAC's target Ministries of Education in the three BRACE focus countries supporting them to implement climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction activities through their schools, the direct beneficiaries will be 210 Ministry of Education and other government officials from these three focus countries, with the indirect beneficiaries being 35,847 families (comprising 69% females) living within the wider communities. The CSESJ project that works in a total of 35 countries by sharing learning from the implementation of BRAC project with other countries.

#### Component 3: Of BRACE will directly reach 1,000, including the education and climate change actors and implementers across each of the three countries, including civil society. As with Component 2, the parallel project financed CSESJ project will support replication of the activities supported through Component 3 to a further 32 countries.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 37

#### Scalability and replicability

This project has been designed from the outset with both stability and reliability in mind. In alignment with the Climate-Smart School (CSS) model, the BRACE will scale up high-impact climate-focused curricula and interventions with other relevant climate-focused projects. The CSSF model will be scaled up to ensure additional schools will receive resources and support for climate education, including the development of a curriculum and teacher training materials (output 1.3), and the development of climate education curriculum and teacher training (output 4.1). Furthermore, the project will develop a replicability plan that includes lessons learned from the pilot phase to guide scaling up and replication elsewhere and adapted to relevant country contexts – this includes standards, guidelines and plans, T&T approaches, approaches in strengthening CSE and EWS, Climate-related School Safety Plans etc.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 38

## Knowledge sharing and learning agenda

141 Through Component 2 and 3, BRACE will showcase how climate action can be scaled up in the education sector by increasing the capacity of teachers to integrate climate change into their teaching practices across different educational systems. Components 2 and 3 have a strong focus on knowledge sharing and cross-country learning events. The CSSEF will support the development of a knowledge management system for scaling up CCF processes such as NAP development in the three target countries. Knowledge products on these topics will be produced through workshops, webinars, and other events organized by the CSSEF and shared with stakeholders locally and through webinars and cross-country learning events for the three target countries.

142 The MEAL framework will be used to evaluate the impact of interventions in the three target countries using the MEAL framework, to identify causal impact of the interventions. Both BRACE and CSSEF will support learning events as part of the research process. These will include workshops, webinars, and other events organized by the CSSEF. Some of the interventions are relatively new within the country contexts and as such BRACE is in a unique position to test these ideas on a moderate scale and share key findings with policy bodies.

### D.3 Sustainable development impact: 500 words; approximately | 1 page


---
**Page 45**
---

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 39

#### 143 BRACE will contribute to several Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). Specifically: SDG4: Quality education for all; SDG5: Achieve gender equality and empower all women and girls; SDG6: Ensure availability and sustainable management of water and sanitation for all; SDG8: Promote inclusive, safe, resilient and sustainable settlements; SDG13: Take urgent action to combat climate change and its impacts.

#### 144. There are three co-benefits delivered through the BRACE project:

- **Co-benefit 1:** Gender and social inclusion BRACE will indirectly benefit 4,128,574 females during implementation. Increased access to education for girls and women will be achieved by providing GRSI (gender-sensitive) water infrastructure sector, beyond what is delivered in the core project, based on social attitudes and behavioural changes arising from the introduction of gender equality principles into the curriculum. This will also provide opportunities for girls’ education and knowledge sharing opportunities through which the continued advocacy of gender climate needs, impacts and action will be strengthened. The BRACE project will also contribute to SDG5 by providing access to safe drinking water/WASH facilities will be provided. These are also likely to be used by the community, either at times of extreme weather events or during the dry season. This will provide opportunities for girls’ education and knowledge sharing through promoting menstrual hygiene, and creating dignifying kits for girls and teachers at schools, allowing for knock-on capacity building in terms of gender equality and climate change adaptation. The project will also contribute to SDG10 by reducing the burden on women and girls who bear the brunt of activities related to water collection and washing. BRACE will also contribute to SDG8 through providing opportunities for girls’ education and knowledge sharing through the DRR committees which involve children, women and people with disabilities in remote communities. New or updated building designs will be developed that are gender-sensitive and climate-resilient. The project will also contribute to SDG13 by addressing climate-related school safety plans. EWS information dissemination will adopt language which is age, gender and disability responsive.

- **Co-benefit 2:** Environmental impact through school greening via reduced emissions from infrastructure paired with the introduction of sustainable agriculture practices. This will be achieved by providing opportunities for schools to include tree planting and water efficient irrigation systems, for shading, windbreaks and cooling effects. Supporting schools in developing their own climate action plans (CAP) will also contribute to SDG13. The project will also contribute to SDG6 through improved WASH facilities, including latrines, improved waste management system and improved hygiene practices.

- **Co-benefit 3:** Social: Increased Locally-led Adaptation (LLA): Influencing the LLA agenda working at the local school level with children, resident teachers and parents/community members. LLA can be amplified through project development and implementation of climate action plans (CAP) which will include a focus on gender equality. This will lead to increased roadmap development and collaboration/south-south learning. Such interventions lend themselves to LLA such as through the introduction of tree planting and water efficient irrigation systems, for shading, windbreaks and cooling effects. School engagement may amplify further through knowledge sharing as well as opportunities for advocacy at key events such as the World Climate Change Conference (COP21). The project will also contribute to SDG5 by ensuring that climate action is strongly aligned with the principles of Locally-led Adaptation which will empower children as key stakeholders in decision-making and learning climate action. This co-benefit is achieved through outcomes 1, 2 and 3.

#### D.4 Needs of recipient (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

The recipients of BRACE include schools, school communities (children, parents, teachers and those living nearby to the schools) and the education sector as a whole. The education sector’s vulnerability to climate change is evident from the direct impact on school infrastructure, the indirect effects on learning and health, and the broader economic and social impacts that are likely to be experienced by the education sector in the future. This will lead to the education system to withdraw and adapt to the continued and worsening challenges posed by a changing climate. The countries targeted for BRACE include: Bangladesh, Benin, Burkina Faso, Côte d’Ivoire, Ethiopia, Ghana, Kenya, Sudan and Tonga of droughts, heatwaves, reduced-rainfall wildfires, rising sea levels, coastal erosion, floods and food insecurity. These are all likely to be exacerbated by climate change leading to increased vulnerability. Lack of financial investment in the education system to address these needs is extremely high.
