# GREEN CLIMATE FUND

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 40

In order to ensure that society is informed and capable of responding to climate change challenges, the climate vulnerability in the education sector must be addressed. The education sector must integrate climate risk management into its planning processes, including disaster risk reduction strategies, incorporating climate change into the curriculum, build pedagogical capacity and construct knowledge among teachers and educators, and supporting communities to adapt to climate change impacts.

### Cambodia

Cambodia is one of the poorest countries among the Association of Southeast Asian Nations (ASEAN) characterized by limited educational opportunities, high income inequality and poor job prospects. The country's vulnerability to climate change is exacerbated by its geography, with 90% of the land area being low-lying agrarian country, with 76% of the population living in rural areas. Along with the Philippines, Cambodia is regarded as one of the most vulnerable countries globally to climate change and extreme weather events. It is extremely vulnerable to the consequences of climate change. The ND-GAIN ranks Cambodia 14th most vulnerable country out of 180 countries. The country has experienced a number of natural disasters in recent years due to severe climate change vulnerabilities, ranking as the second most vulnerable country globally to climate hazards according to the Global Climate Risk Index.

### South Sudan

The UNDP, South Sudan is recognized as being particularly vulnerable to economic shocks while having urgent and immediate adaptation needs. South Sudan is ranked by the ND-GAIN as the 8th most vulnerable country in the world. The country has experienced a number of natural disasters in recent years due to severe climate change vulnerabilities, ranking as the second most vulnerable country globally to climate hazards according to the Global Climate Risk Index.

### Tonga

Tonga's school children, surrounding communities and the education system is already – and will continue to - face sea level rise, droughts, flooding, and extreme weather including tropical cyclones and heatwaves. Climate change has a significant impact on the country’s ability to provide quality education for its students through specific courses in schools and during stakeholder consultations, government stakeholders emphasized the need to quickly address climate change impacts.

A World Bank report confirmed by consultations with government stakeholders indicated that schools especially on the outer islands are highly vulnerable to climate risks and urgency need infrastructural support. Past climatic


---
**Page 47**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 41

D

events in 2018 and 2020 were highly disruptive of children's education and associated with high financial costs.

Although there are previous projects focused on school safety and continuity related to disasters, the climate lens has been missing.

Unfortunately, domestic and international funding sources are insufficient to meet the adaptation needs of the education sector. Most development assistance is directed towards disaster risk reduction (DRR) and focuses primarily on immediate development priorities with only a minor focus on climate change, and often with a mitigation focus.

152 Country ownership is critical for successful implementation of climate-resilient projects. However, this capacity is generally lacking in the target countries. It's clear that as climate impacts and risks increase, the viability of any investments for the education sector will be increasingly compromised unless they are designed to be resilient to these changes. We need to collaborate, access finance, prioritise climate actions and share knowledge and lessons with a view to strengthen the country ownership focus in the process.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 42

## Policy Alignment and Support

153. BRACE is designed to enhance the resilience of the education sector in South Sudan, Cambodia, and Tonga against the impacts of climate change. Country ownership is embedded through BRACE's strong partnerships with government ministries and institutions responsible for education, disaster risk management, environment, climate change strategies, and gender action plans of these countries. BRACE aims to support national climate and education sectors while addressing common challenge faced by these diverse regions.

### Policy Alignment and Support

154. BRACE aligns with the national climate change strategies and educational policies of South Sudan, Cambodia, and Tonga. In South Sudan, the project aligns with the NAP and the Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs), prioritizing climate-resilient infrastructure and education continuity. The project also aligns with the Climate Change Policy (CCP) 2014-2023 and the National Action Plan for Disaster Risk Reduction (NAP-DRR), which emphasize integrating climate change adaptation into disaster risk reduction strategies, as well as the Education Sector Development Framework (ESDF) 2015-2025 and the Joint National Action Plan for Climate Change Adaptation and Disaster Risk Management (JNAPCCADRM). In Cambodia, BRACE aligns with the NDCs, NAP-DRR, and including education.

### Alignment with Gender Action Plans and Policies

156. Such alignment ensures that BRACE will support the implementation of national strategies by improving the climate resilience of school infrastructure, integrating climate change education into curricula, and enhancing disaster preparedness. It aligns with South Sudan's National Education Policy (2014-2030), Cambodia's Gender Mainstreaming Strategic Plan (2015-2025) and Tonga's National Action Plan for Climate Change Adaptation and Disaster Risk Management (NAPCCADRM). These strategies ensure that girls, boys, women, and men have equal access to climate-resilient educational facilities. Similarly, in Tonga BRACE aligns with the national gender equality policies and action plans. These interventions are inclusive of all genders and children with disabilities.

### Complementarity and Coherence with Government Policies and Plans

157. BRACE is designed to complement existing government strategies and frameworks, ensuring coherence with national development plans and sectoral strategies in South Sudan, Cambodia, and Tonga. The project leverages the existing institutional structures of these countries by aligning its activities with their climate change adaptation, climate-resilient infrastructure and educational continuity. In Cambodia, BRACE complements the National Socio-Economic Development Plan (2014-2030) and the National Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan (NCCSAP), which focus on environmental sustainability and natural resource management. Tonga benefits from the project's alignment with previous initiatives such as the Renewable Energy Project (REP) and the Sustainable Energy for All (SEforALL) and the Renewable Energy Project (TREP), which focus on enhancing the resilience of school infrastructure and improving energy access.

158. By building on these initiatives, BRACE maximizes its impact and contributes to a coordinated national response in each country. This ensures that projects are aligned with existing frameworks, allowing for the efficient use of resources and ensuring that project activities are sustainable and replicable.

### Key Partner Engagement and Government Leadership

159. The successful implementation of this proposal depends on strong partnerships with government ministries and institutions responsible for education, disaster risk management, environment, climate change strategies, and gender action plans in each country. In South Sudan, the Ministry of General Education and Instruction, the Ministry of Environment, and the Ministry of Disaster Management and Environment provide critical leadership to ensure that project activities align with national priorities. Similarly, in Cambodia, BRACE works closely with local government and development partners to integrate climate resilience into the education sector. In Tonga, the Ministry of Education, Climate Change and Disaster Management (ECDM), Environment, Climate Change and Communications (MECC) provide leadership.

160. In each country, BRACE engages local governments, community organizations, and school management committees in project planning and implementation. These stakeholders are continually involved to ensure ownership among local stakeholders; the project ensures that the benefits of climate resilience are maintained long after the project's completion.


---
**Page 49**
---

Ownership and Stakeholder Consultations

101. Please see Annex 7 for more information. Stakeholder engagement has been a critical component of BRAC's development work in all three target countries. Extensive consultations with government agencies, local communities, non-governmental organizations, and development partners have ensured that the project aligns with both national priorities and international commitments. These consultations have also helped to build the resilience of local infrastructure and integrating climate change education into the curriculum. In Cambodia, discussions with the Ministry of Education, Youth and Sport (MoEYS) and other stakeholders have ensured that the project is responsive to local capacities and vulnerabilities. Similarly, in Tonga, participatory processes involving government agencies, civil society organizations, and development partners have helped to identify opportunities related to climate change adaptation.

102. The BRAC Project has a strong track record of engaging with its target communities through its project design but also fostered broad support for its implementation. BRAC will continue to engage stakeholders throughout the project implementation, ensuring that they are involved in decision-making and providing feedback on the project's management, budget, and stakeholder feedback.

103. The project has engaged local communities and educational institutions in the project’s design and implementation and will further ensure that interventions are contextually appropriate and have a lasting impact.


The BRACE Project's expected impact: With an estimated total budget of USD 48.7 million, the projects GCF grant is estimated at USD 16.7 million, with a grant-to-financing ratio of 0.85 to 1. The project is expected to deliver to 6.6 million people in Cambodia, 239,000 people in Lao PDR and 40,000 people in Tonga through its various interventions.

Co-financiation justification: The proposed concessional financing level for the BRACE project is justified based on the ongoing need for highly concessional financing in these three target countries. This type of financing is vital for the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and the Paris Agreement, which are particularly relevant to the three target countries but also bring about a positive social change. These countries are particularly vulnerable to the adverse effects of climate change and have limited resources to address this challenge. The project will provide basic education shows quality of public good? It is typically funded by governments using national taxes or other forms of revenue, and it is often provided free of charge to all eligible individuals.

Budgetary constraints in target countries: Various factors, including budgeting, macroeconomic, and internal government organization aspects, contribute to this need for grant financing.


1. Cambodia: The country's fiscal deficit widened in 2023 due to increased spending to households and firms affected by the war in Ukraine, rising prices of goods and services, and increased spending associated with the elections and the 2023 South-East Asia Games have significantly strained the country’s resources. The public debt service ratio is projected to increase from 15% in 2022 to 24% in 2027. This will reduce the amount of public goods, including climate adaptation change the education system.


2. Lao PDR: The country has experienced a series of natural disasters and economic challenges resulting from several shocks, including the Red Sea crisis, the war in Sudan, domestic policy declines, and climate change. These factors have led to an increase in public debt service ratio, which is projected to rise from 15% in 2023 to 48% in 2027. This will reduce the amount of public goods, including climate adaptation change the education system.


3. Tonga: The country has experienced a series of natural disasters and economic challenges resulting from several shocks, including the Red Sea crisis, the war in Sudan, domestic policy declines, and climate change. These factors have led to an increase in public debt service ratio, which is projected to rise from 15% in 2023 to 48% in 2027. This will reduce the amount of public goods, including climate adaptation change the education system.


The public good concept encompasses non-rivalrous and non-excludable goods: While some argue that private basic education higher education is a public good, it is not necessarily so. The public good concept does not apply to private basic education higher education because it is rivalrous and excludable. Education benefits every member of society, making it essential for all of us to contribute to its funding through taxes.

## EFA modelling results

The ex-ante EFA was pursued separately for each of the three target countries in economic terms using two perspectives: (1) the perspective of direct beneficiaries on whose assistance and indicative public primary school infrastructure the project will be implemented; and (2) the perspective of the government to obtain BRACED funding. The EFA results were calculated over 15 years using a discount rate of 4% for all three countries, except for South Sudan where it was 6%. The table below shows how the results might change under differing climate hardships. The EFA results were calculated over 15 years using an annual discount rate of (a) 4%, and (b) 6%.

The detailed per-target-country results can be seen in Annex 3 of the FP package.

## Application of the best practices

The BRACED project is dedicated to maximizing impact by implementing proven interventions and best practices in targeted schools. Using a participatory and integrated approach, our objective is to create virtuous cycles of implementation, testing, and feedback to scale up efforts and generate economic returns for the intended beneficiaries and the entire economy.

## Replicability

### Low

Government entities in South Sudan, Cambodia, and Timor-Leste have developed unified rolling plan, unit and sector climate resilient, and gender-sensitive policies and strategies. However, institutional constraints at local, sub-national, and national levels limit the implementation of adaptation planning processes.

Ministries of education specialize in a range of educational processes, and train teachers to educate children thought knowledge of family cycles. They apply standardized methodologies (curriculum and testing) and are exempt from the replication of their usage.

The BRACE project will provide mechanisms for education sector, inter-sector coordination, access to climate information, finance, and implementation of adaptation actions at scale. These constraints also prevent governments have integrated institutional knowledge sharing and dissemination across

The activities and outcomes of output 1.2 (infrastructure), output 1.3 (continuity management), and output 1.4 (curriculum) will be provided through the project intervention. In some cases, these outputs may not be reached by the project intervention. In some cases, if infrastructure is not available or not suitable for adaptation (such as infrastructure) and not available, this could be addressed through capacity building in management practices and curriculum can be integrated into existing systems such as teacher training or development of management capacity.

The replication of output 1.1 is intended to spread the knowledge, policies, plans, and systems established in the 3 countries do not require national replication but will seek to replicate good practice through education sector (teachers, schools, and NGOs) engaged in Component 3.

## Sustainability

The introduction of new technologies such as solar panels in South Sudan, Cambodia and Tonga often fail to take off due to a lack of robust government environmental investment training and capacity strengthening, weak systems and procedures to replicate and scale up, and a lack of widespread ownership particularly from groups traditionally excluded areas, women, girls, boys, youth, and other groups that experience inequality and discrimination.

### Low

The largest concern is to effectively embed into the education system an understanding of climate change impacts on education and to provide the tools, systems, policies, and training for teachers' capabilities for respective institutions to implement necessary strengthening and adaptations to their existing ways of working in order to increase resilience of the education system to climate change impacts and the curriculum they teach. As noted above there is a need to build up to replicate and scale up systems and procedures.

Therefore, the project will focus on embedding into the deep educational systems and processes where knowledge and understanding can be sustained.

## Core indicator: Degree to which GCF investments contribute to effective knowledge management processes, and use of good practices and methodologies and standards

### No systematic approach to sharing knowledge and good practices for climate resilient education.

- **Monitoring the arrangements by annual monitoring and reporting arrangement (MRA) will be a key element of the project-specific performance management system. The MRA will include detailed information on the roles and responsibilities for data collection and management, information flow and reporting systems, finalized indicators and means of verification, monitoring protocols and tools, implementation plans and schedules to be defined within 6 months of project inception. Output data will be collected an ongoing basis as part of monitoring activities to track progress against each indicator. process midterm evaluation will be conducted to assess project progress against the outcome indicators and recommend improvements for the last two years of the project.

# F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1 Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)

### Selected Risk Factor 1 - Low Technical Capacity

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and Operational | High | Medium |

#### Description
- Limited technical capacity among local stakeholders, including education authorities and community members.
- This limits the ability to effectively implement climate change adaptation measures in South Sudan, Cambodia, and Tonga, potentially leading to suboptimal project outcomes.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
1. The BRACE initiative will implement a capacity building program targeting government officials, local communities, and project partners across South Sudan, Cambodia, and Tonga.
2. This program will focus on establishing a strong technical foundation for climate change adaptation in schools and communities aligned with national frameworks like Cambodia's Comprehensive School Safety Framework (CSSF) and the Child-Friendly Schools (CFS) framework.
3. The initiative will also support capacity building through knowledge sharing and coordination, particularly through activities such as virtual and offline child-and-youth-led community dialogues on climate change adaptation.

#### Selected Risk Factor 2 - Climate Vulnerability

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Medium | Medium |

#### Description
- High vulnerability to climate change impacts due to extreme weather events and sea-level rise, could disrupt project activities and threaten the safety and livelihoods of target communities in South Sudan, Cambodia, and Tonga.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
1. BRACE will incorporate climate risk assessments into its planning and implementation phases to ensure that infrastructure projects are resilient against climate change impacts.
2. The initiative will also work with local authorities to enhance the project's ability to withstand extreme weather, including improving airfield, drainage, and accessibility.
3. In Cambodia, the initiative will support the development of a national early warning system (EWS) platform for real-time impact monitoring, enabling schools to better prepare and respond to climate-related events.

#### Selected Risk Factor 3 - Fraud and Corruption

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| MUIF | Medium | Low |


---
**Page 109**
---

### Description

171. The risk of fraud and corruption in financial management and procurement processes could lead to the misallocation of funds, compromising the project's integrity and outcomes.

172. Key mitigation measures will include strict adherence to anti-Raids and anti-corruption policies, compliance with national institutional structures, and the application of robust monitoring and reporting procedures. BRACIE will establish a dedicated team for financial management and procurement oversight, with independent external oversight provided by an independent PMD team. Digital tools will be employed to track expenditures and procurements in real-time, providing opportunities for early detection of irregularities. A whistleblower hotline will be established to encourage the reporting of suspicious activities. An internal audit mechanism will be established to ensure compliance with financial regulations and procedures. A whistleblowing protection mechanism will be established to encourage the reporting of suspicious activities.

Selected Risk Factor 4 - Construction Risk

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical | Low | Medium |

173. Lack of design input and requisite supervisory budget results in poor quality of design and poor workmanship.

### Mitigation Measures

174. Supervision will be carried out by qualified Structural Engineers/Construction Managers or a qualified third party. Technical consultations will include the Save the Children Regional Office Construction Advisor, who will provide technical advice on construction supervision and also covers construction costs, staff supervision, and fleet requirements. Safety and security assessments will be conducted to ensure that all safety and security measures are in place before commencing work. The retrofitting design aims to enhance building resilience and will include planning that covers site surveys, water and power connections, and other relevant considerations. The project team will conduct a feasibility study on these considerations, building consent acquisition, and market survey for material availability. Adequate time will be allocated for the completion of all necessary documentation (including Safety Audit and Accessibility Assessment), tendering, contractor assessment, construction implementation, and post-construction monitoring. A qualified third party will be engaged to carry out the proposed rehabilitation/retrofitting of school buildings under ongoing structural engineering assessment by a qualified civil engineer and will incorporate design principles for vulnerable beneficiaries, integrated WASH, and inclusion measures.

# G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words; approximately 1.5 pages)

### Summary of Risks

#### ESS 2 Labour and working conditions

The project proposes activities including the construction of footings/foundations related to the projected impacts of climate change.

Project activities will include some small-scale physical works such as concrete pouring, which may potentially result in exposure to typical physical health and safety risks associated with these types of works. This risk is assessed as low since all these activities are small-scale and can be managed through standard operating procedures (SOPs) and the provision of personal protective equipment (PPE).

#### ESS 3 Resource Efficiency and Pollution Prevention

Small quantities of concrete will be required for concrete footings/foundations of some project types which may potentially lead to localized contamination or soil erosion if not washed away.

With concrete use, there will be small quantities of concrete slurry waste which can be skin-to-skin. The ESAP provides management measures for the use of concrete.

Concrete will be prepared on bunded and covered hard stand surface.
- All excess concrete production will be collected to allow particulates to settle out before being discharged.
- Slurry from concrete production will be collected allowed to settle, then pumped into a slurry tank. The slurry can be reused by the community for infrastructure projects.

Workers will be provided with appropriate PPE. This is considered low risk given the expected small quantities of waste and the fact that standard management measures are known to be effective and easily implemented.

#### ESS 6 Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Management of Livestock Resources

The project proposed to develop gardens in South Sudan, which may potentially lead to the potential for invasive plant species. Only a few garden plots will be planted at schools in South Sudan. Under the project's activity exclusion list, only one school garden plot per school is allowed.

### Mitigation Factors/Measures of Management Strategies

Project activities will include some small-scale physical works such as concrete pouring which may potentially result in exposure to typical physical health and safety risks associated with these types of works.
- This risk is assessed as low since all these activities are small-scale and can be managed through standard operating procedures (SOPs) and the provision of personal protective equipment (PPE).

There is the potential for small-scale physical works requiring labourers to employ children in some project countries. This risk is assessed as low since the activities will not require large numbers of workers, and where they do occur, schools where mitigation measures can be readily implemented are prioritized.

The ESAP provides management measures for the use of concrete.
- All excess concrete production will be collected to allow particulates to settle out before being discharged.
- Slurry from concrete production will be collected allowed to settle, then pumped into a slurry tank. The slurry can be reused by the community for infrastructure projects.

Workers will be provided with appropriate PPE. This is considered low risk given the expected small quantities of waste and the fact that standard management measures are known to be effective and easily implemented.

This is considered low risk as the project has few places to avoid due to the use of native species and the gardens being planted in areas within already altered environments.

The project does not have any activities impacting the lands or resources of indigenous groups and there are no activities that would require Free, Prior and Informed Consent (FPIC).

To ensure best practice in consultation activities is used, the project will integrate the inclusion of all identified indigenous minority groups into Project design and stakeholder engagement. The project activity design as well as the SEP integrates the inclusion of any identified indigenous minority group.

This is considered low risk as no lands or resources used by indigenous people other minority groups are required for or impacted by the project.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 107

## The Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan was developed by gender experts across the three countries using secondary data including key informant interviews and focus group discussions.

The analyses presented in Annex B considers the ways in which gender and social norms interact with adaptive capacities and climate change vulnerability, as well as the different impacts of climate change on women and men. The analysis covers five domains and includes recommendations for the project across all components and outputs. The Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan is structured around the following three component logic frameworks for the 3 countries as well as Components 2 and 3. The five domains of analysis are:

* **Law, policy and institutional framework**
* **Social norms, roles, responsibilities and time use**
* **Participation in decision-making processes**
* **Access to control over resources and services**
* **Safety and security**

## The project aims to improve the adaptive capacities and climate resilience of children, their communities and households by addressing gender inequalities that are exacerbated by climate change due to climate difference depending on many interacting factors including but not limited to, their age, gender, disability, ethnicity, race, economic status, diversity and social class.

All 103 countries identified that women, girls, men, and boys all are impacted differently by the socially assigned roles and responsibilities. Women and girls have a higher risk of being affected by climate change due to their specific social roles and power hierarchies within the family that are deeply ingrained in society. These affect the way marginalized groups are able to access resources and services, as well as their ability to participate (e.g., early morning earnings), increasing gender-based violence and child, early and forced marriage and unions, limited involvement in decision-making processes, and lack of control over resources and services.

Women and girls are more vulnerable to climate-induced diseases due to economic and social inequalities. In Cambodia, women and girls are particularly vulnerable during the rainy season when they have less energy during floods and droughts, while in Tonga, remote communities are particularly vulnerable. The project will implement a gender-sensitive approach that includes specific measures for women and girls with disabilities.

Safety and safety plans and climate resilience measures will be developed to address their specific vulnerabilities.

In South Sudan, patriarchal norms and conflict limit women’s participation in public life, particularly in climate and disaster risk management. Women are also more vulnerable than men during floods and droughts due to gender roles and girls. To mitigate this, the project aims for gender-balanced representation in governance structures, among others.

In Tonga, extreme weather events such as cyclones, expose women and girls to GBV in evacuation centers. To mitigate this, the project will implement a gender-sensitive approach that includes specific measures for women and girls with disabilities.

Climate change disproportionately impacts girls’ education. In Cambodia and South Sudan, traditional gender roles often prevent girls from accessing education over climate, poverty, or conflict. The project aims to address this by ensuring access to education in all schools. The BRACCE project will retrofit school infrastructure in Cambodia, ensuring access to gender-responsive water, sanitation and hygiene facilities for girls. In South Sudan, the project aims to ensure that communities close to floods and conflict, safety plans will help ensure girls’ and other marginalized groups’ continued access to food and shelter.

Gender inequality is associated with a 1% increase in CEFMIL globally. Globally almost 9 million girls face extreme risk of climate change due to gender inequalities. In South Sudan, the project aims to address this by ensuring that women are better able and exposed to other forms of GBV. Climate disasters and the long-term impacts of the climate crisis can lead to increased levels of violence against women and girls in conflict-affected areas. The project will ensure that GBV also increases during crises and families may decide to remove girls from school or have them with many to keep their children alive.

Finally, the exclusion of marginalized groups—including people with disabilities, ethnic minorities, and LGBTIQ+—is a major barrier to climate change adaptation in South Sudan. The project aims to address this by ensuring that women and girls with disabilities in Cambodia and indigenous groups struggle to access education and climate services. To address these challenges, the project will ensure that all schools are accessible for children with disabilities, including braille, sign language, and multiple languages, enabling meaningful participation for all.

For South Sudan, the project aims to ensure that women’s organizations representative engage in the BRACCE Coordination Mechanism and ensure this is reflected in the national gender policy. The project will also ensure that women’s organizations are included in the development and implementation of the Climate Change and Education Declaration Roadmap.

The project has established a gender equality and social inclusion (GEIS) steering committee, which monitors GEIS indicators and have budget allocated to GEIS activities to ensure equitable outcomes for all stakeholders.

## Save the Children Australia (SCA), as the Accredited Entity (AE), will oversee financial management and procurement for the BRACE project, ensuring full compliance with Green Climate Fund (GCF) Maturity standards.

### Financial Management Structure

The financial management structure for the BRACE project is informed by comprehensive financial management capacity assessments and due diligence conducted on all Executing Entities (EEs). The EE financial management capacity will be assessed through a Financial Management Assessment, reviewed financial systems, procedures, and capacity in each country, resulting in tailored financial arrangements.

- **Tonga:** Save the Children Tonga (SCTON) is a newly registered legal entity, and its financial and procurement management capacity is currently low. As such, SCA will provide training and capacity building to SCTON on GCF-specific roles, policies, and reporting requirements to enhance SCTON's capacity and reduce risk.
- **Cambodia:** Save the Children International Cambodia (SCICAMB) was assessed as having sufficient financial management capacity but requires further development in procurement. SCICAMB has a strong relationship with international donors; SCICAMB has procurement and financial systems well-aligned with GCF standards, ensuring compliance.

### South Sudan: Save the Children International South Sudan (SCISSD)

SCISSD operates in a more challenging environment. While SCISSD has a well-established financial management system, the due diligence assessment revealed that it is not fully aligned with GCF Maturity standards and does not have a robust procurement entity. To mitigate these risks, financial management will include phased disbursements, strict reporting requirements, and regular reviews.

### Financial Reporting and Audits

Financial reports will be submitted to SCA, detailing the use of funds and ensuring transparency and accountability in project implementation. An annual audit will be conducted annually with results shared with SCA and GCF to ensure transparency and accountability. The financial management structure will include a robust internal control system, including regular reviews used to achieve project goals.

### Strategic Procurement

SCA will implement a strategic procurement approach across all countries and address any risks related to fund management through regular reviews, including spot checks, and by implementing corrective actions as needed. This includes the establishment of EE procurement entities for projects that require specific procedures outlined in Annex 16: Procurement Plan.

The Procurement Plan provides a high-level overview of the procurement methods and processes that will be used throughout the project, ensuring transparency and accountability.

### Reporting and Monitoring

EEs will be responsible for executing procurement activities under the oversight of SCA. EE policies that align with SCA and meet GCF requirements. These entities will handle local procurement activities, including the selection of suppliers, while SCA will oversee larger procurements such as infrastructure improvements, WASH infrastructure upgrades, including borehole rehabilitation and well construction, and other large-scale projects. The EE will also be responsible for sustainable livelihoods, which receive close oversight from SCA to adhere to international best practices.

### Assessments

The assessments conducted by SCA will identify potential risks related to procurement activities in countries with more challenging environments, such as South Sudan. These assessments identified potential risks related to procurement activities and will inform the development of a tailored approach for each country. This approach to procurement will be implemented, with high-risk activities subject to additional scrutiny and prior review by SCA. This will ensure that all procurement activities align with rigorous competitive bidding processes and due diligence standards.

SCA will closely monitor the financial and procurement performance of the PIUs through financial reporting, which will provide detailed insights into fund utilization, procurement progress, and any delays in disbursement. SCA will work collaboratively with the PIUs and PSOs to resolve these promptly, ensuring that project implementation remains on track.

These will also comply with GCF environmental and social safeguards as outlined in the project's Environmental and Social Action Plans (ESAPs), detailed in Annex 6.

**Kingdom of Cambodia**

Ministry of Environment
N°...3195/065...MoE

To: The Green Climate Fund (GCF)
Phnom Penh, 22 April 2024.

Re: No objection letter in respect of the funding proposal titled "Building the Climate Resilience of Children and Communities through the Education Sector (BRACE)" submitted by Save the Children Australia.

Dear Madam, Sir,

We refer to the funding proposal titled Building the Climate Resilience of Children and Communities through the Education Sector (BRACE) in Cambodia submitted by Save the Children Australia and received on 14 March 2024.

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| Building the Climate Resilience of Children and Communities through the Education Sector (BRACE) is a multi-country, multi-year focused project with an integrated and systemic way to enhance climate resilience. By integrating climate change education into education systems, the project ensures educational continuity in times of climate shocks, establishing a transformative approach to tackling climate change within the education sector. | The sustainability of BRACE's interventions hinges on each country's governance stability and financial ability to scale recommended practices. |

| The initiative establishes knowledge-sharing platforms and learning spaces that enable cross-country collaboration and long-term | Logistical challenges may exist in remote or conflict-affected areas, especially in South Sudan and Tonga. |

# Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

## Project background

### Climate change poses an existential threat to education systems globally, with one billion children living in countries at extreme climate risk.

The BRACE project brings innovative solutions to address this challenge by integrating climate resilience into education systems. It aims to improve climate adaptation and mitigation strategies within school infrastructure systems and curriculum reform. The project’s unique approach addresses immediate climate impacts on schools while also building long-term adaptive capacity through policy reform and institutional strengthening.

### The BRACE project will target the least developed countries (LDCs), South Sudan and Cambodia, focusing on four key areas: improving education quality in response to rising temperatures, changing rainfall patterns, extreme events such as droughts, floods and high/extreme temperature events, which result in a range of education-related impacts.

The project will implement its activities through a multi-pronged approach:

(a) Implement at-scale pilots in target countries; implying climate-resilient school infrastructure projects, improved school safety systems and climate-focused curricula.
(b) Increased information and finance access: assist education ministries in accessing climate finance, planning adaptation and creating financing road maps;
(c) Technical assistance and capacity building: provide technical assistance to education sector stakeholders directly and support for the respective ministries of education; linking to the global education community and facilitating change to access knowledge and resources for climate-resilient sustainable development in the education sector, thus benefiting 8640/884 million people indirectly.

7. The environmental and social safeguards (ESG) category for this project is C.

## Component-by-component analysis

### Component 1: Building climate resilient school infrastructure and systems (total cost: USD 38.6 million; GCF cost: USD 30 million)

6. Component I of the project is designed to enhance the resilience of the education sector in Cambodia, South Sudan and Tonga in the face of current and future climate risks. It adopts a comprehensive, multi-level approach—engaging national, subnational, and school-level actors—to establish an enabling environment for climate change adaptation and sustainable transformation.

   (Output 1.1: Enabling systems and policies are in place and supported)
      * Activity 1.1.1: Facilitate preparation and endorsement of climate and education policies, plans and guidelines;
      * Activity 1.1.2: Share information on climate change within ministries of education and other relevant line ministries at the national and subnational levels;

      * Activity 1.1.4: Support children to participate in climate change and education policy dialogue.

      * Activity 1.2.1: Develop a national standard for climate-resilient school facilities;

It will also address climate impacts and risks in 850 schools. In Cambodia, the project targets at least 240 primary schools, which are particularly vulnerable to extreme weather events. In Cambodia it will directly reach 292,185 students (including 367,200 female) and indirectly benefit 6,031,400 people (1,962,128 female), amounting to 24 per cent of the population. In Laos, the project will directly reach an estimated 105,000 students (including an estimated 201,242 people, including schoolchildren and community members, with over half (52 per cent) being female). population, and indirectly benefit 36,762 people (18,381 female), equivalent to 33 per cent of the population.

### Under component 2, "Enhancing access to climate finance for education", the BRACE project aims to support the ministries of education in the three target countries by helping them access climate change-related financial resources. The project will focus on building capacity within the education sector. It will directly benefit 6210 ministry of education and government officials across these countries, as well as 35,742 students. The project will also support the implementation of the Green Schools Education System (GSES) project which will complement these efforts by sharing lessons learned from the BRACE project implementation.

### Under component 3, "Coordination and knowledge-sharing on climate and education", more than 1,000 education and climate actors and organizations in each of the countries will be benefited.

#### 2.3 Paradigm shift potential
Scale: N/A

The BRACE project represents a paradigm shift in school safety and climate education by integrating it into the Comprehensive School Safety Framework (CSSF). It focuses on improving school infrastructure, safety management systems, and enhancing climate change knowledge and skills among students. The project addresses barriers in governance, gender and social inclusion within the education sector through interventions that promote social attitude and behavior change. The project will also support the implementation of the Green Schools Education System (GSES) to create climate-resilient pathways aligned with sustainable development and educational strategies. It will focus on building capacity for school safety management systems, enhance school safety and resilience, and strengthen disaster preparedness. Additionally, the BRACE project will support the integration of climate change into national education policies to access climate finance and embed climate-related policies.

The BRACE project is designed for scalability and replicability, aligning with global policy objectives on climate change and education. It aims to expand the CSSF model to more schools, enhancing infrastructure, safety management systems and climate education in a sustainable manner. The project will also support the development of tools that facilitate adaptation in different contexts. Additionally, climate finance tools and resources developed through the project will further support its scalability and replicability across various regions.

The BRACE project emphasizes knowledge-sharing and learning through dedicated components 2 and 3, which are designed to build capacity within the education sector across three countries, and e-learning platforms for broader dissemination. Components 2 and 3 of the project will also focus on cross-country learning and capacity-building for ministries of education on climate finance, engaging in UNCCD processes like NAP development, and supporting the implementation of climate change adaptation programs, webinars and learning events. The BRACE project is positioned to test new interventions on a moderate scale and share findings widely.

#### 3.3 Sustainable development potential
Scale: N/A

The BRACE project aims to deliver gender-specific and social inclusion benefits. It directly benefits approximately 4.1 million people. It focuses on fostering gender equality and social inclusion (GES) within the education sector through interventions that promote social attitude and behavior change, as well as providing opportunities for advocacy related to gendered climate needs and actions. Key initiatives include providing gendered, age-appropriate, and disability-friendly WASH facilities, which can also


---
**Page 128**
---

serve the community during extreme weather. The project promotes menstrual hygiene and provides dignity kits for girls and teachers, enhancing community capacity. Improved WASH facilities near schools and communities aim to reduce the burden on women and girls related to water collection and sanitation. The project also supports school-based disaster risk reduction committees involving children, women and people with disabilities in remote areas. It incorporates climate change adaptation into existing disaster preparedness plans and emergency safety plans. Early warning systems will use language that is responsive to age, gender and disabilities.

28. The BRAC project also aims to achieve environmental co-benefits by implementing school gardens at the community level. These gardens are designed to improve local infrastructure and using photovoltaic (PV) panels for lighting and connectivity, and natural resource management activities such as tree planting and water harvesting/irrigation systems. Supporting school food gardens will also encourage the community to adopt practices that promote agroecology. Additionally, the project enhances environmental impact through improved WASH facilities, waste management and energy efficiency.

25. The BRAC project enhances social co-benefits by promoting locally led adaptation (LLA) at the school level. It engages children, teachers and community members to influence the LLA approach in schools. This is achieved through a series of activities including training, mentoring and implementation of KAPs. School-led initiatives and knowledge-sharing amplify LLA efforts, resulting in greater ownership by students and teachers. The project also organizes regular conferences. This group-up approach empowers children as key stakeholders in climate action and decision-making.

22. The project also contributes to the Sustainable Development Goals, particularly goals 4 (Quality Education), 5 (Gender Equality), 6 (Clean Water and Sanitation), 11 (Sustainable Cities and Communities) and 13 (Climate Action).

### Needs of the recipients

Scale: N/A

#### 3.4 Needs of the recipients

20. The climate change vulnerabilities affect school infrastructure, learning and health, and have broader environmental and social impacts, highlighting the need for climate-resilient investments. In Cambodia, South Sudan and Tonga, climate hazards such as droughts, heatwaves, floods, storms and sea-level rise are increasingly severe and more frequent, exacerbated by insufficient financial investment in education systems. To build a climate-informed society, the educational sector must be better prepared to adapt to these challenges and mitigate risks. This includes constructing resilient infrastructure, developing DRR measures, incorporating climate change into curricula, enhancing teachers' knowledge and capacity, and supporting community adaptation.

24. Cambodia, one of the poorest countries in South-East Asia, is particularly exposed to floods, droughts, heatwaves, cyclones and sea-level rise threats which damage school infrastructure, disrupt learning and increase risks for children. The country ranks 60th out of 189 countries on the Global Education Index (GEI) and 64th in UNESCO's Children’s Climate Risk Index, indicating a high risk to children from water scarcity, flooding and heatwaves. Cambodia is also ranked as one of the world's most climate-risk-prone countries globally, highlighting the urgent need for climate-resilient investments.

25. South Sudan, an LDC, faces significant vulnerability to climate shocks and has urgent adaptation needs. It ranks as the 8th most vulnerable country globally according to the Notre Dame Global Adaptation Index (ND-GAIN). The country's low-lying topography, poor drainage, South Sudan’s lowland and riverine topography make schools and communities highly susceptible to successive flooding due to severe rainfall, leading to school closure and severe infrastructure damage.


---
**Page 129**
---

3.5 Country ownership

The BRACE project aligns with the national climate change strategies and educational policies of Cambodia, South Sudan and Tonga to support the development of climate-resilient education systems. It is aligned with the National Disaster Risk Management Strategy (NDRS), the Cambodia Climate Change Strategic Plan and NAP-DRR, and Tonga's Strategic Development Plan and National Disaster Risk Management Plan, emphasizing climate adaptation and disaster risk management, emphasizing climate adaptation and resilience in education. This project contributes to the national strategies of South Sudan for inclusive education for GEEs children with disabilities, women and internally displaced people. It aligns with South Sudan’s National Gender Policy, Cambodia's Gender Mainstreaming Strategic Plan in Climate Change and Disaster Risk Reduction (CC-DRR), and Tonga’s National Gender Policy. The project also ensures gender perspectives into climate adaptation and DRR. By ensuring equal access to climate-resilient education for all children, including girls and children with disabilities across all genders and children with disabilities in these countries.

In each country, BRACE engages local governments, community organizations and school-based stakeholders to ensure that the project activities are continually relevant and sustainable. By fostering a sense of ownership among local stakeholders the project ensures that the project activities will be sustained beyond its implementation period. Stakeholder engagement has been crucial in the development and implementation of the BRACE project across all three countries. This includes collaboration with government agencies, local communities, non-governmental organizations (NGOs) and development partners to ensure that the project is aligned with national priorities for climate-resilient education and climate education in South Sudan, identifying key intervention areas in Cambodia, and addressing local climate challenges in Tonga. These participatory processes have shaped the project activities and ensured their relevance and sustainability throughout implementation, ensuring alignment with national and community priorities and allowing for adaptive management based on feedback.

3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

The BRACE project is expected to directly benefit 608,195 individuals and indirectly benefit over 2 million people through improved education infrastructure and system-wide resilience measures. It targets the most climate-vulnerable populations in the education sector, aiming to improve access to quality education for children with disabilities, girls, and youth, and utility of school facilities and embed climate risk awareness and adaptation capacity into the education sector. The South Sudan BRACE grant is expected to address the lack of private sector incentives in this space. The grant allows the pilot countries to implement transformational measures that would otherwise be difficult or impossible to achieve on their own, contributing to long-term learning and scalability in other climate-vulnerable education systems.


---
**Page 130**

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards

The project aims to increase the climate resilience of the education sector and its stakeholders in the target countries, both at the national and subnational levels by:

- Capacitating existing institutions,
- Enhancing access to finance and climate information,
- Promoting cross-border financing for climate-resilient infrastructure,
- Improving occupational health and safety risks and/or impacts related to retrofitting of school facilities including water supply systems,
- Providing training on safe construction practices,
- Increasing access to renewable energy for lighting and internet connectivity, improving shade through tree planting, and reducing the risk of flooding in schools by providing flood protection measures such as drinking water systems. The Af has developed an Environmental and Social Assessment and Remediation Plan (ESARP) for this project. The ESARP includes a list of environmental and social safeguard measures including ensuring workers will be provided with appropriate training on how to use tools, equipment and materials prior to commencement of works and all will be provided with appropriate personal protective equipment. A Gender Action Plan (GAP) has been developed in line with the GCF and Engagement Specialist to implement the ESARP will also be established.

The Af will engage with relevant government ministries, NGOs, civil society organizations, multilateral organizations, donors and the private sector in the development of the project to ensure it aligns with the needs and priorities of each country’s education sector. A stakeholder engagement plan is developed, and a grievance redress mechanism will also be put in place.

#### Sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH)

The revised GCF Environmental and Social Policy adopted by decision BIM-2012/II requires safeguarding from SEAH in GCF-financed projects. The Af has conducted an initial review of the project’s draft environmental proposal. As a standard practice, the Af conducts SEAH risk assessments for all its projects including country-specific assessment for this project. Key SEAH safeguarding measures have been put in place.


---
**Page 131**
---

been included in the SEAH grievance mechanism in alignment with the approach of the AE to child protection and a SEAH/gender-based violence continuum of care, while employing a survivor-centred approach. The rights, needs and wishes of survivors (or victims) will be the foremost consideration when addressing issues related to access to justice, safety, security, safety, non-discrimination and access to information key aspects of the SEAH protection and response including violence against children, and gender-based violence cases.

### GCF Gender Action Plan

The GCF Gender Action Plan (GAP) is a strategic framework for the target countries and project sites have populations considered Indigenous (Cambodia, South Sudan and Tonga). The project will ensure that all activities are culturally appropriate. This means that at the project site intervention level, school communities are homogeneous in language, culture and practices. Thus, projects will not impact on or exclude any particular group of Indigenous Peoples under their jurisdiction at the project site level. All physical activities will take place within existing defined school footprints where the school has documented a lease or secure usage rights with the landowner. The project will ensure that all lands and resources used by Indigenous Peoples or other minority groups will be subject to any policies and procedures in place for the project site, including environmental impact assessment processes for each project country into the project design and stakeholder engagement. The AE has given a commitment to engage with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy (GIP) at an early stage of the project development process. This approach ensures the careful involvement and consideration of Indigenous Peoples without excluding them from the project. All activities will be culturally appropriate, and Indigenous Peoples Policy are included in the environmental and social action plan and so no Indigenous Peoples plans or Indigenous Peoples planning framework was required. In line with its role as a facilitator, the AE has committed to ensuring that all relevant information is shared with the AE and executing entities (EE). In line with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy, the GIP guidance will be followed at this point will be available for assistance at any stage, including before a claim has been made.

### 4.2 Gender policy

The AE provided a gender assessment and action plan with the funding proposal and therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Updated Gender Policy. The gender association plan (GAP) is a strategic framework that will guide the implementation of gender updates in the target countries of Cambodia, South Sudan and Tonga. The AE also ensured that this process aligned with their gender mainstreaming guidelines, their gender equality and inclusion policies, and the GCF’s updated gender policy. The GAP was developed by the AE in consultation with plans. A detailed analysis was made of all five key assessment domains, namely: laws, policies and institutional frameworks; access to resources and services; decision-making processes; power over resources and services; and safety, dignity and well-being. The gaps identified in each of the domains were then used as a basis for developing gender action plans.

The AE submitted a comprehensive gender action plan (GAP) to mainstream gender and social inclusion throughout the BRIDGE project. Under component 1, “Building climate-resilient school infrastructure”, the GAP will ensure that all schools are built according to guidelines on the review and adoption of school design that is green, safe, healthy, inclusive and gender-responsive. The GAP will also ensure that the construction process follows a participatory approach; and inclusion of GIS considerations in climate-related school safety training and the differentiating between boys’ and girls’ needs and vulnerabilities during the construction phase. In addition to this, the infrastructure and WASH assessments will include age, gender and disability-specific needs and identify any differentiated risks. Under component 2, “Enhancing access to climate finance for schools”, the GAP will ensure that all project activities are designed in a way that promotes gender-related cross-sectoral coordination with key line ministries; and education-focused GES context for the project. The AE has committed to ensuring that the project will be aligned with “the focus on climate and education”, the project will, among other gender strategic activities, map, track

# Risks

## Overall project/programme assessment (medium risk)

The project aims to establish climate-resilient education systems in two LDCs, South Sudan and Cambodia, and one SIDS, Tonga, that are exposed to increasing average temperatures, more frequent and intense rainfall events, increased frequency of droughts and high/extreme temperature events. The GCF is requested to provide a full grant of USD 46.7 million. GEF, UNICEF and the World Bank are contributing or co-financing USD 8.6 million in the form of grants.

## Accredited entity/executing entity capacity to execute the current programme (medium risk)

The MoE has an extensive track record and global experience in implementing climate adaptation projects. This funding proposal requests the first GCF-funded project to focus on mitigating climate risks in the education sector.

## Project/programme-specific execution risks (medium risk)

### Political instability: political instability arising from conflicts, governance challenges, and related social unrest in South Sudan and Cambodia could deplete climate resilience initiatives.
- Mitigation measures can involve making the project more locally led or better targeted to specific communities.
- The project is designed to be resilient against such external-level unrest as the project still operates at the community level.

### Compliance risk (medium risk)

The project presents a moderate compliance risk. Procurement and financial management risks are associated with implementing entities, such as Save Children Tonga (SCTON), expose the project to risks of misallocation and non-compliance due to underdeveloped systems and insufficient capacity. The MoE will continue to provide guidance on how to align their processes with GCF-specific requirements, coupled with ongoing monitoring to ensure adherence to GCF policies.

### Multi-layered compliance framework in place to mitigate these risks
- Leveraging anti-money-laundering and countering the financing of terrorism (AML/CFT) policies.
- Enhanced due diligence measures for implementing entities and their financial mechanisms.
- Digital financial tracking tools will be used to enhance transparency, while periodic reviews will be conducted by the MoE and SCTON.
- Regular audits, spot checks, and ongoing partner assessments will ensure compliance with procurement and financial integrity standards. A whistle-blower mechanism is also in place to encourage reporting of any irregularities or non-compliance, including those related to corruption.

### Residual risk remains at a manageable level
Contingent on continuous oversight and adaptive compliance strategies. Based on the implemented mitigation measures, the residual

#### 4.5 Results monitoring and reporting

10. This is an adaptation project that will contribute to 3 of the 4 GCF adaptation results areas, such as AR1 (Most vulnerable people and communities), AR2A (Health, well-being, food and water security), and AR3A (Infrastructure and built environment). The project is expected to reach approximately 50% of the population in Cambodia and South Sudan; 70% of the population in Tonga; and 100% of the population in South Sudan.

## Legal assessment

The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 20 December 2019 (the "AMA"), and it became effective on 20 May 2020. The Accredited Entity's five-year accreditation term was lapsed on 19 May 2023 but was extended by Decision B37/18, paragraph 4(b), of the Board to 19 May 2025. The extension is subject to the end of the accreditation term or the date at which a revised accreditation framework is adopted by the Board.

The Accredited Entity has provided evidence that it has obtained all internal approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project.

The proposed project will be implemented in the Kingdom of Cambodia, the Republic of South Sudan, and the Kingdom of Tonga. In the case of the Kingdom of Cambodia and the Republic of South Sudan, GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in these countries, which risks need to be further assessed. Moreover, the ability of GCF to undertake redress activities for project participants may also be limited by the existence of privileges and immunities for relevant GCF personnel.

To address the matters raised in this section, it is recommended that any approval by the Board be made subject to the following conditions:

(i) Submission of a revised project document as a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval; and

(ii) Completion of the legal diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP274

## Proposal name:
Building the Climate Resilience of Children and Communities through the Education Sector (BRACE)

### Accredited entity:
Save the Children Australia

### Countries:
Cambodia, South Sudan, Tonga

### Project size:
Small

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Overview
The submission of this funding proposal for the multi-country "Building the Climate Resilience of Children and Communities through the Education Sector (BRACE)" project is under the framework of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) to support the implementation of the public-sector adaptation programme in environmental and social safeguards risk category C-1.

BRACE will focus on strengthening climate resilience in the education sector in three countries: Cambodia, South Sudan, and Tonga. These are developing countries of South Asia and Cambodia, and the small island developing State of Tonga.

The BRACE initiative was conceived during the Climate Finance dialogue in the margins of the twenty-first session of the Conference of the Parties (COP 21) to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (January 2016, appendix A). The dialogue was convened by Save the Children, with support from the Global Partnership for Education (GPE), and aimed at accelerating climate action by linking the education and climate agendas. Consequently GPE is providing technical assistance in the form of a grant to support the BRACE initiative through the education sector under the Climate Smart Education Systems Initiative (CSES®).

The three countries participating in the BRACE project are exposed to increasing temperatures, more frequent and intense rainfall events leading to increased flooding, droughts, floods, and high extreme temperature events, which result in a range of impacts on the environment such as loss of water bodies, water salination and hygiene (WASH) facilities, and school materials are damaged or destroyed by extreme weather events or simply eroded by harsher climate conditions.

1. The assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP) is based on the funding proposal received on 16 April 2015 (updated with minor modifications on 12 May 2015) and is informed by a set of written questions and responses provided to the ITAP in June 2015.

2. GCF refers to the United Nations Climate Change Conference that took place in Durban, United Arab Emirates from 3-8 December 2011.

3. The Global Partnership for Education (GPE) is a multi-stakeholder partnership hosted by the World Bank, supports governments and civil society organizations in developing countries to improve access to quality education for all children, especially girls, with a focus on early childhood development, primary education, and secondary education.

4. The United Nations Educational, Scientific and Cultural Organization and Save the Children Australia are the implementing partners of BRACE.

5. As a landlocked country, South Sudan is not directly affected by sea level rise.


---
**Page 136**
---

5. School disruptions (from climate and non-climate drivers) can affect children’s attendance and learning outcomes, increase the likelihood of children dropping out of schools, and affect physical safety and psychosocial well-being. Girls are likely to face higher risks of persistent negative impacts on their education than boys.
6. The funding proposal identifies common barriers to climate change adaptation in the education sector in all three countries to include limited institutional capacities and weak inter-sector coordination. It proposes a multi-country project that will address these barriers by building technical expertise to support timely action, and financial constraints.

7. The funding proposal aims to integrate climate resilience into education systems, by adopting a long-term approach to build capacity for adaptation through building long-term adaptive capacity through policy reform and institutional strengthening. The project will be delivered in three phases:

(a) Component 1: Building resilient-school infrastructure and systems will support enabling climate-resilient education policies, support RSD schools to become safer and more resilient, and integrate climate change into school-based disaster-focused curricula in national education systems.

(b) Component 2: Climate risk-informed financing for education will assist the education sector to access finance for climate action, create finance road maps, and support climate risk-informed decision-making.

(c) Component 3: Building climate-resilient education platforms will build climate-education-to-evidence platforms and facilitate peer learning and South-South collaboration between the three participating countries.

8. The total project cost is USD 72,940,561 with an initial GCF grant funds of USD 40,751,264 with a funding of USD 32,189,297, with an implementation period of 5 years and an overall duration of 7.5 years. The project will be implemented through the efforts undertaken by BRACE, specifically for Components 2 and 3, thereby helping to sustain, scale and replicate BRACE approaches for other countries.

9. The Climate Action Alliance (CAA) is a coalition of six international non-governmental organizations registered in Australia, SCA will act as the executing entity (EE) for Component 1. The CAA consists of: Save the Children Australia, CARE International, registered in Cambodia as an international non-governmental organization; and Royal Government of Cambodia, acting through the Ministry of Education, Youth and Sport (Cambodia). BRACE is a coalition of four international non-governmental organizations (South Sudan), and Save the Children Tonga Trust, registered in Tonga as an international non-governmental organization.

10. There are separate governance and management arrangements for each of the three countries, including national-level project steering committees with multi-stakeholder participation. The CAA will be responsible to the EE and will be overseen by the respective Save the Children entities in each of the three countries.


### 1.2 Impact potential

#### Scale: Medium to High

Overview: BRACE proposes to tackle challenges of climate-related increases in children dropping out of school, absenteeism of children and teachers from school, learning loss and impacts on psychosocial well-being related impacts, and the physical impacts climate hazards have on school facilities.

13. Country selection: The AE clarified to the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP), that BRACE drew from CSDP’s selection of 15 countries that was based on climate risk


---
**Page 137**
---

and vulnerability using three climate risk indices and the Human Development Index. BRACE further identified the three participating countries from across different geographies and contexts based on government willingness, ability to participate, and the status of the country's GCF project cycle. The AE has also considered alignment with other development priorities within the education sector across different contexts. The AE intends that the BRACE approach could be replicated in other contexts.

13. The ITAP sought clarification on how conflict and fragility informed country selection and project implementation. The AE explained that a Fragility Context Analysis for 2025 has been prepared for each of the three countries, which identifies key drivers of fragility such as conflict, humanitarian needs, and economic factors that contribute to the country's fragility. The AE also noted that the BRACE approach is not prescriptive on how BRACE will apply a conflict sensitivity approach throughout the project cycle.

14. Selection of schools: Target areas in each participating country were selected based on climate-related vulnerabilities, risk exposure, and risk vulnerability. This was done through stakeholder consultations. This includes three provinces in Cambodia, six counties in Kenya states in South Sudan, and two districts in Tanzania.

15. The selection of schools is based on eligibility criteria and the final list will be verified with the respective governments and with other development partners to ensure there is no overlap between the schools selected for BRACE and those already receiving climate adjustment as the school selection across the three countries will be confirmed during the inception phase (annex 2). The AE has also considered alignment with other development priorities, including education. All projects are subject to a feasibility study (annex 2), which is comprehensive and includes: climate risks vulnerability and exposure to multiple climate-related hazards; geographic location; poor/unstable infrastructure conditions; high poverty levels; low access to basic services; lack of access to clean water in communities; and high educational needs. BRACE has also considered alignment with established development priorities, including education, health, and social protection that are safe and sustainable, supporting equity and inclusivity by targeting schools serving disadvantaged or marginalized groups.
