### Beneficiaries:
The beneficiary calculations presented in annexes 17a and 17b are very detailed, drawn on available county school census data, and explain the underlying assumptions. The calculation is based on a sample of schools that were visited by school management committees, education officials and parents and community members, while individual schools were selected to represent the diversity of schools in each country and communities that could broadly benefit from early warning systems and climate information systems.

The total number of people reached under the three components (which totals 607,988 people) compared to the annexes 17a and 17b is 342,110.

### The funding proposal sets out how the 575 targeted schools will be supported through output areas under Component 1. Overall, 77 schools will benefit from climate-resilient infrastructure (output area 1.1), while 89 schools will benefit from school water and sanitation systems (output area 1.2). 406 schools will benefit from school safety and educational continuity management (output area 1.3), and 580 schools will be targeted for community-based disaster risk reduction activities (output area 1.4).

Table 1 provides a high-level additional indication of beneficiaries across components and outputs of the BRACE project, for direct and indirect beneficiaries in all three countries.


---
**Page 139**
---

The benefits include increased safety of children, educational personnel and caregivers in and around school to climate-related impacts (outputs 1.2, 1.3 and 1.4); school personnel, government and community at large are better prepared to tackle climate change vulnerability due to improved capacity building (output 1.5); enhanced access to education for children with disabilities (output 1.3), enhanced integration of actions targeting the education sector in national and subnational adaptation plans (output 2.1) and strategies, policies and programs that build climate-resilient education systems (output 2.2); and enhanced access to and exchange of knowledge on climate change impacts in the education sector (outputs 3.1 and 3.2).

The TAP asked for clarification regarding the potential indirect climate-related impacts and learning loss, and baselines used by the BRACE project. While acknowledging data limitations, the AE highlighted that the TAP is aware of the “BRACE Project: Climate-Related School Disruptions in 2024” which highlights the severe impact of climate-related events on children’s education. The AE explained that in Cambodia, 358,799 students were affected by climate-related school disruptions in 2024; in Indonesia, 16,000 students were affected; and in South Sudan, 2,200,000 students were affected by climate-related disruptions in 2024 with hurricanes, floods, landslides, droughts and heatwaves.

The AE has acknowledged that non-climate drivers of vulnerability also impact the education sector, and notes examples of how climate drivers (bodges, droughts, high temperatures) can exacerbate existing vulnerabilities. The AE clarified that the project is of higher magnitude.” The AE has clarified that the BRACE project will solely focus on demonstrating how the climate-related school disruptions are linked to learning loss.

The AE has confirmed to the TAP that the scope of BRACE will not extend to reaching out-of-school children or incentivizing them to join schools as these strategies fall within the core mandate of other projects. The AE explained that the project is focused and intended to contribute to building a climate-resilient education sector and will be complemented by national efforts in this area. The TAP asked about the potential for BRACE to reach all children that could reach all schoolchildren in the participating countries. The TAP understands that the achievement of this goal depends on the context, but as long as relevant ministries are built, climate change adaptation is integrated into education plans, policies and budgets.

The TAP also recommends that the project put in place adequate safeguards to ensure that, under Component 1, the focus of school infrastructure upgrades remains on achieving positive learning outcomes. The AE explained that there will be no spillover effects such as hidden fees or costs that are passed on to families or communities.

Based on the above, the TAP assesses the impact potential as medium to high.


### Paradigm shift potential

Scale: Medium

BRACE is designed on the premise that education is a cornerstone of building human capital and it is therefore a vital aspect of building the capacities of individuals, communities and societies in order to adapt to climate change. The project is based on the theory of change which embeds in the theory of change which presents the logic of how the three components come together to achieve the overall goal. The TAP has identified several potential specific barriers associated with governance, social, informational, financial, technological, institutional and gender and social inclusion.

The TAP concludes that there is a paradigm shift by building climate-resilient school infrastructure and systems to enable the education sector to be more resilient to the impacts of


---
**Page 140**
---

climate change at national and subnational levels in the three targeted countries. This is to be delivered through four outputs on integrating climate change into education sector policies and plans (output 1.1); safer and greener school facilities through retrofitting schools and strengthening building codes and standards (output 1.2); improved school infrastructure (output 1.2); school safety and educational continuity management systems, and strengthened disaster risk reduction measures in the education sector (output 1.3); and embedding climate change resilience in teaching and learning, and strengthening teachers’ capacity to lead similar climate initiatives in their schools and communities (output 1.4).

The AIF has clarified that the project will apply the established Comprehensive School Health Framework (CSHF) as a guiding principle for school design and construction. The CSHF is based on specific local contexts and evidenced climate needs to ensure that standards are maintained for these infrastructure-related works. Design will also take into account aspects of building materials, energy efficiency, water conservation, waste management, and appropriate use of construction and building regulations. Therefore, while the project will standardize the analysis and design process, it is not intended to be prescriptive.

Component 2 proposes to improve access of education ministries to climate finance in the three participating countries to increase the resilience of the education sector to climate change. This will include a review of existing policies and practices for climate risk assessment and influencing (output 2.1), and climate finance action in the education sector (output 2.2).

Component 3 proposes to establish an education sector platform for enhanced climate action in the education sector. This will include establishment of a climate resilient education co-investment platform (output 3.1) and peer learning and exchange (output 3.2).


Contextual factors. In the view of the TAP, the three assumptions in the theory of change are not equally valid across all three countries. The different country contexts will be critical to the achievement of the intended outcomes and in enacting a paradigm shift in each of the three participating countries. For instance, the assumption in the proposed theory of change that climate change will lead to increased school dropouts among children may drop out to support families facing climate-driven economic shocks or where other responsibilities are required (as initially listed in the proposal) may be significant reasons behind student or teacher absenteeism.

The extent to which key education stakeholders can effectively adopt and implement climate-resilient education policies, plans, and practices will depend on a number of contextual factors. These include country-specific institutional capacity (e.g., fragility and conflict, political stability, economic shocks, willingness of stakeholders to participate in the project), as well as the availability of resources (e.g., financial resources available to meet the enormous needs of the sector). One or three participating countries fall under fragile states, which may present challenges for the education sector. The extent to which project interventions achieve a paradigm shift partially if instability or conflict affect project progress or sustainability.


Schools. Across all countries and grades reached, the types of activities across schools in each country, and the proportion of direct and indirect beneficiaries varies significantly by country. For example, 100 percent of primary school students are expected to benefit from BRACE’s potential impact; extent would approach approximately 1 per cent for Cambodia, 7 per cent for South Sudan, and 31 per cent for Tonga (information captured in table 1 above). While this may limit the scale of the project, it is important to note that the countries are considered pilot sites that BRACE will lay the groundwork for future scaling efforts.


---
**Page 141**
---

The ITAP considers that the planned development of a longer-term financing strategy, proposed under Component 2, will be critical to help unlock funding for sustaining and scaling efforts under this project. The ITAP welcomes the project's early consideration of developing climate finance strategies (CFS) in line with GCF guidance (Annex V). It also builds on support to education ministries to develop concept notes for access to GCF finance, consistent with GCF criteria.

Alignment with CSSES: The AE has clarified to the ITAP that the intent of both CSSES and BRACE is to show the value of this work and "the how" of integrating climate change into the education sector. The AE also noted that the project's focus on building capacity for scaling, with parallel financing from the GPE CSSES was valuable and could also help in replicating and scaling up the project’s approach. The AE has clarified to the ITAP that the project will help to create an enabling environment, achieve synergies and coordinated action with other donor-funded education initiatives, and enable replication more broadly across the other focus countries identified by the GPE CSSES.

Knowledge and learning: All three project components include activities to support knowledge sharing and learning. The project will build on existing efforts in dialogue with stakeholders, building wider knowledge, and deriving lessons from the Comprehensive School Safety Framework model across different contexts. The project will also leverage the CSSES to develop a set of guidance documents that will provide a comprehensive component of guidance and knowledge products on climate change and education, and the targeted dissemination of these products through existing networks in the three focus countries. The ITAP views this as a useful approach that can help to strengthen learning and support uptake across the GPE CSSES. The project will also contribute to the design and implementation of the BRACE programme in three diverse country contexts to inform future GCF climate education projects.

BRACE: The BRACE climate and education financing coordination platform, proposed under Component 3, could offer high potential for knowledge-sharing and learning, and climate action in the education sector. However, the ITAP notes that there are still some uncertainties around such coordination as not yet clearly defined, and the AE has clarified to the ITAP that the process of developing a BRACE platform will require engagement with key stakeholders, as well as the willingness of a suitable hosting organization to support the platform.

BRACE also proposes to bring together ministries of education and environment in external forums (including COP 20) for lesson-sharing. The proposal ensures these that meetings will be held at least once per year and will contribute to the implementation of the Common Agenda for Education and Climate Change launched at COP 28. The ITAP suggests that the project consider whether a BRACE-funded platform offers the most suitable avenue, or whether other mechanisms (e.g., GPE CSSES) might be more appropriate. If such an extended reach may be better placed to facilitate such a sector. The AE has clarified that the development of a BRACE platform is not a requirement for the project and that the proposed implementation in the three BRACE focus countries, is in response to a request from GCF.

Overall, the ITAP assesses the paradigm shift potential as medium.


### 1.4 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

BRACE is aligned with the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), specifically SDG 4: quality education for all; and SDG 5: achieve gender equality and empower all women and girls. It also contributes to SDG 6: ensure access to water and sanitation for all; SDG 11: sustainable cities and communities; and SDG 13: take urgent action to combat climate change and its impacts.


---
**Page 142**
---

### Go-Benefit 1: Gender equity and social inclusion.

The funding proposal indirectly targets 4,128,574 females during implementation, with the co-benefits to be mainly delivered through activities under Component 1, which focuses on promoting a clean and green environment in targeted schools. The project will also provide training for teachers and school staff on gender equality policies, standards, and the provision of additional support to girls and children with disabilities.

### Go-Benefit 2: Environmental.

The co-benefits will mainly be delivered through activities under Component 1, which is focused on promoting a clean and green environment in targeted schools. The project will reduce greenhouse gas emissions from school infrastructure through the use of sustainable materials, improvement in WASH facilities, and energy management systems. It will also install solar panels for lighting and connectivity.

### Go-Benefit 3: Increased locally-led adaptation (action).

The co-benefits will be delivered by engaging with a wide set of local stakeholders, including school children, residents living near schools, parents, teachers, and community leaders. The project will develop and implement school safety plans, child-led clubs, and the use of knowledge platforms and discussion forums to empower local communities. It will also provide training for teachers on climate change adaptation and can provide safe shelters for communities during extreme climate and weather events.

### In its written response to the TAP, the AE confirmed that IRACE aligns with the Principles for Disaster Risk Reduction (PDRR) and the 10 Strategic Objectives of the AE. These principles include: devolved decision-making at the school level through clubs; addressing structural inequalities in schools; promoting gender equality and social inclusion; enhancing community understanding of climate risk; ensuring transparency and accountability; and collaborative action. It also outlines indicators in the monitoring and evaluation framework that capture


---
**Page 143**
---

1. **Needs of the recipient** Scale: High

   All three participating countries have multiple developmental challenges that compound the obstacles faced by the education sector. Cambodia, a lower middle-income country, has limited access to financial resources and human capital. According to the Global Climate Risk Index (2021), Cambodia ranks as the 19th most vulnerable country in terms of climate change impacts. Tonga is considered an upper-middle income country with increasing rising rates of poverty and vulnerability among its most vulnerable groups, and it relies heavily on international assistance, foreign aid, and loans.

   According to the ND-GAIN (2021), South Sudan is the sixth most vulnerable country in the world to climate change. The country faces significant challenges related to climate change and environmental shocks, and most schools in South Sudan are in areas prone to flooding.

2. **Annex 2 of the funding proposal highlights the vulnerability of school children and other community members to climate-related adaptation gaps.** It also clearly sets out proposed adaptations to address these gaps. The following examples illustrate the vulnerability of schools in Cambodia (pages 79–84), South Sudan (page 175), and Tonga (page 207) respectively. These are representative of the challenges faced by schools across all three countries.

   *The education sector often remains underfunded by public sector resources due to economic challenges and other competing priorities. Limited access to financial resources, and limited human capital, further exacerbate this situation.*

3. **Consequently, climate-related education impacts are likely to women, contributing to further vulnerability in the context of climate change and environmental shocks.**

4. **The project is designed to be inclusive and follows Save The Children’s nine principles for meaningful and ethical children’s participation across its global programs: advocacy, and capacity building.** It aims to ensure that the highest proportions of students from vulnerable groups and where interventions are likely to reduce inequalities enhance access to safe, climate-resilient learning environments or emergency shelters.

5. **Overall, the ITAF assesses the needs of the recipient as high.**

6. **Country ownership Scale: Medium**

| | National Climate Change Adaptation Strategy and Disaster Risk Management 2018–2028 |
| | Aligns with the national education policy framework, to ensure that interventions are inclusive of all genders and children with disabilities. |

## Each of the three participating countries reference the education sector in some capacity either in their country national adaptation plans (NAPs) and/or nationally determined contributions (NDCs). The NDC for South Sudan (updated 2021) includes a focus on the education sector related to upgrading school curricula to include climate change, and for delivering climate change education. The NDC for Cambodia also refers to knowledge exchange and coordination across relevant ministries is intended to facilitate the implementation of the national gender mainstreaming strategy in the education sector. The NDC for South Sudan (updated 2021) does not include a specific focus on education, but identifies climate-change activities that can be integrated into the school curriculum, such as increasing the understanding of climate change broadly as well as upgrading school resources.


---
**Page 145**
---

infrastructures to be more resilient to climate change impacts.1 In Tonga, school safety and climate resilient education are a priority within the Tonga Education Policy Framework. The second Joint National Action Plan includes a focus on incorporating climate resilience topics into school curricula.

A lingering concern for the ITAP is that the integration of climate into the education sector may be relatively nascent, and that other urgent issues may take priority particularly in a resource-constrained context.

The AE has gone on to clarify its role in the ITAP that all three countries include climate change in their education sector plans, signaling the importance of addressing this issue in the education sector. The AE has also clarified that the project will build upon existing national CSEIS; the BRACE project proposes to address this gap by helping to guide integration of climate change into the education sector through a series of workshops and meetings as part of the NAP processes, through support for the development and implementation of climate finance multi-stakeholder platforms, and through engagement with the education ministry and with other line ministries.

Partnerships and stakeholder engagements: The AE has clarified to the ITAP that all activities will be implemented in partnership with Ministries of Education through CSEIS workshops, as well as during consultation meetings with other government agencies and partners during the design of the project.

The proposal has rightly identified that the successful implementation of BRACE depends on strong partnerships with government ministries and local authorities in each country. The AE has provided a detailed list of key stakeholders to be engaged within the funding proposal for instance, in New South Wales, the Ministry of Education and Instruction will provide strategic oversight and ensure alignment with national priorities; in Cambodia, the Ministry of Education, Youth and Sports, the National Committee on Climate Change and Disaster Management will provide strategic oversight and ensure alignment with national priorities; in Cambodia, the Ministry of Environment, the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries, and in Tonga, the Ministry of Education and Training and the Ministry of Meteorology, Energy, Information, Disaster Management, Environment, Climate Change and Communications will provide strategic oversight and ensure alignment with national priorities. However, in view of the ITAP, the proposal lacks detail on the actual extent of their involvement in the project design phase, and ITAP development is recommended to be developed during project inception.

The ITAP considers the Ministries of Finance as important stakeholders in any process to secure additional funding for climate change adaptation (CSEIS). The AE has clarified to the ITAP that Ministries of Finance were engaged in the critical foundational work of CSEIS and have signed off on the NAPs. The AE has also provided a detailed list of key stakeholders, including suggestions to further incorporate and integrate Ministries of Finance into project activities, as well as their involvement during the design phase.

Alignment with complementary projects and initiatives for each participating country has been documented in the funding proposal package, showing how BRACE activities could potentially be aligned with other national priorities.

## Efficiency and effectiveness

### Scale: Medium

The programme will be implemented over a period of 5 years, with a project lifespan of 10 years. The total project funding of USD 837,234 comprises a request for GCF grant funds of USD 690,000 (82.5%), the European Union (EU) contribution of USD 100,000 (12%) and the Global Environment Facility (GEF) contribution of USD 4,960,076%, the World Bank (USD 500,000) and the United Nations Children’s Fund (UNICEF) contribution.

The funding proposal argues that this low co-financing rate of 1.83:1 is justified given the public nature of these interventions in a context where public finance budgets in all three countries are under pressure. The project will be implemented by BRAC, with three participating countries and the three component areas set out in table below.

| Project component | Total component budget (USD millions) | GCF grant contribution (USD millions) | GCF funds as a proportion of total component budget |
|---|---|---|---|
| Component 1: Building climate-resilient school infrastructure and systems | 34,068.92 | 34,068.92 | 100% |
| Component 2: Scaling up climate finance for education | 672,903 | 672,903 | 1.7% |
| Component 3: Communication and knowledge-sharing on climate and education | 2,583,105 | 2,583,105 | 5.9% |
| Project management costs | 2,516,023 | 1,904,579 | 76.5% |
| Total: | **6,621,000** | **5,896,423** | 89.4% |

Note: The totals represent figures that have been rounded up by the GCF.

### Additionality of GCF resources

BRAC brings additioality by equipping under-funded education systems in the three countries for climate change adaptation. As education is considered a public good, this also opens the potential for BRAC to offer economic and social benefits.


---
**Page 147**
---

development benefits in the longer term. Table 1 of the economic and financial analysis (annex 3a) emphasizes the scarcity of public funding for climate adaptation and for the education sector in all three countries, and notes that financing for climate action is heavily reliant on external resources. The analysis also highlights that the countries face significant capacity constraints, and current resources are unlikely to be able to tackle the climate vulnerabilities faced by the education sector.

75. Approximately 15 per cent of GCF resources will finance climate-proofing of schools as a sub-activity under Component 1. The improvements of school facilities under output 1.2 could include: installation of solar panels and/or photovoltaic systems to reduce electricity consumption; installation of solar water heating systems; installation of rainwater harvesting systems; installation of solar-powered water pumps; installation of solar-powered lighting systems; installation of solar-powered ventilation systems; installation of solar-powered air conditioning systems; installation of solar-powered water heaters; installation of solar-powered water purifiers; and installation of solar-powered water filters. The improvements under output 1.3 could include: installation of solar panels and/or photovoltaic systems to improve access to renewable energy, lighting and internet connectivity (Component 2); installation of solar-powered water pumps for irrigation purposes; installation of solar-powered water heaters; installation of solar-powered ventilation systems; installation of solar-powered air conditioning systems; installation of solar-powered water purifiers; and installation of solar-powered water filters.

76. As other development partners and national education programmes finance school infrastructure improvements, the GCF will provide technical assistance to ensure alignment with climate change adaptation priorities. The GCF will also provide funding clarification from the AEC regarding the additionality of GCF resources. Specifically in the context of climate change adaptation, the GCF will support the development of a common framework and create synergies with the work of other donors to maximize the overall impact and effectiveness of BRACI interventions. The AEC has clarified that all BRACI activities will be climate-resilient by design. The GCF will provide technical assistance for donor engagement, as opposed to meeting general educational development needs. The IFAP considers that the GCF should not be expected to finance additional resources during implementation.

77. An ex-ante assessment of economic benefits was conducted for each participating country under the Regional Concentration Pathway (RCP) scenarios RCP 4.5 and RCP 8.5. The economic and financial analysis, based on a 15-year horizon and utilizing a conservative discount rate of 3 per cent, indicates that the project is likely to be cost-effective.

78. Sustainability and exit strategy: The overall exit strategy demonstrates a commitment to sustainability by enabling a transition to local ownership, integrating climate adaptation into school management practices, and ensuring that lessons learned are integrated into national systems. Support to education ministries to develop a road map to access climate finance resources will be provided through the development of a National Climate Change Strategy developed under Component 3, could provide an ongoing forum for stakeholder coordination. In the context of the exit strategy, it is recognized that there may not be sufficient specific exit strategies for each of the three countries with key milestones; hence, there is a risk that project activities will not be sustainable in the long term. The IFAP recommends that the project take on wider shifts and any emerging risks should also be built into the exit strategies.

79. Operations and maintenance (O&M): The project envisages equipping local stakeholders to manage and maintain the installed systems, including solar panels. O&M is envisaged to be undertaken through community-led maintenance committees (South Sudan), by training teachers in the use of renewable energy technologies (Uganda), and by providing basic training to school staff on problem solving and maintenance. The ITAP recommends that the project develops a fully costed O&M plan that is agreed with local stakeholders, including the communities.

80. Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E): The initial M&E plan is clearly set out [annex 13], with an emphasis on the importance of data collection during the inception phase in collaboration with project M&E staff at each of the three countries and in compliance with the Save Children Monitoring, Evaluation, Accountability and Learning Framework. The IFAP recommends that a robust monitoring framework will be important for assessing project impact across all three countries. The M&E plan will need to take into account risks related to data availability and quality particularly as this spans national

### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

#### If approved by the Board, this would be the first GCF climate project in the education sector. The project has a strong focus on climate change adaptation and resilience building area; it offers an opportunity to embed climate adaptation into national education systems and to build the adaptive capacity and climate resilience of communities in the three participating countries that are particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts. The alignment with the GCF SRES initiative across three different contexts, the alignment with the GPE CSSE initiative is a positive feature of this project. It also provides an opportunity for the Board to take action on climate risks and to elevate the urgency of addressing climate risks in the education sector at a global level.

The ITAP also proposes recommendations to the AE as follows:

- For Part 1, it recommends that the proposed infrastructure upgrades achieve positive climate impact for children and that there are no negative spillover effects (see paragraph 27 above).
- Develop an operational sustainability plan that extends beyond a reliance on GCF funding and seeks wider sources of funding (see paragraph 37 above).
- Develop a fully costed M&E plan that is agreed with local stakeholders so that these are not merely technical exercises.
- Detail the overall M&E approach, with proper baselines, so that it is fit-for-purpose across contexts, can be used for adaptive management during implementation, demonstrates alignment with GCF SRES initiative (see paragraph 78 above), and can track the additionality of GCF resources (see paragraph 74 above).
- The ITAP recommends that the Board approve this funding proposal.

# Introduction

1) The Building the Climate Resilience of Children and Communities through the Education Sector (BRACE) project aims to improve the adaptive capacities and climate resilience of children, their communities and schools, recognising that children and their communities are impacted by climate change differently depending on many intersecting factors including age, gender, ethnicity, race, socio-economic status, refugee status and gender identity.

2) The overarching objective of this project is to tackle the impacts of climate change on the education sector in three very different countries with diverse climate hazards and social contexts. By demonstrating that a multi-sector approach can be effective across these very different countries, BRACE will be able to demonstrate an adaptable model that can be replicated elsewhere. This will help children access education, improve their ability to access climate finance, and support the global education community to better coordinate responses to climate change.

3) If this intervention does not happen, the Business as Usual will result in increased climate vulnerability, school closures, children dropping out of school, gender inequity, and other negative impacts on children’s lives.

4) BRACE will be achieved via implementation of activities under three components, each with outputs and activities leading to Outcome 3 – Education and climate stakeholders are connected and working together for enhanced climate action in the education sector.

5) For further information about the project, please see Appendix A.

6) This analysis considers the ways in which gender and social norms interact with adaptive capacity and resilience. It is based on the Gender Equality and Social Inclusion (GESI) Assessment Tool. The assessment sets out key data and analysis across five domains and includes recommendations for action. The GESI Action Plan will be developed as part of this approach to embed gender equality and social inclusion (GESI) across project activities and operations. The Gender Equality and Social Inclusion Action Plan highlights GESI commitments, actions and the proposed GESI monitoring framework for the three countries as well as Components 2 and 3.

7) The five domains are:

* Domain 1: Laws, Policies and Institutions
* Domain 2: Workload, Roles, Responsibilities and Time Use
* Domain 3: Participation, Leadership and Decision-Making
* Domain 4: Access to Resources and Services
* Domain 5: Safety, Dignity and Well-Being

8) The Gender Equality and Social Inclusion Action Plan is a strategic framework designed to ensure that gender equality and social inclusion are mainstreamed. Gender and social inclusion are systematically integrated into the BRACE project. Its primary goal is to address gender disparities in education and climate change adaptation. The GESI Action Plan will be refined at the project’s onset and throughout its implementation, provides a foundation for action.

9) Based on the analysis of the five domains, the GESI Action Plan will identify and mitigate any adverse gender impacts or risks. Additionally, it will implement positive actions to promote gender equality and empower children in all their diversity through the

## Methodology

9) The gender assessment and action plan were prepared by the feasibility teams located in Dar es Salaam, Sudan and Tonga as well as GESI specialists in the countries and regions. It was developed in line with the GCF Gender Policy, Gender Mainstreaming Guidelines, the GCF Gender Action Plan, the GCF Gender Strategy, and Country-level GCF Gender Equality and Inclusion Action Plans. The teams used a combination of primary and secondary data collection methods as explained below.

10) The availability of data varied across countries given the different contexts. Where possible, data was collected across countries to show similarities and comparisons to facilitate learning from each other in the context of the GCF Gender Action Plan.

### 1) Primary data:

- Data were collected in South Sudan and Tonga.
    - **South Sudan:** Data were collected through ongoing assessments conducted by the Ministry of Education (MoE), UNICEF, and others. This involved direct engagement through key informant interviews with national, state and local Government officials, civil society organizations, women-led and gender-diverse groups, teachers, civil society organizations and the wider community including girls and women. For ongoing education programs such as the South Sudan Education Response Plan (SSERP), the Global Partnership for Education (GPE) and the Education Cannot Wait (ECW) Multi-Year Resilience Programme rapid gender and inclusive analysis was conducted in 2021, 2022 and 2023. Data were collected between October 2023 June and 2024. These assessments were carried out through key informant interviews with national government officials, civil society organizations, women-led and gender-diverse groups, youth-led organizations, teachers, parents, students, community members, and academics. Standard questions were used across all countries. Please see Appendices B for a consultation overview for South Sudan.
    - **Tonga:** Data were collected through ongoing assessments conducted using key informant interviews with national government officials, civil society organizations, women-led and gender-diverse groups, youth-led organizations, teachers, parents, students, community members, and academics. This includes senior officials from Tonga Ministry of Meteorology, Energy, Information, Disaster Management, Environment, Agriculture, Water Resources, Telecommunications, Ministry of Internal Affairs, Ministry of Education, regional and national academic institutions.

### 2) Secondary data:

- Across the three countries and at global level for Components 2 and 3. secondary data were collected from a variety of sources, including government reports, UNICEF country office reports, World Bank Country Assistance Reports (CARs), and evaluations and are cited throughout the assessment. This includes the inception documents of four countries under the Global Partnership for Education (GPE) funded Climate Resilient Gender System Initiative (CRGESI) which is linked to Component 2.

### 3) Gender Action Plan:

- The GAP was developed aligned directly with the overall project logframe. The action plan does not have a direct link to the project objective. There are no activities for output 1.1 for Tonga as they are not implementing output 1 in the overall project.
- The budget was calculated by creating an excel sheet that detailed 1) all the staff times for those working on GESI and child protection, and 2) activity costs across countries for all three components. We then estimated what percentage of the


---
**Page 157**
---

budget would be needed to conduct the GESI activities highlighted in the workplan per country per activity.

* For example, under activity 1.3.1 on capacity building we added up the staff times of those budgeted to be working on GESI (Gender Adviser, GESI Coordinator, and Gender focal point) for this activity which totalled = $18,933. Tonga doesn't have activities under 1.3.1. The total costs for the activity would be $205,467. Therefore, the amount of budget to allocate towards GESI based on the CAP is = $41,328. Dividing this with the staff times = 26040.

* The staff time as a percentage of activities assumed to go to GESI is calculated per activity and varies across them.

# Global Findings on Gender Equality and Social Inclusion and Climate Change

14) The social impacts of climate change are not distributed equally but depend on intersecting factors such as age, sex, race, ethnic group, sexual orientation, gender identity, gender expression and sex characteristics (SOGIESC) status etc. Children are shown to be at higher risk than adults of environmental degradation, food insecurity, water scarcity, conflict, displacement, and poor hygiene.

15) Women, girls, and particularly adolescent girls are more likely to live in poverty, have less access to knowledge and education, are more likely to be malnourished, and are disproportionately affected by climate change impacts such as water and other natural resources. These responsibilities are likely to increase when men are forced to migrate.

16) Girls face the double burden of gender- and age-based discrimination. Girls are also more vulnerable than boys to climate-related disasters because of their lower body mass and men's and discriminatory gender norms they are less likely to be involved in policies, decision-making processes, and disaster risk reduction (DRR). These can increase sexual violence, GBV (gender-based violence), SGBV (domestic abuse and intimate partner violence) and child marriage. Studies have highlighted a spike in gender-based violence (GBV) and sexual assault during climate-related disasters.

17) Girls must therefore adapt and respond to the impacts of climate change in addition to their existing vulnerabilities, including early and forced marriage and unions (CEFMU), lack of access to education, and adolescent pregnancy. These are exacerbated by poverty, conflict or displacement, and gender-based violence against girls living in poverty, conflict or displaced settings, or girls from ethnic minorities living in conflict zones.

18) Gender and social discriminatory norms and stereotypes can dictate the receptivity of policies and programmes to address climate change. This is particularly true for people with diverse SOGIESC, people with disabilities, and those living in poverty, conflict or displacement. This can result in policies and programmes that do not address the specific vulnerabilities of these groups and can exacerbate inequality.

## Education

Climate shocks can disrupt schooling and education, but they are also a protective factor against CEST. Evidence suggests that if trends continue, by 2035 climate change could subtract 12.5 million girls each year not completing their education due to the impacts of climate change - for every additional year of schooling for girls on average, a county's Notre Dame-Global Adaptation Index (ND-GAIN) score would be expected to increase by 3.2 points.

20) Countries that have focused on girls' equal access to education have suffered fewer losses from droughts and floods than those with lower levels of girl education.12

Child, early and forced marriage and unions (CEFMU)

21) Analysis of historical data shows that a 1% increase or decrease in rainfall is associated with a 1% increase in CEFMU globally.13 New analysis by Save the Children shows that CEFMU has increased from 50 million girls to 64 million girls and CEFMU every year and that around two-thirds of CEFMU happens in regions with higher-than-average levels of drought and food shortages, rates of CEFMU rose by 119% in 2022 compared to 2021.13

22) Gender-based violence (GBV) is a major risk factor for girls being married young, increasing the likelihood of a girl being married. These include being out-of-school, living in poverty, and experiencing climate-related disasters.

Climate Change Risk and Policies

23) The Children's Climate Risk Index (CCRI) indicates that almost every child is exposed to at least one environmental hazard and 50% are exposed to two or more environmental hazards and 2.2 billion children are exposed to at least two overlapping environmental issues. The same index reveals that children are less able to protect themselves from immediate danger and more vulnerable to long-term impacts of climate change than adults.

24) In terms of gender, according to the 2022 Equal Measures 2030 Sustainable Development Goals (SDG) Gender Index, only one country's SDG score makes a reference to three goals that is furthest behind on gender equality issues.16 In 2016, it was a total of 184 of 190 nationally determined contributions (NDCs) submitted under the Paris Agreement by 53 countries that included a gender reference were non-Annex I [developing] countries. Seven countries had no gender references in their NDCs at all and four did not mention gender.17 Analysis in 2019, highlights that only one country's NDC makes a reference to girls' education. There are only two additional countries refer to girls explicitly, a reflection of the omission of the gendered climate impacts on children and education in climate strategies.18

# Country Level Findings on Gender Equality and Social Inclusion

## Key Findings

### Cambodia:

25) Climate change disproportionately affects persons who are poor, women, children, and people with disabilities. Female employment in agriculture as share of total female employment is higher than male employment in agriculture due to the fact that they perform manual labour. Because they depend on livelihoods that are sensitive to climate change, they are more vulnerable to its effects.

26) Women's vulnerability is further exacerbated by the socially assigned discriminatory gender roles and responsibilities at the household level, which often prevent women from accessing resources and opportunities within the family that are deeply ingrained in society.

27) In many cases, women have limited access to land, credit, education, and training in areas related to environmental preservation and conservation. Women's ongoing poverty, restricted access to reproductive and sexual health services, lack of decision-making power, and limited educational possibilities make them more sensitive to climate change than men. Women with disabilities are frequently exposed to and vulnerable to climate change and disaster-induced risks.

28) In addition to being less able to adapt to changes in climatic conditions due to their lower socioeconomic status, women face significant challenges when it comes to decision-making processes, women and girls living in poverty, indigenous women, women with disabilities, and women who are victims of violence. Women with disabilities are frequently exposed to and vulnerable to climate change and disaster-induced risks.

29) The Asia Foundation states that because women are not included in water management decisions, they face significant challenges when it comes to decision-making processes, women and girls living in poverty, indigenous women, women with disabilities, and women who are victims of violence. Women with disabilities frequently experience discrimination and lack access to education and training.

### South Sudan:

30) In South Sudan, women, boys and girls experience distinct socio-economic conditions that are shaped by their gender. Women's economic status is related to socio-cultural factors linked to the division of labour, which affect the possibility of access to education, training and other services, income generation, credit and land ownership. Women and girls support the integration of climate change and conflict in South Sudan on a direct and profound ways. Since they are often the ones responsible for providing food, water,

and energy for their families, they face greater challenges in accessing these resources. Climate change has had an outpicted impact on South Sudan, most recently with the largest flooding in Unity State in nearly 50 years. These extreme weather events, illustrated by increased rainfall and flooding, have led to damage of homes and buildings and the indirect economic pressures on families are having a negative impact on access to education for children.

31) Conflict, however, adds to the insecurity, as many women and girls face increased risks of violence and insecurity carrying out basic tasks including their education. With greater social and economic inequalities in South Sudan, women and girls are more vulnerable to exploitation and abuse. Violent conflict tends to exacerbate existing inequalities and vulnerabilities.

32) The role of women in leadership is a key factor for promoting gender equality, advancing the position of women in the society, mainstreaming women's participation in decision-making processes and ensuring that women have access to leadership. However, the patriarchal nature of the South Sudanese society and the associated cultural norms and practices, which are deeply rooted and difficult to be executed, hinders women’s participation in public life. These factors are exacerbated by the current political climate, which has led to a decline in women's representation with the high literacy rate among women. Further, these factors could be aggravated by the current political climate, while some of these issues are associated with the long-term problems of gender inequality and discrimination against women.

33) Low levels of education limit women’s access to specific training and information in turn limits prospects of economic empowerment and possibilities to generate income and make decisions. Women who have not received adequate education may face even greater discrimination. Within their own communities, they have less decision-making power than men. In addition, the lack of access to education for girls has enormous impact and are often difficult to overcome. Customs, traditions, beliefs and superstitions can impede the exercise of rights that have been explicitly recognised in the legal framework of South Sudan.

Tonga:

34) The Kingdom of Tonga has a strong history of promoting gender equality and empowering women in leadership. This is in part due to their strong cultural connections and the important role older women are given within cultural and community settings.22 This is particularly evident with regard to the role of Queen Salote Tupou III, King George Tupou V and Princesses of the Kingdom of Tonga being vital voices for gender equality.

35) Key figures in the promotion of gender equality include Queen Salote Tupou III who has been instrumental in gender equity commitments such as more women in the labour force and more equitable sharing of household and caregiving responsibilities. Notably, this progress has translated into a more family dynamic approach to a domestic setting, where women have taken on roles that were traditionally seen as the exclusive domain of men.

36) Gender equality is also promoted through education and training for women who are educated and were interviewed. There is broad cultural acceptance of the left community, a culture which values women and their contributions to society. This is reflected in an appropriation of the local word "fakalasi" literally meaning “like a lady.”

37) Despite these positive developments, Tonga still faces challenges related to the critical need to enhance education infrastructure and opportunities for Tongan children. This shared vision underpins the BRACE project as a valuable opportunity to leverage existing efforts and evidence-based strategies for systemic improvements in children’s educational outcomes.

Education

Cambodia:

40) The percentage of female primary school teachers in Cambodia in 2022 was 58.90%, while at the lower secondary school it was 45.33%. [1]

41) In 2021, the net enrolment rate was 91.9% in primary school, against a target of 86–87%. [2] The gender parity index (GPI) for girls was 87.4%. [3]
* While the primary school enrolment rate between boys and girls differed only by 1.3 percentage points at the primary level, this gap widened to 9.5 percentage points than for boys.
* The net completion rate at the lower secondary level was 68.6% and 39.3% at the upper secondary level.

42) Less than half of all students completed lower secondary school.
* Throughout the school cycle, girls achieve higher rates of completion than boys. In 2021, the gap between them was 10.3 percentage points at the lower secondary level and 9.5 percentage points at upper secondary level.

43) According to the 2019 Cambodia Population Census [4], 86.2% of the population aged 15+ were literate in 2019, with a gender parity index (GPI) for males and females of 97.9%. The GPI was almost achieved in 2019 in this age group, at 89.3% for males versus 83.4% for females.

South Sudan:

43) The percentage of female pre-primary schoolteachers in South Sudan in 2021 was 63.5%. There was no further data for other school levels.
44) More than half (57%) of all children aged between six and eleven years old were out of school:
* Primary school level was 77% girls vs. 72% boys
* Lower secondary school was 68.3% girls vs. 51.6% boys
* Upper secondary school was 49.0% girls vs. 38.0% boys

45) Despite efforts from the Government of South Sudan and the international community, there is still a significant gender gap in access to higher education, especially in rural areas.
46) Low literacy rates are on the rise, with girls' education considered "abused" in some South Sudanese states. In South Sudan only 34.4% of the population aged 15+ were literate which is the third lowest literacy rate in the world [5].
47) A United Nations report found that more than half of young South Sudanese women are largely unaware of their right to education. Girls are often excluded from educational opportunities and face significant barriers to accessing education.

Tonga:

48) The percentage of female schoolteachers across primary and secondary schools in Tonga is 71%.
49) The out-of-school rate for primary schools in Tonga was 4%. Of those 54% are boys. Overall, this is the lowest out of school rate across the three countries.

## Sexual and Gender-Based Violence and Child Marriage

### Cambodia

- Approximately **19%** of girls and **15.4%** of boys in Cambodia are married before they are **18 years old**.
- Women and girls who have experienced violence or GBV* Available data gathered from 2013 to 2015 cited in the UNICEF Country Plan for Action on Violence Against Women 2019–2023 reveals that around **20% of women aged between 15 and 49** have experienced physical or sexual violence from their spouse. Approximately **44%** of girls reported having experienced sexual abuse at least once before the age of 18.

### South Sudan

- CEFM is alarmingly common in South Sudan, with **52%** of girls married before the age of **18**, giving it the world's *highest prevalence* of CEFM.
- According to a 2017 study by the UNICEF Country Office, this is driven by high levels of social acceptance of violence:
    - **63%** of men agreed that "women should tolerate violence in order to keep her family together."
    - Two-thirds (68%) of women in South Sudan experience GBV* at some point in their lifetime.
    - 68% of women and 63% of men agreed that "there are times when a woman deserves to be beaten."
    - Women (47%) were more likely than men (37%) to agree that "it is okay for a man to hit his wife if she doesn't have sex with him."

### Tonga

- Approximately **100%** of girls are married before the age of 18 in Tonga. *While the minimum age of marriage is 18 years old, it is permissible from 15 years of age with parental consent.*
- Tonga has been identified to have some of the highest rates of violence against children across 40 countries in the Asia-Pacific region, with approximately **80%** of children experiencing GBV*.
- SGFV is a pervasive issue in Tonga with approximately **20%** of ever-partnered women having experienced *physical violence from an intimate partner in their lifetime.* Increasing access to information and gender-inclusive water, sanitation, and hygiene services are recommended.

## Marginalised groups

### Cambodia:

59) Lesbian, gay, bisexual, transgender, queer and Intersex (LGBTQ+) people in Cambodia face significant challenges; more than 80% faced family violence, 10% experienced sexual violence or forced to get married, and about 35% had considered suicide.

59) Children with diverse SOIGESC: children with disabilities, children from low socioeconomic households and children from ethnic minorities have multiple barriers to access education. The Save the Children study on children with diverse SOIGESC in 2020:

60) In 2019, 38% of children aged five years or older were living below the poverty line (compared with 14% of the total population). Of this number, children aged below 18 accounted for 51%.

61) Among children in Cambodia who are not attending school, 20% have never attended primary school; 37% complete primary school, 10% secondary education, and 4% high school.

62) Access to health services is also a challenge. The maternal mortality ratio for women aged 5 years or older with a disability was about 9.6% of Cambodia's total population in 2018; the levels of disability varied, ranging from some difficulty to not being able to perform daily activities.

### South Sudan:

63) South Sudan faces significant challenges in addressing the needs of marginalised groups, particularly in conflict-affected and rural areas. Women and girls are disproportionately impacted by limited access to education and healthcare, often confined by early marriage or gender-based violence.

64) Persons with disabilities face stigmatisation and a lack of accessible services. Internally displaced persons (IDPs) and refugees, displaced by conflict and climate change, experience significant challenges in accessing basic services and opportunities for education with limited recognition of their rights. These groups are especially vulnerable to the impacts of conflict and displacement.

### Tonga:

66) Diverse gender identities are much more widely accepted in Tonga than diverse sexual orientations. There is broad cultural acceptance of the intersex community, a cultural group who have always been at the core of Tongan society. LGBTQ+ children can become targets due to the social vulnerability created by their sexuality or apparent/assumed

### Cambodia:

- **Multidimensional Poverty**: 16.6% of the population is multidimensionally poor while an additional 20.3% is considered vulnerable to multidimensional poverty.
- **Urban vs Rural**: The proportion of people living in urban areas is 42%, compared to 58% in rural areas.

### Labour Force Participation:

- **Working Age Population (15+)**: The labour force participation rate for the working-age population aged 15 or older was 60.9%. Among women, it was 73.7% in urban areas and 72.0% in rural areas.
- **Women's Participation**: Women represented only 41% of the female workforce compared to 80% for men.

### Disability Employment:

- **Formally Employed Persons with Disabilities**: The number of formally employed persons with disabilities in Cambodia amounted to approximately 3,200 (or about 0.5%) and accounted for less than 1% of total employment in the private sector.
- **National Social Protection Council**: Established a National Social Protection Council in 2017 to develop social protection policies and programmes.

### South Sudan:

- **Unemployment Rate**: The unemployment rate among those aged 15 years or older is 9.45% in South Sudan.
- **Land Access**: Land access plays a critical role in sustaining rural livelihoods, especially for women who are more likely to be landless than men. Land tenure systems vary significantly between smallerholder farmers and those who cultivate large-scale commercial farms.

76| Large-scale land acquisitions in South Sudan have further exacerbated land inequality. Studies suggest that around 9% of the total land area in South Sudan has been allocated for foreign investment, often at the expense of local communities. As of 2020, an estimated 15 million hectares (37 million acres) were under lease to foreign agribusiness or resource extraction projects, much of which is situated in fertile regions crucial for smallholder farming and pastoral livelihoods. The loss of land has displaced people due to conflict and has also strained land tenure systems, making it difficult for displaced populations to reclaim their land, contributing to rising tensions over land access.

77| Women are disproportionately affected by these changes as they often face additional barriers to land access due to customary practices that favor male landowners. Only 12% of women in rural South Sudan have formal titles to land, and many lack the resources or knowledge necessary for sustainable agricultural practices or invest in their land. These issues highlight the urgent need for policies that address gender inequalities in land ownership and access in South Sudan. Women face severe disadvantages in land tenure security, which can limit their ability to secure credit, start businesses, or lead in public affairs. Historically, in the past 20 years, women headships have increased only 13.6% compared with men's 48%. They are also marginalized in access to property rights and land-related services.

Tonga:

78| Tonga’s multidimensional poverty measure, and data collected via the 2015-2016 Household Income and Expenditure Survey, estimates 22% of the population assessed as poor. 14% are vulnerable as a result of deprivation, and 22% are vulnerable in terms of poverty.

In 2019, the Tongan labour force participation rate stood at 46.7%. Men have a higher labor force participation rate than women (53%) with the highest rates among those aged groups 15-64. 

80| In 2019, 50% of households were headed by women. Seasonal-worker schemes and other forms of labour migration to nearby countries such as Australia, New Zealand, and the Cook Islands are common in Tonga, with many women leaving their homes abroad and leave women to manage houses alone.

Climate Change and Disaster Risk Management

81| The above statistics highlight the various ways in which discriminatory gender and social norms can exacerbate inequalities. This is further evidenced by the existence of CEFMU and GBV. Climate change has differential impacts on children depending on intersecting factors such as age, sex, location, and socioeconomic status. Women are disproportionately affected among others. Climate change can further exacerbate gender inequality while gender- responsive policies can help to mitigate these inequalities. The following table provides statistics on climate change and interactively, including data from the CCRI.

The CCRI is structured according to two central pillars: exposure to climate and environmental hazards and vulnerability to those hazards. By combining the two categories, the CCRI brings 57 variables together to measure risk across 163 countries.

The combination of hazard, exposure and vulnerability aligns with the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) 2014 working definition of risk.

### Cambodia

82) Cambodia ranks in the top third of countries at risk for children and young people on climate change and environment stress according to the CCRI (46th of 163). The report highlights that children are particularly vulnerable to heatwaves, flooding and vector-borne disease and require investments in social services, particularly access to WASH facilities. These investments will help protect children’s futures from the impacts of climate change.

83) Implementing gender-sensitive policies, strategies and programmes, of the government, that look at the mainstreaming of gender equality in climate action, are hampered by many factors including a lack of data on women and girls, limited understanding of gender concepts, persistent discriminatory social norms and traditional gender roles, limited age, and sexually disaggregated data and a big gap between planning and budgetary climate change and gender mainstreaming implementation at both national and subnational levels.

### South Sudan

84) Global reports from the IPCC has consistently highlighted the disproportionate impact of climate change on women, particularly in vulnerable regions like South Sudan. Climate change exacerbates existing vulnerabilities related to poverty, displacement, and food insecurity. Women, primarily responsible for household water and food provision, face increased burdens due to climate-related shocks such as droughts, floods, and heatwaves that reduce economic livelihoods and deepen gender inequalities.

85) Women are disproportionately affected by social, economic, and cultural disadvantages that limit their access to resources, decision-making, and economic opportunities. The majority of South Sudan’s agricultural workforce consists of women; yet they face challenges in land ownership and access to resources like water and seeds. Climate change has led to increased droughts, floods, and increased flooding, have reduced crop yields or destroyed crops before harvest. Additionally, climate change is reducing the productivity of their farms and agricultural inputs.

86) Climate-related disasters such as floods and girls are disproportionately affected by extreme weather events such as floods and droughts, which are becoming more frequent in South Sudan. The increase risk of displacement and food insecurity has a significant impact on women’s health and well-being, and South Sudan is no exception.

87) Food security is also impacted by climate change disproportionately impacts women and girls in South Sudan, where recurring droughts and floods have led to a sharp decline in food production and access. Women are more vulnerable than men because they are primary caregivers and food providers; they are hit hardest by food insecurity, often leading to malnutrition and other health problems.

88) Gender-sensitive adaptation strategies. The IPCC AR5 report underlines the importance of women’s participation in climate change adaptation policies. In South Sudan, where women are key to agricultural production, gender-responsive approaches are crucial for building resilience in climate-affected communities.

**Page 171**
---

89 South Sudan ranks very high on the CCRI at joint 7th of 163 countries at risk for children and young people on climate change and environment stress.

### Tonga

**90) A 2018 Analysis of Tropical Cyclone Gita in the Kingdom of Tonga identified heightened vulnerabilities for specific populations, including individuals with disabilities, the elderly, widowed women, single men, and those living alone. These groups are more likely to be heads of households, single mothers (especially those with many dependents), and people with diverse sexual orientations.**

**91) Women's livelihoods in Tonga are heavily reliant on natural resources, such as mulberry and pandanus leaves for weaving, which are vulnerable to climate change impacts. This vulnerability is exacerbated by gender-based disparities in aid distribution. Female-headed households, particularly those led by widowed women or single mothers with small families, are frequently overlooked by aid providers, who often require the woman to be employed full-time.**

**92) Save the Children's Regional Situation Analysis identified heightened risks of child sexual abuse and violence in the Pacific following natural hazards. Participants attributed this risk to factors such as displacement, lack of access to education, poverty, intergenerational trauma, cultural norms, poverty, family separation, and climate change being key drivers of violence against children. These factors are exacerbated by gender-based violence, parental absence, substance abuse, and economic hardship.**

**93) The role of women in disaster preparedness and decision-making in Tonga remains unclear. However, disaster preparedness committees are predominantly male-dominated, with women often excluded from decision-making processes during emergency situations. Following Tropical Cyclone Gita, a survey of women in evacuation centres revealed that they were not involved in the planning or implementation of emergency reporting that they were simply following instructions. These findings highlight the need for greater inclusion in disaster preparedness and response.**

# Country Analysis on Gender Equality and Social Inclusion

The following section highlights key findings and data in the five domain areas across all three countries. The data are presented for all countries to identify similarities and differences across countries and support shared learning during project implementation.

## Domain I: Laws, Policies and Institutions

### Cambodia

(94) The Cambodian government has signed and ratified all core international human rights treaties and conventions related to GEISI. This includes the UN Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), the CRC, and UN Convention on the Rights of the Child (CRC), and UN Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities (CRPD). The texts of all three of these treaties and the texts of the ratified treaties have then been integrated in Cambodia’s domestic laws.

(95) Cambodia has taken numerous steps to advance gender equality through laws and policies. These include the establishment of the Ministry of Women's Affairs, the establishment of the Ministry of Women's Affairs, and creation of new gender equality policies, including a law specifically on domestic violence.


## Gender components of national policies

### The Constitution of Cambodia (1993)

- *Men and women have equal rights before the law and enjoy equal participation in political life, according to social and cultural norms* (Article 35), *equality in employment and equal pay for equal work.* (Article 42)
- It explicitly prohibits "all forms of discrimination against woman" (Article 45).14

### Development policies

Cambodia has mainstreamed gender equality into its national development strategies, plans and programs.

For example, gender equality and women's empowerment are part of the Cambodian government’s National Strategic Plan for Growth, Employment, Equity, Efficiency and Sustainability (NSP-GEES) 2013–2017. Gender equality is also a key element of Paragraph 4: Resilience, Sustainable and Inclusive Development, and the National Action Plan to Implement this strategy.

### Ministry of Women's Affairs

Cambodia institutionalised gender equality through the establishment of:

- *Ministry of Women’s Affairs (MoWA)* in 1997
- Cambodian National Council for Women in 2001
- Technical Working Group on Gender Equality in 2003
- Gender Mainstreaming Action Groups in 2005 across line ministries, and many others.


---
**Page 173**
---

Gender specific policies

Responding to the Program Strategy, MoWiA developed the national policy on gender equality and women’s empowerment known as Neary Ratanak Strategic Plan with the current plan (Phase V) set for 2024-2028.

The strategic plan aims to combat GBV in both the private and public spheres and empowers women through education, training, and employment. It includes development programs across all sectors and at all levels of government institutions.
The National Action Plan Against Violence Against Women was also formulated based on the vision of Neary Ratanak V.

Cambodia used to treat domestic violence as 'family issue' and was acceptable in the Cambodian culture although Cambodia's Penal Code prohibits physical and sexual assaults and violence. This type of violence was no longer tolerated after the Law on the Prevention of Domestic Violence Act (Law No 102) was adopted in September 2009. Domestic violence has since been criminalised.

Education Strategic Plan 2019-2023

Gender mainstreaming in the education sector is considered a key action to contribute the implementing the Reangtakor Strategy Phase 4 of the Royal Government and Neary Ratanak Strategic Plan, especially in the primary and secondary education sectors.
The Ministry of Education Youth and Sports (MoEYS) has developed the Gender Mainstreaming Strategic Plan in Education Sector 2016-2020 and mainstreamed some issues related to gender equality into other sub-sectors including Education Strategic Plan 2019-2023 and Teacher Policy Action Plan.

Disability and Social Inclusion Laws and Policies

UN Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities (CRPD)

Cambodia signed the CRPD in 2007 and ratified the Convention in 2012.
In 2009, Cambodia passed a Law on the Protection and the Promotion of the Rights of Persons with Disabilities.

Criticisms have been made suggesting that the law fails to adopt a human rights-based approach. It uses language that references stigma against people with disabilities rather than addressing access to education, transportation, social services, and independent living.
As such, there is no full compliance with international human rights laws and standards.
The Cambodian government then decided to draft a new disability law to fully align with the UN CRPD.

National Disability Strategic Plan (NDSP)

With a strong commitment to promote and protect the rights of persons with disabilities, the Cambodian government has formulated the National Disability Strategic Plan aiming to


---
**Page 174**
---

improve the quality of life and promote equality of persons with disabilities. It focuses on nine strategic objectives, including:

- increasing access to quality and sustainable services such as health, rehabilitation, vocational training, economic security, social services, accessibility, access to rights, and promoting equal participation of people with disabilities.

The current NSDP is for 2019–2023^1^ and the new NSDP 2024-2028 is still in draft, awaiting approval.


### Disability Action Council (DAC)

In July 2006, Cambodia established the Disability Action Council (DAC)^2^ to implement Cambodia’s Law on the Protection and the Promotion of the Rights of Persons with Disabilities.

Disability rights and inclusion is a cross-ministerial responsibility; however, the Ministry of Social Affairs, Veterans and Youth Rehabilitation (MoSVY) is the core ministry in charge of this work.

MoSVY has officially launched the “disability identification card” for persons with disabilities in late 2023 as part of strengthening social protection system to foster equitable and inclusive development.


As of October 2023, 288,690 persons with disabilities have been identified by commune focal points across the country; with the ministry having issued 222,148 cards for persons with disabilities^3^.

### LGBTI+ policies

In Cambodia, there is no legislation that criminalises LGBTQI+ people; however, at the same time, there is no explicit law to protect them as well. although the Cambodian Constitution states that all Cambodians have equal rights and are entitled to receive protection from the state.

The Cambodian Human Rights Committee has been appointed as a government body to handle human rights issues in the country, including those of LGBTQI+ persons.


### Climate Change Laws and Policies

#### Climate change in gender policies

As a government body to promote gender equality and women’s empowerment and a member of the Cambodian agencies on climate change and disaster management, MoWRA has prioritised gender equality as part of its national climate security strategy plan (Goal 6). MoWRA has developed a:

- National Gender Equality Strategy and Climate Change Action Plan 2019–2023
- Master Plan for Gender and Climate Change 2019–2030
- Action Plan on Gender and Climate Change 2019–2023

The MoWRA has also established the Climate Change Unit. Several pilot projects have been implemented in four provinces related to gender analysis, capacity building for women and climate change resilience and adaptation, safe WASH, disaster risk reduction (DRR) during flood and drought situations.
