290 officials (251 women) from the Provincial Department of Women’s Affairs and Provincial Women Development Centers received training on gender and climate change adaptation strategies, and were equipped to implement these strategies with family economic development for women on saving and renewable energy.

### Climate policies

Cambodia has ratified the UN Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) in 1995. In 2006, Cambodia established a National Climate Change Committee with the mandate to coordinate climate change activities across government departments’ policies, strategies, regulations, plans and programs in response to climate change.

The Committee then worked with multi-stakeholders to develop a 10-year Cambodia Climate Action Plan (CCAP) that aims for a low-carbon economy, green, low-carbon, climate-resilient, equitable, sustainable and knowledge-based society. Within the strategic plan, MoWA is tasked to promote gender in climate change response through the development of gender-sensitive policies, strategies, regulations and operational rules for gender and climate change initiatives and make these operational through gender-sensitive budgeting mechanisms. The CCAP also aims at establishing the necessary mechanisms for scaling up the proven experiences on gender and climate change, and elicit and apply lessons learned from gender and climate change for sharing and learning in national, regional and global forums.

### Cambodia’s Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC)

Cambodia’s Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) seeks to address the differential impact of climate change on women and men. Women play a critical role in the development, implementation, monitoring, and enforcement of climate actions across sectors and are therefore included as key stakeholders in the NDC process. The MoWA is involved in the development of climate policies, including the NDC and National Adaptation Plan (NAP). The NDC also aims at addressing gender equality issues on women, children, disabled people, and other marginalised groups is largely ancillary.

Cambodia’s NDC and Long-Term Strategy for Carbon Neutrality: Progress Report 2022 revealed that the proportion of female teachers in primary education in Cambodia varied between 10.64 per cent in 2022. In the education sector, climate subjects were integrated into the curriculum of schools, and trainings of education officials.

### Sustainable Development

The National Climate Change Committee is part of Cambodia’s National Council for Sustainable Development (NCSD) established in 2010 and chaired by the Minister of Environment Ministry, along with three other government bodies, including the National Council of Green Growth, the National Biodiversity Secretariat, and the National Biodiversity Steering Committee.


---
**Page 176**
---

The main goal of the National Council for Sustainable Development is to promote sustainable development and to ensure economic, environmental, social and cultural balance.

### Disaster Management

Cambodia established a National Committee for Disaster Management (NCDM) in 1995 with its structure at both national and subnational levels. The Government drew on lessons learned from the country's experience with regularly occurring natural hazards with the mandate to provide timely and effective emergency relief to the victims of disasters and develop preventive measures to reduce loss of lives and property.

The Guideline for Mainstreaming Gender in Inclusive Disaster Management 2021 developed by the NCDM provides a framework for integrating gender considerations across the entire disaster management cycle, including prevention, mitigation, preparedness, response, recovery, and rehabilitation. The guideline also encourages the participation of groups vulnerable to the impacts of disasters, such as women, youth, children, the elderly, and people with disabilities in the National Forum on Disaster Risk Reduction.

### South Sudan

The Government of South Sudan has committed to international frameworks related to GEIS by ratifying core treaties such as the CEDAW, the CRC, and the CRPD. These international instruments provide a legal framework for national policies aimed at advancing GEIS. While South Sudan's capacity to fully implement these treaties has been hampered by conflict, these conventions guide national policy development.

South Sudan's Constitution of 2011 enshrines gender equality as a fundamental principle in all spheres of life and prohibits discrimination based on sex, race, religion, language, political affiliation, social status, or any other condition. The Constitution also mandates the enactment of laws, policies, and institutions. The Transitional Constitution of 2011 enshrines gender equality as a fundamental principle in all spheres of life and prohibits discrimination based on sex.

Furthermore, the government established the Ministry for Gender Equality and Child Development (MGECD) to promote women's rights and social inclusion across governance initiatives. Key legislation such as the National Policy on Sexual and Gender-Based Violence Bill (2019), also have been implemented to combat gender-based violence and promote women’s empowerment.

### Gender components of national policies

The Constitutional Translators of South Sudan (2011) The Government guarantees equality and protection of women's rights. Article 16 explicitly mandates equal participation of women in public life, political representation, and decision-making processes. It also prohibits discrimination against women and encourages the elimination of all forms of cultural misdeeds in education, employment, and leadership.

Article 16 of the Constitution mandates that "women shall have the right to participate fully in public life and have equal rights with men."


---
**Page 177**
---

The Constitution also addresses GBV, outlining the state’s responsibility to protect women from all forms of violence, including sexual exploitation during conflicts.

### National Gender Policy (2015)

The National Gender Policy serves as the primary framework guiding gender equality efforts in South Sudan. It emphasizes gender mainstreaming across all sectors—education, health, agriculture, and governance—and seeks to promote women’s economic empowerment and political participation. The policy aims to enhance legal frameworks that protect women's rights and increase women's representation in decision-making positions.

### Sexual and Gender-Based Violence (SGBV) Bill (2019)

The SGBV Bill focuses on addressing violence against women and girls in both public and private spheres. It includes provisions to prevent, punish, and protect victims of GBV. The law aims to ensure that survivors receive medical attention within 72 hours of conflict, ensuring that GBVs receive legal, medical, and psychological support.

### National Action Plan on Women, Peace, and Security (2015-2020)

In response to UN Security Council Resolution 1325, South Sudan developed a National Action Plan to increase the participation of women in peacebuilding and post-conflict recovery efforts. The plan addresses issues such as gender-based violence, sexual exploitation, and national reconciliation efforts, addressing the disproportionate impact of conflict on women and girls.

### South Sudan Women's Empowerment Strategy and Action Plan (2015-2030)

This strategy was developed to promote women’s participation in economic activities and leadership. It focuses on creating an enabling environment for women to engage in small- and medium-sized enterprises and addresses barriers like limited access to financial services, land ownership, and education.

The National Gender Coordination Forum has also been instrumental in advancing gender mainstreaming across government ministries.

### Education Sector Strategic Plan (2017-2022)

The Education Sector Strategic Plan focuses on increasing girls' enrollment and retention in schools. It outlines a series of interventions to address the root causes of school dropouts, including early marriage and other gender-based barriers that prevent girls from completing their education.

### National Girl’s Education Strategy 2024-2028 for South Sudan

This strategy sets out a comprehensive framework to address the persistent challenges hindering girls' access to education in South Sudan. It emphasizes the transformational role that educated women play in promoting sustainable development and the overall economic growth of the country. The strategy aims to create an enabling environment where girls can realize their full potential and contribute to the nation's development.

# Disability and Social Inclusion Laws and Policies

## National Disability and Inclusion Policy (2018)

Although focused on persons with disabilities, this policy includes a gendered lens to ensure that women with disabilities have equal opportunities in accessing education, healthcare, and economic empowerment. The policy also addresses the specific needs of women with disabilities and emphasizes the need for inclusive development programs.

## Social Inclusion Policies for LGBTQ+ Communities

South Sudan's National Policy on Gender Equality is in the protection of LGBTQ+ rights, and there are no explicit laws criminalizing or protecting individuals based on sexual orientation or gender identity. LGBTQ+ persons often face discrimination and exclusion, and the lack of a clear legal framework leaves them vulnerable to societal stigma.

# Climate Change Laws and Policies

## National Climate Change Policy (2016)

South Sudan's National Climate Change Policy integrates gender considerations, recognizing that women are disproportionately affected by climate change due to their roles in agriculture. The policy emphasizes the need for gender-sensitive adaptation and mitigation strategies, advocating for women's participation in decision-making related to climate change resilience and adaptation efforts.

## The National Adaptation Plan (NAP) (2016)

This was developed in response to the growing impacts of climate change, with a focus on building adaptive capacity among vulnerable populations like women, children, and marginalized communities. The NAP aims to address both immediate and long-term climate adaptation needs while aligning with South Sudan's national development priorities as well as its international commitments under the Paris Agreement. It integrates gender considerations, emphasizing the importance of women in climate resilience and adaptation strategies.

## Ministry of Environment and Forestry

The Ministry of Environment and Forestry works closely with the MoGCSM to ensure that climate change policies are gender-responsive.

# Tonga

## The Tonga Strategic Development Framework 2015-2025 is underpinned by the motto God's Kingdom in Tonga. This highlights the important role of faith and how it is intertwined with national structures across the Kingdom.

### Gender Laws and Policies

#### The National Women's Empowerment and Gender Equality Tonga Policy

The National Women's Empowerment and Gender Equality Tonga policy and its Strategic Plan of Action 2019-2025 is a key tool to guide national gender policies. This revised policy continues to promote the concept of ensuring that Tonga's development benefits are distributed and equally accessible to everyone.


---
**Page 179**
---

The policy is also consistent with Tonga’s Strategic Development Framework 2015-2025 (TSDF II), National Outcome 3 for a more inclusive sustainable and empowering human development.

Embedded within this policy is gender mainstreaming in all government plans and services which has been regarded as one of the priority outcomes.


Voluntary National Review

The Tonga Government’s Voluntary National Review 2019 provides an overview of progress made on the Tongan Strategic Development Framework and the country's clarity to implement its targets in the SDGs.

During this review process, the government identified five (5) SDG indicators that are most relevant to its development priorities. One such indicator is SDG 5.c., which tracks the “proportion of women who have access to adequate sexual and reproductive health care services for gender equality and women’s empowerment.” This highlights the government's focus on strengthening transparency and accountability in achieving gender equality goals.


UN Commission on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW)

Tonga acknowledges women’s increase in development, with gains in leadership roles and education. However, challenges remain, especially in political participation and ratifying CEDAW.

Opposition from religious and community leaders has stalled progress, despite CEDAW's potential to strengthen the legal framework for gender equality.


Ratification would strengthen legal protections against violence and abuse, fostering a more equitable society.101 Key informants indicated that they continue to work with national leaders on this issue.


Domestic Violence

Tonga has implemented a multi-faceted approach to address domestic violence. The Family Protection Act (FPA) of 2013 offers legal protection for family members, establishing the Family Protection Unit (FPU). It also provides financial support and promotes well-being.

Financial support for non-governmental organisations (NGOs) working on FPA implementation has been provided by the UNDP.


Additionally, the National Women’s Empowerment and Gender Equality Tonga (WEGET) Policy 2019-2025 recognises domestic violence as a significant social issue. The document outlines strategies to address this issue through prevention or reduce its prevalence. Tangible steps have been taken to support victims and survivors.

These include:

• the establishment of the Family Protection Legal Aid Centre in 2018;
• a renovated police station with a dedicated safe space for children in the Domestic Violence Unit; and
• a 24/7 counselling hotline operated by key service providers.


---
**Page 180**
---

The Women’s Affairs and Gender Equality Division within the Ministry of Internal Affairs serves as the national machinery, playing a central role in coordinating, monitoring, and evaluating policies and programmes.

Marital rape was outlawed in 1995. Additionally, the Criminal Offences Act recognises specific crimes such as “procuring the prostitution of females,” “procuring the defilement of females,” and “abduction of women.” However, the Family Protection Act (2013) criminalises domestic violence and recognises violence against children, including sexual abuse.

Tonga grapples with the tension between cultural norms and legal protections regarding violence. While corporal punishment is often viewed as an accepted form of parental discipline, the Criminal Offences Act (1988) prohibits assault, body harm, and cruelty towards children under 16 years old.

Similarly, the FPA criminalises domestic violence and recognises violence against children, or committed in their presence, as an aggravating factor. This legal framework suggests a move toward greater protection for women and children but traditional practices may persist.


Pacific Platform for Action on Gender Equality

The Pacific Platform for Action on Gender Equality (PPAGE) and the revitalised Pacific Leaders Gender Equality Declaration (PLGED) serve as crucial regional instruments that align with broader global commitments, including those made under the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action of 1995. The PPAGE is a key component of the 2050 Strategy for the Blue Pacific Continent.

It specifically calls for the meaningful participation of all Pacific peoples, particularly women, in decision-making processes through engagement action including access to climate finance, disaster risk management, the protection of persons affected by climate change-related disasters and emergencies, and the sustainable management and use of oceans and river resources for development.


National Policy on Gender and Development

Tonga has made commitments towards progressing gender equality through the implementation of its National Policy on Gender and Development (RNPAGD) and its Strategic Plan of Action.

The Tonga RNPAGD, launched in May 2014, provides a significant platform to improve the lives of women, men, boys, girls though strategic objectives and associated interventions. The six priority outcomes of the Tonga RNPAGD are geared toward mainstreaming gender equality and the empowerment of women in Tonga.


The Constitution

The Kingdom of Tonga stands out in the Pacific as a constitutional monarchy that maintained its independence. Its 1875 Constitution established a Westminster-style government with a tiered system of power: the royal family and nobles, ceremonial chiefs, and commoners.

While this system fostered autonomy, it also presented challenges for achieving gender equality.


---
**Page 181**
---

The Legislative Assembly’s amendments, including the introduction of 17 elected seats in 2014, offer a promising path towards a more inclusive political landscape.

## Age of Consent

The age of consent for girls is 15, while there is no minimum age of consent for boys. Under the Criminal Offences Act 1988 and subsequent Amendments, rape is an offence that can only be committed by a man against a woman or girl. This legal disparity raises concerns about protection from sexual abuse. The legal age of marriage for both men and women is 16, although parental consent is mandatory for anyone under 18.

## Disability and Social Inclusion Laws and Policies

### National Policy on Disability Inclusive Development (2014-2016)

Tonga’s National Policy on Disability Inclusive Development promotes a “whole-of-government” approach. It encourages all government bodies to collaborate to improve the lives of people with disabilities. It involves civil society organisations as partners and aims to:

- Develop and implement laws for disability inclusion.
- Expand support services for people with disabilities.
- Increase accessibility in public spaces and transport.

### New Education Act

The New Education Act (2013) promotes inclusive education, meaning all children have the right to quality education regardless of ability. It raised the compulsory school age and emphasised early childhood development. The policy has had a positive impact on high literacy rates for both women and men, with a substantial majority of adults able to read and write. However, there are still gender disparities in educational outcomes, particularly with boys having a higher completion rate than girls. Despite high literacy rates, child marriage and teenage pregnancy remain significant issues.

### Social Inclusion

There are various laws indirectly promoting social inclusion, like the Health Services Act (2013) and Government Residencies Bill (2013, under consultation). These promote access to healthcare and fair labour practices, which can benefit vulnerable populations. Under Tonga’s 1875 Constitution and subsequent Amendments, there is a general guarantee of equality before the law regardless of race or social status or class. The constitution does not explicitly prohibit discrimination based on sex, sexual orientation, gender identity, and expression, and sex characteristics.

**Page 182**

# Climate Change Laws and Policies

## Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC)

The Kingdom of Tonga’s NDC puts a strong emphasis on ensuring the consideration of intersectional and intergenerational aspects such as gender, income, age, etc. It includes provisions for the integration of climate change considerations into development with local communities and indigenous peoples in a gender-responsive manner. The document however remains limited in its reference to implement or progress the Gender Action Plan (GAP).

## Climate Change Policy

Tonga’s Climate Change Policy (2016) recognises the differentiated impacts faced by men and women as a result of the varying social, environmental and economic situations and seeks to address these through gender-responsive policies and programmes. This is also demonstrated in the leadership and technical team of the Department of Climate Change, which includes representatives from the Ministry of Education, Disaster Management, Environment, Climate Change and Communications.

The National Policy on Gender and Development and Strategic Plan are fully aligned with this policy and the NDC. They need to be reviewed by relevant government agencies for alignment.

Domain 2: Social Norms and Roles, Responsibilities and Time Use

(99) Discriminatory social and gender norms, roles and stereotypes have significant impacts on the way different populations groups across the three countries experience climate change and their capacities to adapt. These socially constructed norms and rules determine how women and men are expected to behave in all aspects of life.

(100) These pervasive norms affect many areas of life, covered further in Domains 3-5, with particular relevance for women's roles as caregivers and protectors, especially those living in poverty, indigenous people and other marginalised groups.


Cambodia:

(101) Cambodia is steeped in patriarchal traditions and discriminatory gender norms are rarely noted. These societal norms have dictated girls' and women's subordination to men and boys in both private and public spheres.

(102) A 2019 study found that "the pervasive belief that traditional stereotypes and norms were the main barriers hindering Cambodia's women from gaining broader opportunities, including education, employment, political participation, and access to social decision-making processes, acting against domestic violence, and enjoying an adequate standard of living."

(103) According to the UN in Cambodia "social norms and beliefs that restrict what women can do are a major barrier to their empowerment. These include gender stereotypes. Gender norms are also embedded in government policies, such as the National Children Policy which promotes the idea of women's responsibility for the care of children and the home."

(104) Social expectations of women and men are evident in day-to-day interactions in workplaces and communities and are frequently cited as barriers to working women. The 2019 Cambodia Labour Force Survey found that "women are entitled to sex regardless of consent, directly contribute to GBV." 

(105) Women's roles in the household are also gendered. In rural areas, the roles of women and men are socially and culturally constructed in Cambodian society with men often seen as breadwinners while women are expected to be homemakers and primary caregivers and homemakers. According to the 2019 Cambodia Labour Force Survey, women's share of unpaid work is significantly higher than that done by a man. Almost the same gender distribution in household heads emerged across both urban and rural areas.

(105) In terms of climate change, these discriminatory gender stereotypes can limit girls and women from developing the necessary skills and capacities to adapt to the changing climate. Women are more likely to be affected by climate change than men because women belong to ethnic, racial and religious minority groups, and with women's disabilities and age, they are more vulnerable to climate change. Women of disadvantaged socio-economic status, the lower positions they are in, limited education, cultural norms and gender stereotypes.

(106) Further, due to cultural norms, women are often seen as passive victims rather able to take action. Thus, reality, women are often responsible for protecting the household's

107\. The perception of women as victims is also considered a reason why there are fewer women in leadership positions and why women continue to face discrimination due to their persistent poverty, limited educational opportunities, lack of decision-making power, and inadequate access to reproductive and sexual health services linked to pregnancy and childbirth.

108\. With climate change negatively affecting water availability and quality, the gender bias in domestic work is likely to increase. Women are responsible for a large proportion of domestic water consumption, 123 using dirty water for cooking and cleaning with risk of pathogen transmission.

109\. Restrictive gender norms are reflected in the widely unequal distribution of unpaid domestic labor between men and women. In South Sudan, for example, almost 30% of employed women reported that their partners did not provide any additional assistance to them when they were ill or injured according to the 2014 UN Women report. The report continues to reveal that climate change and disasters add to women's unpaid work in affected regions. In the absence of other support, unpaid work may increase, leading to a decrease in time available for income-generating activities on their education.

110\. Barriers such as livelihoods and unpaid domestic and care work can keep women in more vulnerable work, limiting the opportunities to expand businesses, advance their careers or get jobs. This is particularly true when women are unable to access credit, further education, leisure or community engagement. Despite unpaid care and domestic work being considered major obstacles to gender equality, public policies and development efforts rarely target it.

### South Sudan

111\. In South Sudan, women, men, girls and boys experience different socio-economic conditions. Deep-rooted patriarchal traditions, cultural beliefs and discriminatory gender norms reinforce gender inequalities across the social, economic and political spheres and limit women's access to resources such as land, credit, education, employment training and technical advice, decision-making processes, income generation, and credit and savings.

112\. Women and girls experience the intersection of climate change and conflict in South Sudan. The lack of infrastructure and services, including roads, schools, health facilities for providing food, water, and energy for their families, they face greater challenges in accessing resources and services than men do. For example, women are more vulnerable to South Sudan, most recently with the largest flooding in Unity State in nearly 50 years. These extreme weather events have led to the destruction of infrastructure such as the building of educational buildings and the indirect economic pressures on families are having a negative impact on access to education at all levels, especially for young women and girls.

113\. Conflict, however, adds to the insecurity, as many women and girls face increased risk of sexual violence. Women and girls also experience greater vulnerability in conflict with greater social capital, men often have greater options for coping with the stresses of instability and are more likely to be able to access resources such as food and medicines.

114\. The current peace-building phase can be an opportunity for advancing gender equity and including women's perspectives in post-conflict approaches and increasing their


---
**Page 185**
---

participation in leadership. However, the patriarchal customs of South Sudanese society and the associated laws can prevent women’s participation in public life and peacebuilding, exacerbated by the underlying lack of political will, limited funding, ethnic politics, and gender inequality.

### Lower education levels and limited access to specific training and information in turn limit women's ability to participate effectively. Women are often excluded from decision-making processes at all levels, including local, national, and international, and make decisions about expenditures and resources. Indigenous women and girls face even greater discrimination. Within their own communities, they have less decision-making power than men. In many cases, women’s voices are not heard or taken seriously; when they do speak up, their views may be dismissed as irrelevant or unimportant.

### Factors such as religion, traditional customs, and social norms influence people's views on their environmental responsibilities and social norms. The belief that "the will of God" dictates societal roles and responsibilities often leads to the exclusion of women from decision-making in the family and community. High literacy rates and deference to authority perpetuate harmful attitudes towards women.

### Women and girls suffer time poverty because they bear the brunt of unpaid labour, which limits their time and opportunities for education and skill development. Flooding has increased significantly over recent years, with many families living in flood-prone areas or often in floodwaters, and must keep the household functioning even during extreme weather events.

### Traditional customs and practices on property limit women’s economic power. Women are not allowed to inherit land; they cannot own livestock; they do not have access to credit; they cannot inherit land; they have rights and entitlements to land for cultivation upon marriage; these women are often excluded from decision-making in the family or community when a man is married, divorced.

### It is important to note that ethnicity, language, religion, social class and rural/urban living affect gender relations differently, even within the same ethnic group. 

### Gender-based violence (GBV) is prevalent among different ethnic groups; the size of a person's herd being a key marker of wealth. Consequently, cattle is often a common catalyst of inter-communal violence, even before the current conflict. In many areas, women are not allowed to own livestock.

and an emerging patriarchal influence. Traditional customs and Christian values have, at times, been interpreted to justify a more paternalistic social hierarchy, creating tension with the historical power dynamics of the "Fatu" system.

124. [...]

125. Men may take on roles involving community leadership, including serving as chiefs or elders, while women often play important roles in community gatherings and social events, such as attending church services and participating in religious ceremonies. Women also play supporting roles in community leadership, providing input during meetings and helping to ensure that decisions are made with the needs of all people in mind. Both men and women participate in social and cultural activities, although the nature of these varies by context. For example, women may attend church or other community meetings or engaging in traditional ceremonies, while women may gather for social events such as weddings.

126. Men often allocate time to income-generating activities such as working in the fields, raising livestock, and managing small businesses. Women are responsible for the primary care of children and elderly within homes while also taking on roles outside of the home for employment or community engagement such as with church groups, hospital committees, and other organizations. This has led to a shift in gender roles with increasing sharing of childcare and household chores. This was attributed to increasing educational opportunities for women.

127. Both men, women and gender diverse individuals engage in efforts to promote economic development through various activities including community-based savings and loans associations, youth empowerment programs, awareness-raising campaigns, and advocacy initiatives.

128. A significant proportion of young people within the diverse SOGESGC community often engage in informal adoption of young people from extended families or communities, sometimes with a view toward marriage. The SOGESGC community is comprised of community members cohabiting and pooling resources. It's anticipated that this will continue to evolve as people gain access to optional social assistance, including tasks like cooking and providing care for the underprivileged within their local communities.

129. Around 34% of people are moderately deprived, with a further 20% experiencing severe deprivation. Men were less likely to meet the threshold for Time Use Deprivation. People living in rural areas were more likely to meet the threshold than those meeting the threshold for any deprivation.

130. The Tongan economy heavily relies on remittances from Tongans working overseas as well as foreign aid. The United States is the main source of remittances, followed by Australia and New Zealand.

131. During and after extreme weather events and natural hazards, social norms in Tonga traditionally encourage women to stay at home with their families during usual domestic labour, often take charge of preserving water, food, medicine, and valuables. This aligns with her role in tasks like cooking, cleaning, and water treatment. Additionally, women are more likely than men to evacuate to safer areas or safer areas. Conversely, men who are more engaged in agriculture, are expected to reinforce homes and other structures during extreme weather events and natural hazards.

132. These distinct roles highlight the intersection of gender and disaster preparedness in Tongan society. The education sector is deemed to be feminised, and

### Cambodia

134\. Women have been found to have less power to voice their opinions and influence decision-making processes in both their families and in wider society. Further, the engagement of women in climate change actions often depend on the decisions and requirements made by men. The gendered impacts of climate change are evident in these smaller communities where women are more vulnerable than men.

135\. In water politics, women's participation is far from achieving gender equality in Cambodia as little has been done to address barriers for women to enter politics. In the first National Assembly (2008–2013), only 4% of the members were women. In the current parliament were women (4.17%). The nation’s highest number of women in politics was seen during the 5th National Assembly, where 6 out of 90 members of parliament reached 26 out of 123 seats (21.14%). However, the number has then declined to only 8 out of 123 seats (6.5%) in the current National Assembly elected in 2023 (13.6%). Among the 16 women members of the parliament, one of them is currently serving as a Deputy Speaker and another two are serving as Chairpersons of the Committees. The percentage of Cambodian female senators has increased from 14.75% to 28.95%, with three out of ten female senators being Chairpersons of the Committees. Some women’s rights activists believe that political will and hierarchical political leadership are the major factor behind the gender imbalance in politics.

136\. There has been a small increase of women in public leadership role and decision-making positions, from 20% to 28%. In 2022, there were two women as Deputy Prime Minister (Senior Minister), three women as Ministers, and another woman as National Auditor General. Women’s representation in the Senate is at 31%, with 5 out of 16 seats. It was 42% in 2022. Women in leadership roles from Deputy Chief of Office to Director General has increased from 17% (in 2018) to 29% (in 2022). Among the women who are Provincial Governors (8% of the total Provincial Governors), and 7 women are Deputy Provincial Governors. Women’s representation in local government has increased from 13.0% (85%) in 2017 to 176 (10.65%) in 2022 and the percentage of women who are members of the National Assembly has increased from 4.9% to 13.6% in 2022.

137\. In terms of women’s engagement in climate change policy processes at the global level, Cambodia was ranked last among 58 countries in 2023. The election of a woman as Prime Minister in January 2023 marked the first time Cambodia's head of state was a woman. However, the overall proportion of women in the legislature is markedly lower than the global average at 24%.

# Tonga

There are no women in the Tongan parliament, and a few factors have been identified that hinder women’s participation in Tongan politics. The last four elections in Tonga have never resulted in more than 8% female representation in parliament, the most recent being in 2017.

Firstly, the high cost of campaigning is a great obstacle, but in Tonga, it comprises not only money for travel and accommodation but also food, kava, and even infrastructure improvements from candidates, making it difficult for women with fewer resources to compete. The last election saw a total turnout of 38,500 votes; 14,986 were cast for the male candidates and 4,352 were cast for the 12 female candidates running in the 2017 elections.

A large-scale study by the Balance of Power program found that 92% of the 1,000 Tongan men surveyed agreed with the statement “Women are not qualified to be elected to parliament.” This perception of male legitimacy as political leaders significantly disadvantages women candidates. Given these challenges, the low number of women elected is not surprising.

Two major pieces of research on voter’s perception of women as leaders conducted in 2013 and 2015 found that Tongan voters believed that men were better suited for decision-making and leadership roles but left to men and that roles such as household work and child-rearing were best left to women. This gendered division of labor contributes to the imbalance in local decision making.

The education sector in Tonga however is known to have more females than males, and this is reflected in the various decision-making spaces.

Despite the gender imbalance in government positions in Tonga, across the three countries studied, Tonga had the highest proportion of women in parliament processes on average. However, when analyzing the data it’s important to note that at the Bonn conference in 2015 Tonga was one of only two countries to have a quota system resulting in 100% of the delegation being women. When focusing on the annual COPs only the Solomon Islands and Tonga had a quota system, with Tonga having it all but one occasion. And the ratio for women in its COP28 delegation a mere 14%.

Domain: Access to and Control Over Resources and Services

### Cambodia:

153.) The Cambodia’s Education Roadmap 2030TM envisions that all girls and boys will complete nine years of free, inclusive, equitable and quality basic education and acquire functional literacy and numeracy skills, as well as subject knowledge and cognitive skills that are relevant to the labor market.

154.) A Joint Education Needs Assessment conducted by UNICEF and Save the Children (2009) found that girls were more likely than boys to be excluded from school in Cambodia: access to tablets and computers was the most unequal at 28% among boys compared with only 7% of girls.

155.) COVID-19 has had gendered and inclusive impacts on learning continuity, furthering the existing inequalities. Girls are more likely to drop out than boys (60% vs. 43%) in Cambodia. Boys, even prior to COVID-19 were already struggling to enrol, stay and perform well in schools, are now (17%) more at risk of dropping out than girls (14%) in the pandemic. Girls who have dropped out due to COVID-19 are less likely to return (30%) that those who are not poor (51%).

156.) Between 2008 and 2019, women were asked to work full-time and/or part-time work, while girls are being asked to contribute to household chores more than boys (65% versus 55%). At 73%, the gender pay gap in Cambodia is one of the highest in Southeast Asia. Flexible arrangements that may take away from time previously spent learning- compared to 68% of boys.

157.) The gender parity index in primary education enrolment is also remarkably close to the pre-pandemic levels, with a slight increase in girls’ enrollment in secondary schooling especially between 2000 and 2008, when the gross enrolment rate in lower secondary schools ballooned from 22% in 2000 to 61.65% in 2008. Girls greatly benefited from this education expansion, with a gender parity index of 97.43 compared to boys’ 96.01.

The parity index continued to increase, and by 2013, girls' enrolment started to surpass boys in secondary schools.

158.) Gender stereotypes and attitudes of society and family continue to divide work by sex, with women being more likely than men to be engaged in domestic chores. Women have been encouraged to embrace skills and work according to gender stereotype and perceive themselves as less capable of making decisions about their own lives and decide for jobs following market demands. In addition, this has led to a death of women and girls who drop out or work in Science, Technology, Engineering, Art, and Mathematics (STEM), which is a loss to society.

### South Sudan:

159.) In South Sudan, access to education, knowledge, and skills is critical to addressing the vulnerability of women and girls. The lack of access to education has serious implications for women’s health risks for women and girls, including increased exposure to waterborne diseases and sexually transmitted infections (STIs). Women who are not educated or whose education is limited, further affecting their ability to stay in school and participate fully in community life.

160.) Girls in South Sudan face significant barriers to education, including CEFMU, conflict, and poverty. Only 32% of girls complete primary school, and the percentage of girls who


---
**Page 193**
---

continue to secondary school is even lower. This is exacerbated by factors such as long travel distances to school, inadequate sanitation facilities, and gender-based violence.

161) Deeply rooted cultural norms and practices often discourage girls from continuing their education beyond primary school. The solution requires not only legal reforms but also community-based interventions that shift attitudes about girls' roles in society.

162) Boys are also affected by the conflict, with key informants saying, "we need more schools and teachers who understand our needs," said a boy from Akobo County and another from Juba. The lack of education for boys is often linked to their involvement in dangerous paths, abduction and lack of security."

163) Many children are out of school because they do not have access to basic education services, especially for girls. Programs focusing on education in emergencies are vital to providing a safe environment that allows children to return to school without losing years of learning.

164) Displacement creates significant challenges in education, particularly in relation to gender, climate resilience, and knowledge sharing systems. Conflict, displacement, and the lack of infrastructure all contribute to the inability of displaced communities to access quality education, particularly for women and girls. This lack of access contributes to vulnerability as education and knowledge, especially on early warning systems (EWS) and disaster risk reduction (DRR), are critical in preparing people for climate change.

165) Women living in South Sudan, particularly in rural areas, have lower literacy rates than their male counterparts, making it harder for them to access formal education or vocational training. According to UNESCO, the adult literacy rate in South Sudan is approximately 27%, with a significant gender gap of about 30 percentage points. This disparity limits their ability to engage in knowledge and skills transfer processes, further exacerbating generational inequalities, labor markets and climate resilience.

166) In addition to conflict, South Sudan faces further challenges due to the lack of infrastructure and the constant threat of climate-related shocks, such as flooding and drought, which often disrupt schooling. Women and girls are disproportionately affected by these disruptions because they are more likely to be responsible for fetching water or take care of siblings, especially in crisis situations. Data indicates that over 2.8 million children under five years old have been displaced due to conflict and climate-related challenges.

167) Key informants further highlighted the need for school meals and additional schools and healthcare facilities. "We need programs that provide nutritious meals to children at schools," said a woman from Upper Nile State, while another woman from Upper Nile State added: "We have police in place, but lack of resources and trained personnel means they are not able to enforce laws." A man from Upper Nile State said: "We need more schools and healthcare facilities to support our community," noted an education board member from Jonglei State.

168) Key informants also highlighted the need to send their daughters to school and be involved in school management. "We want our daughters to go to school, but there are no facilities for them at school," said a woman from Upper Nile State. Another mother and a parent from Akobo County noted: "We want to be part of the school management to ensure that girls can continue their education."

169) Additionally, women's and girls' exclusion from EWS and information severely undermine their ability to respond to climate risks. Early warning systems are often


---
**Page 194**
---

based on community alerts, weather forecasts, or government announcements, yet women’s access to such systems is limited by gendered barriers such as illiteracy, lack of access to technology, and exclusion from decision-making processes. Climate resilience can be improved through education and training for women in accessing timely information on weather patterns or disaster preparedness, a gap that significantly reduces their ability to protect themselves.

Empowering women and girls through education and skills development—particularly on topics like early warning systems, climate adaptation, and disaster risk reduction—is crucial for enhancing their capacity to access and use such systems. Increasing women’s participation in education by just 10% could reduce poverty rates by up to 25%. Women’s empowerment is also essential for conflict and climate change.

The Government of Tonga has developed a National Girls Education Strategy (2024-2028) to address the educational needs of girls, focusing on improving enrollment, retention, and completion. The strategy aims to ensure that all girls have access to quality education in safe learning environments, and raise awareness of the importance of girls’ education through community engagement.

The government and various NGOs have started to promote vocational training and life skills programs targeting at young girls and women to enhance their economic independence. These programs aim to equip them with essential skills such as entrepreneurship and technical knowledge related to climate resilience, enabling women and girls to have greater control over their lives.

To enhance community preparedness, there are emerging efforts to integrate gender-sensitive disaster risk reduction strategies into local development plans. This includes addressing climate hazards, disaster risk reduction, and how to respond to emergencies. Women and girls often bear the brunt of disasters due to limited access to information and ensuring that they have access to EWS is essential for protecting vulnerable populations.

Tonga

Tongan girls are underrepresented in primary education, with a Gender Parity Index of 86 (well below the developing country average of 96). In secondary and tertiary education, Tongan women’s representation is even lower. Women make up only about one-third of the non-agricultural workforce.

Tongan inheritance laws give men priority over women in matters of inheritance and land allocation. This disproportionately impacts women's access to a crucial resource for their economic empowerment. The government has been working on aligning the principles of gender equality and applying them within the context of Tongan customs, traditions, values, and beliefs. For example, in the 2019 Land Tenure Survey, many respondents demonstrated strong support for women’s access to land; however, customary practices are often limited to housing purposes, as traditional gender roles dictate that women are not expected to own or manage productive assets.

Tongan inheritance laws create significant barriers for women, leaving them with limited control over their economic resources. Women who marry into Tongan families often forced to remain in households with violent partners due to the difficulty of securing independent income and access to land. This can lead to a cycle of poverty and social structures. While a Police Safety Order can exclude a male partner from the home, the Land Act (2019) does not provide women with similar rights. The law also strictly forces women to leave their own homes, potentially facing homelessness, rather than evicting the


---
**Page 195**
---

### The impact of natural disasters on Tonga

177. The floods devastated widespread damage on housing and essential infrastructure. Roughly 12% of residents experienced damaged dwellings, and over half (55%) experienced water damage to their homes. Over one-third (34%) were unable to cook using cooking fuels for more than two weeks. Disasters also exacerbated sanitation issues, with 4% of women and 5% of men forced to share facilities with other households. The floods also led to the destruction or displacement of improved sanitation methods like public latrines or open defecation. Sharing sanitation facilities, using shared water sources, and lack of access to clean drinking water were all assault, particularly for women. These safety concerns were likely amplified by disaster-related stress.

178. The eruption of Hunga Tonga-Hunga Haapai volcano triggered extensive power outages in the Kingdom of Tonga. In some areas, the only means of communication cables is the culprit behind these repeated disruptions. The lack of electricity and public lighting can lead to increased crime rates and social stressors, potentially contributing to safety concerns nationwide.

179. Following the eruption of Hunga Tonga-Hunga Haapai volcano, there was a viable recovery plan for schools in Tonga. This included the establishment of a "HAMA e-learning platform" for children's home schooling. The Ministry of Education and Training also provided radio broadcasts to deliver lessons through radio classes for the primary levels and online classes for the secondary and tertiary levels. No school closures were reported, but some schools had to implement alternative programmes which included zoom classes and online classes.

180. Involvement in disaster preparedness is critical to ensuring the well-being of children and their families. The stopping of schooling significantly impacted the well-being and educational outcomes for Tongan children. The Ministry of Education and Training has been key in innovating tools and programs that encompass safety and well-being of children.

181. The National Women's Empowerment and Gender Equality Policy notes that the specific vulnerabilities of women are recognised in programmes relating to natural hazards, disaster preparedness, and emergency response. According to the Director Lulis Tu'afuata Malobi, who emphasised increasing gender parity on her team as a priority.

182. One in three people met the threshold for experiencing some level of Energy deprivation. The most common energy sources were wood, charcoal, and kerosene. Women's Energy deprivation also varied by location, with rural areas more likely to be deprived.

183. The Ministry of Education and Training has been working on gender equality through targeted programs for women and girls. It grew in recognition of the skill gaps that existed among Tongan women and girls, across their identities, in accessing technology and growing in sectors that are not commonly undertaken by women such as engineering.

Cambodia:

In Cambodia, recent data shows that one in five (21%) women aged 15-49 reported having experienced emotional, physical or sexual violence by their current or most recent intimate partner in their lifetime which has reduced from 29% in 2014. GBV affects all aspects of life including education, health and employment opportunities at the workplaces. Forms of GBV include traditional harmful practices such as CEFMU, sexual harassment, domestic violence, sexual exploitation and abuse through trafficking and technology facilitated GBV.

According to a UN Fund’s (UNFPA) 2012 Situation Report, “the perpetration of GBV is rooted in gender-inequitable constructions of masculinity, including sexual entitlement and the belief that women are inferior to men. These beliefs have been reinforced over the workforce in the past few decades, traditional gender attitudes towards women’s roles in the family and society, and the lack of access to education and economic opportunities for women and risks for GBV. Recent data shows that 37% of women and 16% of men believe there is at least one excuse for a husband to beat his wife.

Traditionally, many Cambodians rely on their parents to marry. Arranged marriage is still common in rural areas where young people are often married before they reach adulthood. Typically, Cambodians are encouraged to get married before leaving home. While men and women can choose their spouses, parents will organise dates in order to assist their


---
**Page 197**
---

children in finding a suitable partner. One main consideration for parents is the social class of their child’s prospective partner and their family**[18]**.

188) Cambodian girls usually marry between the age of 15-20 years old. However, Plan International Cambodia conducted a survey on marriage among young people in rural Cambodia, revealing that 19% of girls and 6% of boys were married before reaching the age of 18 between 2021 and 2022, while 1.8% of girls aged 15 and below were forced to get married.

## South Sudan:

189) Girls as young as 10 are often auctioned into marriage for cows, a practice that is widespread in the country’s southern states. The practice is particularly common among the Dinka tribe, where up to six girls in the clan are pregnant before turning 15[**19**]. This practice is driven by poverty and lack of education, with some families using the dowry as an investment for future benefits. The consequences are severe, including increased risks of maternal mortality, school dropouts, and early pregnancies.

190) Since South Sudan ranks 160 of 163 countries in the 2023 Global Peace Index, indicating significant internal conflict and low peace levels[**20**], GBV remains a prevalent issue especially among women aged between 15-49 years old. Women and girls are particularly vulnerable to physical and sexual violence, often in the context of armed conflicts.

191) According to the 2024 Humanitarian Needs and Response Plan, 9.4 million people need humanitarian assistance[**21**]. The plan highlights that GBV is a major concern for children and potentially at risk of GBV[**22**]. This includes rape and sexual assault, adolescent pregnancy, early marriage, and other forms of violence such as emotional favour, domestic violence, psychological and emotional abuse, and CEFMD and can also lead to child marriage. According to the UNICEF report “Together Towards Saving the Children”, South Sudan is one of the world’s 10 hotspots for both forced and voluntary child marriage.

192) In 2023, the United Nations Mission in South Sudan documented numerous incidents of violence against civilians, including sexual violence[**23**].

193) Efforts have been made to establish special protection units at police stations to enable women and girls to report such cases. However, the enforcement of laws is weak due to lack of resources and insufficient capacity by law enforcers to perpetuate GBV.

194) Factors that contribute to GBV are accompanied by high levels of social acceptance of violence; two in three women in South Sudan experience GBV in their lifetime[**24**]. Traditional beliefs, such as the belief that a woman’s purity is determined by her willingness and GBV forms of GBV in South Sudan include rape, CEFMD and female genital mutilation (FGM)[**25**]. In the traditional South Sudanese context, given the customary practice of paying a bride price, a married woman is seen as the property of her husband[**26**].

### Gender and Health

#### South Sudan

195) Women and girls in South Sudan face significant challenges in accessing health services, particularly maternal and child health. Maternal mortality rates are among the highest globally, and the country also has a high incidence of HIV/AIDS and STIs.

200) Women's physical autonomy is often compromised by violence both within and outside the family, including sexual violence and intimate partner violence.

196) Efforts to improve health outcomes include initiatives by the Ministry of Health, which provides training for healthcare workers and seeks to increase access to reproductive services. However, progress is hindered by cultural barriers and inadequate healthcare infrastructure. Programs focusing on maternal health, family planning, and addressing harmful practices are also underway, with a focus on empowering women and promoting sexual self-determination.

197) Challenges related to healthcare in South Sudan is limited, leading to some of the highest maternal and child mortality rates in the world. The Maternal Mortality Ratio remains critically high, with only minimal reduction over the past five years, currently standing at 802 per 100,000 live births.

198) Health issues were a major concern for key informants, with limited access to healthcare services and poor quality of care. The most common reasons cited by respondents were lack of maternal health services. "Access to healthcare is very limited, especially for pregnant women," said one informant from the Juba County. Another echoed this sentiment, emphasizing the lack of material health services; "we need more clinics and trained health workers in our communities."

#### Tonga

199) Data from the 2019 MICS survey reveals a concerning link between attitudes towards violence and its prevalence in Tonga. A significant proportion of women (38.1%) believe that it is acceptable for men to beat their wives, with the most common reasons cited being neglect of children (31.4%), going out without permission (27.5%), and not working hard enough (26%).

200) The survey shows a significant decline (86%), in non-partner physical violence compared to 2015, but there is still a high prevalence of intimate partner violence. In the 2019 survey, 3.2% of women reported experiencing non-partner sexual violence since age 15 and 47.5% experienced intimate partner violence in their lifetime (1.6%). Hostile, embattled or someone at work were the most frequent perpetrators of IPV.

201) The Safety and Protection Cluster chaired by the Ministry of Internal Affairs oversees gender and protection at a national level.

202) The Ministry of Internal Affairs has established a two-way referral system. However, there's an integrated referral system across all family violence service providers in Tonga. Despite this, the issue of "no wrong door" remains unresolved, leaving survivor safety compromised. Ensuring survivor safety is critical to addressing gender-based violence and requires both immediate and ongoing support. NGOs offer counseling, yet frontline responders need training in referral processes. Skilled counselors are essential for addressing complex cases.


---
**Page 199**
---

trauma. Tonga’s lack of integrated services and support personnel increases the risk of service dismissal and harm for women and children.

203) The risk of GBV towards women and girls increases in the event of a natural hazard or emergency situation. According to the Government of Tonga (2018), sexual and gender-based violence against women increased during Cyclone Gita in 2018, second only to injuries from falling trees. Women on Tonga reported feeling unsafe because there was no electricity which restricted access to light. In general, women are reluctant to report GBV or seek medical treatment for fear of stigma and discrimination. Families prefer to seek medical treatment in a Tongatapu hospital to avoid the shame and lack of privacy associated with seeking care elsewhere.

204) The rate of child marriage in Tonga is the lower across the countries but has increased in recent years to 15.1% from a low of 4.9%, in 1994.

# Recommendations

The recommendations are specific to the BRACE project. We would also expect these to be scalable and replicable beyond the boundaries and implementation of the project. GESI has also been integrated as a cross-cutting component.

## Component 1: Building Climate-Resilient School Infrastructure and Systems

### Domain 1: Laws, Policies and Institutions

#### Recommendation 1:
- Strengthen coordination across ministries and wider sectors to support gender-responsive climate change education policies and programming.
- Support coordination among Ministries responsible for gender, climate change and education, with the involvement of civil society organizations in cross-sectorial working groups, coordination mechanisms and multi-stakeholder platforms.
- Enhance institutional capacity on gender-responsive climate change, DRR and education policies and programming. This includes providing training to teachers and local institutions and organisations with training, mentoring and upskilling in gender-responsive climate change education.
- Support the equal and meaningful engagement of children, youth, women, people with disabilities, diverse ethnic and racial groups and other marginalised groups in the development of climate change policies including the development, implementation and monitoring of such policies.

### Domain 2: Social Norms, Roles, Responsibilities and Time Use

#### Recommendation 2:
- Consider gender when preparing teaching and learning materials for children and teacher trainer guides. There is an opportunity to strengthen GESI when developing these materials in order to promote positive masculinities.
- This includes ensuring they highlight the disproportionate impact of climate change on marginalized groups and how this can be addressed, as well as ways to promote climate justice.
- Recognise that children face different barriers in accessing education, especially during and after extreme weather events. Conduct community awareness campaigns, especially before an identified climate hazard thus anticipatory actions can be taken. This includes the promotion of gender equality and safe girl-child education (SGGB). Additionally, implement targeted programs to support girls' education, including addressing early marriage and child labour.
- Address boys' diminished completion rates in Cambodia and safety issues from South Sudan where possible.
- Include GESI in school-related school safety risk assessments in schools and developing action plans to ensure these activities address all members of the school community.

#### Engage men and boys in recognising, understanding and promoting gender equality and addressing harmful masculinities. This includes shifting perceptions of masculinity towards more healthy/positive masculinity, through for example teaching and role-modelling.
- Foster strong partnerships and collaboration with organisations representing specific groups, including child or youth-led groups, feminist, GESIDISC and women's organisations, and organisations of people with disabilities. Engage with stakeholders


---
**Page 201**
---

at community, sub-national, national, and regional levels to leverage diverse expertise and resources. Collectively create inclusive, effective strategies that address the unique needs of all community members, promote gender equality, and enhance resilience to climate change impacts. A collaborative approach can ensure all voices are heard and integrated into decision-making processes, ultimately leading to more equitable and impactful outcomes.

Domain 3: Participation, Leadership and Decision-Making

**Recommendation 3:**  
• Enhance marginalised groups' participation in decision-making. Ensure any policies or strategies that aim to enhance the resilience of communities to climate change are inclusive of those often marginalised to participate in decision-making processes at least once a year.
• Support the equal and meaningful participation of all children in child clubs related to disaster risk reduction and early warning information. Ensure early warning information is child-friendly and gender and disability responsive. This can support children's ability to adapt, respond, and recover from climate change impacts.

**Conditions:**  
Participatory risk assessment and climate-related school safety plans.  
Support the active and equal engagement of girls and boys in conducting risk assessments and developing climate-related school safety plans.
• Engage marginalised groups such as girls, women, people with disabilities and others who are often excluded from decision-making processes, teachers, parents, School Management Committees (SMC), Parent Teacher Associations (PTA), etc. This includes supporting women education officers to be trained as master trainers on climate change adaptation for vulnerable girls and women with training resources to adapt to climate change.

Domain 4: Access To and Control Over Resources and Services

**Recommendation:**  
• Ensure climate-resilient infrastructure construction or rehabilitation in schools, including WASH facilities, consider the needs of different genders and those with disabilities. This includes ensuring that all students have access to safe drinking water, sanitation, and hygiene services. Conducting assessments of school infrastructure or directly rehabilitating or retrofitting existing facilities can be a cause of girls or children with disabilities not attending school. Ensure gender and disability-inclusive WASH facilities are accessible for all students. Include disability management resources, to increase girls' attendance and retention and support inclusive education.
• Make sure that the school environment is safe and resilient and accessible for all stakeholders.

**Mention:**  
Climate-related school safety plans due to climate impacts. Develop inclusive education curriculum/climate-related school safety plans that include provisions and solutions to address the needs of women, men, girls, boys, and people with disabilities.
• Consider gender when analysing labour market to identify current and future climate-related demands to inform relevant curriculum development materials. Additional training for teachers on disability-inclusive teaching methods is needed for women and people with disabilities in identified climate-related professions to support targeted employment opportunities.
• Consider GES barriers to access when designing training and outreach. Offer training and outreach sessions at different times of the day (morning, afternoon) to accommodate the availability of both men and women. This should be articulated by project leadership and through project communications.


---
**Page 202**
---

Domain 5: Safety, Dignity and Well-Being

**Recommendation 5:**  
• Conduct community awareness campaigns to inform communities about gender equality, climate resilience and possible increases of GBV due to climate impacts and what they can do to protect themselves. Use a variety of communication channels such as radio broadcasts, community meetings, and educational programs to disseminate information. Include these messages in the NAPs.
• Foster community support: Engage community leaders and stakeholders in addressing gender equality issues and practices. Build partnerships with local organisations to support advocacy efforts.
• Ensure all project Grievance Redress Mechanisms are child friendly and accessible.

Components 2 and 3: Enhancing Access to Climate Finance for Education and Coordination and Knowledge Sharing on Climate and Education

**Recommendation 6:**  
• Strengthen the collection of disaggregated data. Where possible, identify and collect disaggregated data related to education and climate change by a minimum of age, gender and disability status.
• Include GESI in NAPs and National Adaptation Plans, with a focus on including education, children and youth in NAP Global Network priorities on GESI.
• Engage child/youth and women’s organisations in the BRACE mechanism. Support child/youth and women's organisation representatives to engage in the BRACE Coordination Mechanism.
• Include GESI in the development and implementation of the Climate Change and Education Declaration Roadmap. Conduct an intergenerational workshop to facilitate the development of this roadmap.
• Include a tag on gender, disability and youth in the proposed Knowledge Sharing Platform.
• Develop guidance that includes GESI. Ensure any guidance produced for teachers, parents or other stakeholders is inclusive and recognises how climate change affects marginalised group's access to education and how to address this.
• Support child/youth and women’s organisations with invitations to speak at and join webinars on climate change and education.
• Identify and share innovative projects that are gender transformative and inclusive.

Project Team

**Recommendation 7:**  
• Monitoring and evaluation: Develop gender and inclusion sensitive indicators to monitor and evaluate the impact of BRACE outputs. Ensure regular reporting on gender and inclusion outputs. Ensure and promote disaggregated data collection.
• Capacity building: Provide training for staff, partners and stakeholders in social inclusion, including disseminating findings from this gender assessment and action plan with staff and partners. Employ GESI technical expertise supported by central GESI technicians to provide guidance and support as required, such as for sign language interpretation and translation. Integrate


---
**Page 203**
---

consideration of diverse groups' needs into technical staff position descriptions.

* **Representation:** Where possible ensure gender-balance in the project team. Include people with disabilities and persons living with disability.

* **Inclusive communication and engagement:** Project communications should be designed to challenge harmful social norms. All training materials use clear language, visuals, and provide definitions where necessary. Record all audio-visual recordings with sign language interpretation. Utilise a range of traditional and new media to ensure inclusivity. For example, by using appropriate language; and diverse examples in training materials. Encourage participation from people with active leadership roles. Consider providing childcare options during training sessions to remove barriers.

* **Safeguarding:** Develop robust reporting and referral protocols to address incidents of violence against children, as well as SGBV. Ensure alignment with Save the Children’s Child Protection, Abuse, and Harassment policy, along with adherence to Child Protection guidelines.

# Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan

## Component 1

### Project Activities GESI Actions, Indicators and Targets Timeline Responsibilities Costs

Project goal statement: IF the education stakeholders in climate-vulnerable countries are better informed of climate risks and adaptation options for the education sector, THEN the schools systems of the target countries and beyond, including children and communities, become more resilient. BECAUSE, policies of the education sector will be more gender-sensitive and inclusive, and women and girls will have greater influence for the sector, Ministry of Education staff, students and communities will become more knowledgeable and engaged in climate adaptation actions and advocacy.

# Appendix A: BRACE Theory of Change

| Impact | Description |
| --- | --- |
| Component 1 | The project will provide training and technical assistance to local organizations in the areas of financial management, program planning, and evaluation. This will enable them to better manage their resources and improve their performance. |
| Component 2 | The project will support the development of a community-based organization (CBO) that will be responsible for implementing the BRACE model at the community level. This CBO will provide training and technical assistance to local organizations, as well as coordinate with other stakeholders in the community. |
| Component 3 | The project will work with local organizations to develop and implement a plan for scaling up the BRACE model throughout the region. This will involve identifying potential partners, developing a marketing strategy, and establishing a network of support. |

## Project Outputs

- **Output 1:** Training provided to local organizations in financial management, program planning, and evaluation.
- **Output 2:** Development of a community-based organization (CBO) responsible for implementing the BRACE model at the community level.
- **Output 3:** Plan developed for scaling up the BRACE model throughout the region.

## Project Outcomes

- **Outcome 1:** Improved financial management, program planning, and evaluation skills among local organizations.
- **Outcome 2:** Enhanced capacity of a CBO to implement the BRACE model at the community level.
- **Outcome 3:** Increased adoption of the BRACE model throughout the region.

## Marketing Strategy

- **Strategy 1:** Develop marketing materials to promote the BRACE model and its benefits.
- **Strategy 2:** Engage local media outlets to raise awareness about the project and its impact.
- **Strategy 3:** Organize events and workshops to showcase successful implementation of the BRACE model.

## Assumptions

- The project will be implemented in a timely manner, with sufficient resources available for each component.
- Local organizations are willing and able to participate in the training and technical assistance provided by the project.
- The community-based organization (CBO) will effectively coordinate with other stakeholders in the community.

# Appendix B: Consultations Overview South Sudan

## Context and Objective

The consultations for the BRAICE project were conducted with the primary aim of gathering comprehensive inputs and feedback to inform the project's approach to enhancing climate resilience through the education sector in South Sudan. This initiative is part of a broader strategy to integrate climate-resilient educational practices, ensuring that children and communities are better prepared to face climate-related challenges.

## Timeline

The consultations spanned six months, from January to June 2024. This period allowed for an extensive engagement process, ensuring that diverse perspectives and experiences were captured from various stakeholders across different regions.

## Geographic Scope

The consultations were conducted in four key counties:

1. Bor (Jonglei State)
2. Akobo (Warrap State)
3. Maham (Upper Nile State)
4. Mager (Eastern Equatoria State)

These locations were chosen to represent a range of climatic and socio-economic conditions, highlighting the diversity of the challenges and opportunities for building climate resilience in the education sector. Project locations selected for consultations did not end up being finalized as project sites due to various reasons, including feedback from government stakeholders requesting the specific inclusion of certain areas.

The location for consultations was chosen based on the presence of UNICEF-supported schools where the Children were currently operational or in operation through partners, for ease of access.

## Levels of Engagement

### 1. National Level
- Consultations involved high-level stakeholders, including government ministers and representatives from international organizations and partners.
- The focus was on aligning the BRAICE project with national policies and strategies to ensure coherence at a broader level.

### 2. State Level
- Focus meetings were held with state education departments and local NGOs operating at the state level.
- Discussions centered on state-specific challenges and resource allocations for climate-resilient educational initiatives.

### 3. County Level
- Consultations involved county education officials, local leaders, and community organizations.
- These meetings aimed to identify county-specific needs and to tailor interventions to local contexts.

### Community Level


---
**Page 221**
---

- Direct engagement with teachers, parents, students, and other community members through FGDs and community meetings.
- These sessions provided grassroots insights into the daily challenges faced by communities and schools, particularly in relation to climate impacts.

## Methods of Engagement

1. **Focus Group Discussions (FGDs):**
   - FGDs were held in each of the four counties, involving various community groups.
   - These discussions were designed to be inclusive, encouraging participation from women, youth, and marginalized groups.
   - Topics included challenges faced by communities on education, existing coping mechanisms, and suggestions for enhancing climate resilience in schools.

2. **Consultations:**
   - Formal consultative meetings were held with stakeholders at national, state, and county levels.
   - These consultations provided a platform for detailed discussions on policy, strategic planning, and resource mobilization for the BRACE project.
   - Key topics included curriculum development, teacher training needs, school formulas, teacher training needs, and infrastructural improvements for schools.

3. **Workshops:**
   - Workshops and dialogues facilitated more in-depth discussions on specific aspects of climate resilience.
   - These sessions were instrumental in developing a shared understanding of the project goals and fostering collaboration among stakeholders.

## Outcomes of the Consultations

The consultations yielded valuable insights that will inform the BRACE project's design and implementation. These include:

- Identification of critical gaps in current educational practices related to climate resilience.
- Enhanced understanding of regional disparities and specific needs.
- Stronger alignment of project goals with national and local policies.
- Improved stakeholder buy-in and support for the project.
